## II Burglars / 第二章 盗贼

===Original===
Dickie fell asleep between clean, coarse sheets in a hard, narrow bed, for which fourpence had been paid.

“Put yer clobber under yer bolster, likewise yer boots,” was the last instruction of his new friend and “father.”

There had been a bath﻿—or something equally cleansing﻿—in a pail near a fire where ragged but agreeable people were cooking herrings, sausages, and other delicacies on little gridirons or pans that they unrolled from the strange bundles that were their luggage. One man who had no gridiron cooked a piece of steak on the kitchen tongs. Dickie thought him very clever. A very fat woman asked Dickie to toast a herring for her on a bit of wood; and when he had done it she gave him two green apples.

He laid in bed and heard jolly voices talking and singing in the kitchen below. And he thought how pleasant it was to be a tramp, and what jolly fellows the tramps were; for it seemed that all these nice people were “on the road,” and this place where the kitchen was, and the good company and the clean bed for fourpence, was a Tramps’ Hotel﻿—one of many that are scattered over the country and called “Common Lodging-Houses.”

===Chinese===
迪基（Dickie）在一床干净却粗糙的被单之间睡着了，睡的是一张又硬又窄的床，为这张床付了四便士。

「把你的家什塞到长枕头底下，鞋也搁在那儿，」这是他那位新朋友兼新「爸爸」的最后一条嘱咐。

不远处的火堆旁，一只木桶里盛着洗澡水——或者说，盛着某种同样能把人洗净的东西。火堆边围着一群衣衫褴褛却和和气气的人，正在小火叉和平底锅上煎鲱鱼、煎香肠，做种种美味吃食，那些火叉和锅子都是从这个当行李使的奇形怪状的包袱里抖开来的。有个没带火叉的人，就干脆用厨房火钳夹着一块牛排烤。迪基觉得这人本事大极了。一位胖得出奇的大娘请迪基替她在一片木头上烤条鲱鱼；烤好了，她赏了他两只青苹果。

他躺在床上，听楼下厨房里快活的人声又说又唱。他心想，做个流浪汉多惬意呀，流浪汉们又是多好的伙伴；因为看样子，这些和气人儿全是「跑路」的，而这个有厨房、有好伴儿、有四便士干净床铺的地方，就是一座「流浪者客栈」——这样的客栈全国四散都是，官名叫「公共寄宿所」。

===Original===
The singing and laughing went on long after he had fallen asleep, and if, later in the evening, there were loud-voiced arguments, or quarrels even, Dickie did not hear them.

Next morning, quite early, they took the road. From some mysterious source Mr. Beale had obtained an old double perambulator, which must have been made, Dickie thought, for very fat twins, it was so broad and roomy. Artfully piled on the front part was all the furniture needed by travellers who mean to sleep every night at the Inn of the Silver Moon. (That is the inn where they have the beds with the green curtains.)

“What’s all that there?” Dickie asked, pointing to the odd knobbly bundles of all sorts and shapes tied on to the perambulator’s front.

“All our truck what we’ll want on the road,” said Beale.

“And that pillowy bundle on the seat.”

“That’s our clothes. I’ve bought you a little jacket to put on o’ nights if it’s cold or wet. An’ when you want a lift﻿—why, here’s your carriage, and you can sit up ’ere and ride like the Lord Mayor, and I’ll be yer horse; the bundles’ll set on yer knee like a fat babby. Tell yer what, mate﻿—looks to me as if I’d took a fancy to you.”

===Chinese===
他睡熟之后，歌声和笑声还响了很久；至于更晚些时候厨房里有没有高嗓门的争辩，甚至吵嘴，迪基一概没有听见。

第二天清早，他们就上了路。天晓得比尔先生（Mr. Beale）从什么神秘门路弄来一辆旧的双人婴儿车，车身又宽又敞，迪基心想，这车准是替一对胖得出奇的双胞胎造的。车前部灵巧地码着一大堆家什，凡是打算夜夜都睡在「银月客栈」里的旅人，所需的行头全在这儿了。（银月客栈，就是那家床铺挂着绿帐子的客栈。）

「那儿捆着的都是什么呀？」迪基指着绑在婴儿车前头那些奇形怪状、疙疙瘩瘩的包袱问。

「咱们路上要用的全部家什，」比尔说。

「还有座位上那个像枕头似的包袱呢？」

「那是咱们的衣裳。我给你买了件小夹克，夜里天冷天潮就穿上。你要是想搭车嘛——喏，这就是你的马车，你就在这儿坐直喽，像市长大人那样兜风，我给你当马；那些包袱搁在你膝盖上，就像个胖娃娃。跟你说句掏心窝子的话吧，伙计——我瞧着像是看上你了。」

===Original===
“I ’ave to you, I know that,” said Dickie, settling his crutch firmly and putting his hand into Mr. Beale’s. Mr. Beale looked down at the touch.

“Swelp me!” he said helplessly. Then, “Does it hurt you﻿—walking?”

“Not like it did ’fore I went to the orspittle. They said I’d be able to walk to rights if I wore that there beastly boot. But that ’urts worsen anythink.”

“Well,” said Mr. Beale, “you sing out when you get tired and I’ll give yer a ride.”

===Chinese===
「我对你也是，这我知道，」迪基说着，把拐杖稳稳拄好，一只手伸进比尔先生的手里。比尔先生低头看了看这一握。

「老天爷！」他无可奈何地说。随后问：「走路疼不疼？」

「不像进医院以前疼得那么厉害了。他们说，只要戴上那只要命的靴子，我就能好好走路。可那玩意儿比什么都硌人。」

「行了，」比尔先生说，「累了你就吭声，我让你坐车。」

===Original===
“Oh, look,” said Dickie﻿—“the flowers!”

“They’re only weeds,” said Beale. They were, in fact, convolvuluses, little pink ones with their tendrils and leaves laid flat to the dry earth by the wayside, and in a water-meadow below the road level big white ones twining among thick-growing osiers and willows.

Dickie filled his hands with the pink ones, and Mr. Beale let him.

“They’ll die directly,” he said.

“But I shall have them while they’re alive,” said Dickie, as he had said to the pawnbroker about the moonflowers.

===Chinese===
「哦，快看，」迪基说，「花儿！」

「不过是些野草罢了，」比尔说。其实那是旋花：路边是一丛丛粉红的小旋花，卷须和叶子平平地贴在干土上；路畔低于路面的水草甸里，大朵大朵的白旋花在密密的柳条和柳树间缠绕。

迪基摘了满满一把粉红的，比尔先生由着他。

「它们马上就会蔫的，」他说。

「可它们活着的时候，归我呀，」迪基说——当初对当铺老板说起月光花，他也是这么讲的。

===Original===
It was a wonderful day. All the country sights and sounds, that you hardly notice because you have known them every year as long as you can remember, were wonderful magic to the little boy from Deptford. The green hedge, the cows looking over them; the tinkle of sheep-bells; the “baa” of the sheep; the black pigs in a sty close to the road, their breathless rooting and grunting and the shiny, blackleaded cylinders that were their bodies; the stubbly fields where barley stood in sheaves﻿—real barley, like the people next door but three gave to their hens; the woodland shadows and the lights of sudden water; shoulders of brown upland pressed against the open sky; the shrill thrill of the skylark’s song, “like canary birds got loose”; the splendor of distance﻿—you never see distance in Deptford; the magpie that perched on a stump and cocked a bright eye at the travellers; the thing that rustled a long length through dead leaves in a beech coppice, and was, it appeared, a real live snake﻿—all these made the journey a royal progress to Dickie of Deptford. He forgot that he was lame, forgot that he had run away﻿—a fact that had cost him a twinge or two of fear or conscience earlier in the morning. He was happy as a prince is happy, new-come to his inheritance, and it was Mr. Beale, after all, who was the first to remember that there was a carriage in which a tired little boy might ride.

“In you gets,” he said suddenly; “you’ll be fair knocked. You can look about you just as well a-sittin’ down,” he added, laying the crutch across the front of the perambulator. “Never see such a nipper for noticing, neither. Hi! there goes a rabbit. See ’im? Crost the road there? See him?”

===Chinese===
这真是奇妙的一天。乡间的种种景物与声响，你若年年得见、习以为常，是几乎不会留意的；可对这么一个德普特福德（Deptford）来的小男孩，它们全成了奇妙的魔法：绿油油的树篱，从篱笆上探头张望的奶牛；羊铃的叮当；羊儿「咩咩」的叫唤；路旁猪圈里的黑猪，呼哧呼哧地拱地哼哼，滚圆油亮的身子乌黑发亮，活像上了黑铅的圆筒；麦茬地里一捆捆立着的大麦——真真是大麦，就跟隔壁第三家喂母鸡的那种一样；树林的浓荫，忽地一亮的水光；棕褐色高岗的肩头抵着敞亮的天空；云雀歌声里那种尖细的颤音，「像逃出笼子的金丝雀」；还有远方的壮丽——在德普特福德，你从来见不到「远方」；一只喜鹊落在树桩上，冲着旅人灵巧地转着亮眼睛；还有山毛榉矮林里窸窸窣窣拖着长长一路枯叶窜过去的东西，那竟是一条活生生的真蛇——这一切，让德普特福德的迪基觉得这一路走得像帝王出巡。他忘了自己是瘸腿，忘了自己是逃出来的——这件事，今天一早还曾叫他心里发过几阵害怕，或者说，扎过几针愧疚。他快乐得像一位新继承产业的王子；而最后头一个想起「车上还坐得下一个累坏了的小家伙」的，到底是比尔先生。

「上来吧，」他忽然说，「你非累趴下不可。坐着也一样能东瞧西看嘛。」他说着，把拐杖横搁在婴儿车前部。「这么会留神的小家伙，我还真没见过第二个。嘿！那儿蹿过去一只兔子。瞧见没？打路那边窜过去的？瞧见了没？」

===Original===
Dickie saw, and the crown was set on his happiness. A rabbit. Like the ones that his fancy had put in the mouldering hutch at home.

“It’s got loose,” said Dickie, trying to scramble out of the perambulator; “let’s catch ’im and take ’im along.”

“ ’E ain’t loose﻿—’e’s wild,” Mr. Beale explained; “ ’e ain’t never bin caught. Lives out ’ere with ’is little friendses,” he added after a violent effort of imagination﻿—“in ’oles in the ground. Gets ’is own meals and larks about on ’is own.”

“How beautiful!” said Dickie, wriggling with delight. This life of the rabbit, as described by Mr. Beale, was the child’s first glimpse of freedom. “I’d like to be a rabbit.”

“You much better be my little nipper,” said Beale. “Steady on, mate. ’Ow’m I to wheel the bloomin’ pram if you goes on like as if you was a bag of eels?”

===Chinese===
迪基瞧见了，他的快乐这下算戴上了王冠。一只兔子。跟他在家里叫幻想安进那只朽烂兔笼里的那些一模一样。

「它是跑出来的，」迪基说着，就想从婴儿车里往外爬，「咱们把它逮住，带着一块儿走吧。」

「它不是跑出来的——它是野的，」比尔先生解释道，「它从来没叫人逮过。它跟它的小伙伴们住在这外头，」他狠狠发挥了一通想象力，又添上一句，「住在地洞里。自己找饭吃，自己撒欢儿玩，全凭自己。」

「多美呀！」迪基乐得直扭。照比尔先生这么一形容，兔子过的这种日子，就是这孩子头一回望见的自由。「我想当一只兔子。」

「你呀，还是当我的小家伙好，」比尔说，「稳着点儿，老伙计。你这么跟一袋子鳗鱼似的乱扭，我这辆破车还推不推了？」

===Original===
They camped by a copse for the midday meal, sat on the grass, made a fire of sticks, and cooked herrings in a frying-pan, produced from one of the knobbly bundles.

“It’s better’n Fiff of November,” said Dickie; “and I do like you. I like you nexter my own daddy and Mr. Baxter next door.”

“That’s all right,” said Mr. Beale awkwardly.

===Chinese===
中午他们在一小片树丛旁歇脚吃饭：坐在草地上，拾了树枝生起火，从一件疙瘩包袱里掏出煎锅，煎起鲱鱼来。

「这比十一月五号还好玩，」迪基说，「我是真喜欢你。我喜欢你，就排在我亲爸爸和隔壁的巴克斯特先生（Mr. Baxter）后头。」

「这就好，」比尔先生不自在地说。

===Original===
It was in the afternoon that, halfway up a hill, they saw coming over the crest a lady and a little girl.

“Hout yer gets,” said Mr. Beale quickly; “walk as ’oppy as you can, and if they arsts you you say you ain’t ’ad nothing to eat since las’ night and then it was a bit o’ dry bread.”

“Right you are,” said Dickie, enjoying the game.

“An’ mind you call me father.”

“Yuss,” said Dickie, exaggerating his lameness in the most spirited way. It was acting, you see, and all children love acting.

Mr. Beale went more and more slowly, and as the lady and the little girl drew near he stopped altogether and touched his cap. Dickie, quick to imitate, touched his.

===Chinese===
到了下午，在一座山走到半山的地方，他们望见山脊那头过来一位太太和一个小姑娘。

「快下来，」比尔先生连忙说，「使劲装瘸，能装多瘸装多瘸。她们要是问你，你就说打昨晚起什么都没吃过，昨晚吃的也不过是口干面包。」

「好嘞，」迪基说，玩得正来劲。

「记着，管我叫爸爸。」

「是嘞，」迪基应道，把瘸腿演得格外卖力。要知道，这是演戏，而天底下的孩子没有不爱演戏的。

比尔先生越走越慢，等太太和小姑娘走近了，他索性整个站住，抬手碰了碰帽檐。迪基学得飞快，也碰了碰自己的。

===Original===
“Could you spare a trifle, mum,” said Beale, very gently and humbly, “to ’elp us along the road? My little chap, ’e’s lame like wot you see. It’s a ’ard life for the likes of ’im, mum.”

“He ought to be at home with his mother,” said the lady.

Beale drew his coat sleeve across his eyes.

“ ’E ain’t got no mother,” he said; “she was took bad sudden﻿—a chill it was, and struck to her innards. She died in the infirmary. Three months ago it was, mum. And us not able even to get a bit of black for her.”

Dickie sniffed.

===Chinese===
「太太，能赏个零钱吗，」比尔说得又轻又谦卑，「好帮我们爷儿俩赶赶路？我这小家伙，您瞧见的，腿是瘸的。像他这样的孩子，日子苦哇，太太。」

「这样的孩子本该在家里守着他的妈妈，」太太说。

比尔拉起衣袖，在眼睛上抹了一把。

「他没妈了，」他说；「他妈去得急——是着了凉，寒气直攻到脏腑里，人就完了，死在济贫医院里。到今儿整整三个月啦，太太。我们爷儿俩连给她置件黑孝衣的钱都凑不出来。」

迪基抽了抽鼻子。

===Original===
“Poor little man!” said the lady; “you miss your mother, don’t you?”

“Yuss,” said Dickie sadly; “but farver, ’e’s very good to me. I couldn’t get on if it wasn’t for farver.”

“Oh, well done, little ’un!” said Mr. Beale to himself.

“We lay under a ’aystack last night,” he said aloud, “and where we’ll lie tonight gracious only knows, without some kind soul lends us a ’elping ’and.”

The lady fumbled in her pocket, and the little girl said to Dickie﻿—

===Chinese===
「可怜的小人儿！」太太说；「你想妈妈了，是不是？」

「是，」迪基愁苦地说；「不过爸爸待我可好了。要不是有爸爸，我早撑不住啦。」

「好样的，小家伙！」比尔先生自言自语。

「昨晚我们爷儿俩睡在一个干草堆底下，」他放声说道，「今晚睡哪儿，要没有哪位好心人搭把手，就只有老天爷知道了。」

太太伸手在衣袋里摸索，小姑娘对迪基说——

===Original===
“Where are all your toys?”

“I ain’t got but two,” said Dickie, “and they’re at ’ome; one of them’s silver﻿—real silver﻿—my grandfarver ’ad it when ’e was a little boy.”

“But if you’ve got silver you oughtn’t to be begging,” said the lady, shutting up her purse. Beale frowned.

“It only pawns for a shilling,” said Dickie, “and farver knows what store I sets by it.”

“A shillin’s a lot, I grant you that,” said Beale eagerly; “but I wouldn’t go to take away the nipper’s little bit o’ pleasure, not for no shilling I wouldn’t,” he ended nobly, with a fond look at Dickie.

===Chinese===
「你的玩具都哪儿去了？」

「我就有两样，」迪基说，「都搁在家里呢；其中一样是银的——真银的——我爷爷小时候就有的。」

「可你既然有银子，就不该出来讨饭呀，」太太说着收拢了钱包。比尔皱起眉头。

「那东西当了也只值一先令，」迪基说，「再说爸爸知道我多稀罕它。」

「一先令可不是小数，这话我认，」比尔急忙接话，「可要我去夺这孩子的那点儿乐子，给我多少先令我也不干，多少先令也不干。」他慷慨激昂地收住话头，慈爱地望了迪基一眼。

===Original===
“You’re a kind father,” said the lady.

“Yes, isn’t he, mother?” said the little girl. “May I give the little boy my penny?”

The two travellers were left facing each other, the richer by a penny, and oh﻿—wonderful good fortune﻿—a whole half-crown. They exchanged such glances as might pass between two actors as the curtain goes down on a successful dramatic performance.

“You did that bit fine,” said Beale﻿—“fine, you did. You been there before, ain’t ye?”

===Chinese===
「你真是个好爸爸，」太太说。

「可不是嘛，妈妈？」小姑娘说。「我能把这枚便士送给小哥哥吗？」

路边只剩这两位旅人面面相觑，手里多了一枚便士，还有——哦，天大的福气——整整半克朗。两人交换了一个眼色，就像一出好戏闭幕时，台上两个演员之间的那种眼色。

「你那段儿演得真地道，」比尔说——「真地道，没说的。你从前干过这行，是不是？」

===Original===
“No, I never,” said Dickie; “ ’ere’s the steever.”

“You stick to that,” said Beale, radiant with delight; “you’re a fair masterpiece, you are; you earned it honest if ever a kid done. Pats you on the napper, she does, and out with ’arf a dollar! A bit of all right, I call it!”

They went on up the hill as happy as anyone need wish to be.

They had told lies, you observe, and had by these lies managed to get half a crown and a penny out of the charitable; and far from being ashamed of their acts, they were bubbling over with merriment and delight at their own cleverness. Please do not be too shocked. Remember that neither of them knew any better. To the elder tramp lies and begging were natural means of livelihood. To the little tramp the whole thing was a new and entrancing game of make-believe.

===Chinese===
「没有，头一回，」迪基说；「喏，小银币给你。」

「你就好好收着它，」比尔喜得满脸放光；「你真是个了不得的天才，一点不假；天底下要有个孩子是凭本事堂堂正正挣来的，那就是你。太太拍拍你的脑袋瓜子，一伸手就是半克朗！我说，这买卖顶呱呱！」

两人接着往山上走，快活得不能再快活了。

你们瞧见了，他们扯了谎，还靠扯谎从行善的人手里弄到了半克朗零一便士；非但不觉羞耻，反倒为自己的机灵乐得直冒泡。请诸位先别急着震惊。要知道，他们俩谁也不曾受过更好的教养。对年长的流浪汉来说，撒谎和乞讨本是谋生的老法子；对小小的流浪汉来说，这整个儿不过是一场新鲜的、迷人心的假扮游戏。

===Original===
By evening they had seven-and-sixpence.

“Us’ll ’ave a fourpenny doss outer this,” said Beale. “Swelp me Bob, we’ll be ridin’ in our own moty afore we know where we are at this rate.”

“But you said the bed with the green curtains,” urged Dickie.

“Well, p’rhaps you’re right. Lay up for a rainy day, eh? Which this ain’t, not by no means. There’s a ’aystack a bit out of the town, if I remember right. Come on, mate.”

And Dickie for the first time slept out-of-doors. Have you ever slept out-of-doors? The night is full of interesting little sounds that will not, at first, let you sleep﻿—the rustle of little wild things in the hedges, the barking of dogs in distant farms, the chirp of crickets and the croaking of frogs. And in the morning the birds wake you, and you curl down warm among the hay and look up at the sky that is growing lighter and lighter, and breathe the chill, sweet air, and go to sleep again wondering how you have ever been able to lie of nights in one of those shut-up boxes with holes in them which we call houses.

===Chinese===
到了傍晚，他们已经有了七先令六便士。

「咱们这就攒出一笔四便士的铺位钱啦，」比尔说。「老天爷作证，照这么下去，不知不觉咱们就坐上自己的汽车啦。」

「可你说过要睡那张挂着绿帐子的床，」迪基提醒他。

「嗯，兴许你说得对。钱要攒着防雨天，是不是？今儿这天可一点不像要下雨。我记得镇子外头不远有个干草堆。走吧，伙计。」

迪基有生以来头一回睡在露天里。你在露天里睡过吗？夜里到处是引人入胜的小声音，起先叫你睡不着——树篱里小野兽的窸窣，远处农庄的犬吠，蟋蟀的唧唧，青蛙的呱呱。到了早晨，鸟儿把你唤醒，你暖乎乎地蜷在干草里，望着越亮越透的天空，呼吸着清冽甘甜的空气，然后又睡过去，心里直纳闷：人怎么可能在那种四面关死、只开几个窟窿的匣子里过夜——那种匣子，我们管它叫房子。

===Original===
The new game of begging and inventing stories to interest the people from whom it was worth while to beg went on gaily, day by day and week by week; and Dickie, by constant practice, grew so clever at taking his part in the acting that Mr. Beale was quite dazed with admiration.

“Blessed if I ever see such a nipper,” he said, over and over again.

And when they got nearly to Hythe, and met with the red-whiskered man who got up suddenly out of the hedge and said he’d been hanging off and on expecting them for nigh on a week, Mr. Beale sent Dickie into a field to look for mushrooms﻿—which didn’t grow there﻿—expressly that he might have a private conversation with the red-whiskered man﻿—a conversation which began thus﻿—

===Chinese===
这出新的把戏——一面讨钱，一面编故事去打动那些值得打动的施主——兴高采烈地一天天、一星期接一星期地演下去；迪基勤学苦练，把自己那一份戏演得越发精熟，演得比尔先生佩服得直发懵。

「我活这么大，就没见过这样的小家伙，」这句话他翻来覆去地念叨。

等他们快到海斯（Hythe），碰上了那个红络腮胡的男人（the red-whiskered man）——他从树篱里猛地钻出来，说他前前后后等了他们快一个礼拜了——比尔先生立刻打发迪基到田里去找蘑菇——那地里根本不长蘑菇——好让自己跟红络腮胡的男人密谈几句。这场密谈是这样开的头——

===Original===
“Couldn’t get ’ere afore. Couldn’t get a nipper.”

“ ’E’s ’oppy, ’e is; ’e ain’t no good.”

“No good?” said Beale. “That’s all you know! ’E’s a wunner, and no bloomin’ error. Turns the ladies round ’is finger as easy as kiss yer ’and. Clever as a traindawg ’e is﻿—an’ all outer ’is own ’ead. And to ’ear the way ’e does the patter to me on the road. It’s as good as a gaff any day to ’ear ’im. My word! I ain’t sure as I ’adn’t better stick to the road, and keep away from old ’ands like you, Jim.”

“Doin’ well, eh?” said Jim.

===Chinese===
「先前一直赶不过来。也没弄着个小家伙。」

「他是个瘸子，他；中不了用。」

「中不了用？」比尔说。「你懂个啥！他是个宝贝，错不了。太太夫人们叫他拨弄得团团转，容易得就像打个响指。他机灵得跟马戏团的狗一样——还全是他自己脑袋瓜里想出来的。还有他一路上给我帮腔说的那一套，你听听去。听他说话，哪一天都赛过一场杂耍戏。好家伙！我说不定还真不如一直跑我的路，躲开你这种老油子呢，吉姆（Jim）。」

「混得不错，啊？」吉姆说。

===Original===
“Not so dusty,” said Mr. Beale cautiously; “we mugger along some’ow. An’ ’e’s got so red in the face, and plumped out so, they’ll soon say ’e doesn’t want their dibs.”

“Starve ’im a bit,” said the red-whiskered man cheerfully.

Mr. Beale laughed. Then he spat thoughtfully. Then he said﻿—

“It’s rum﻿—I likes to see the little beggar stokin’ up, for all it spoils the market. If ’e gets a bit fat ’e makes it up in cleverness. You should ’ear ’im!” and so forth and so on, till the red-whiskered man said quite crossly﻿—

“Seems to me you’re a bit dotty about this ’ere extry double nipper. I never knew you took like it afore.”

===Chinese===
「还过得去，」比尔先生留了个心眼，「我们总能想方设法捱。再说他小脸吃得红扑扑的，身子也壮实了，过不多久，人家就该说他不稀罕他们的钱了。」

「那就饿他一饿，」红络腮胡的男人兴冲冲地说。

比尔先生笑了。接着若有所思地啐了一口。然后说道——

「说来也怪——小家伙大口吃饭，我爱看，虽说这败坏了行情。他就算长胖一点儿，也能拿机灵补回来。你该听听他说话！」如此这般，絮叨个没完，直说得红络腮胡的男人生起气来——

「要我说，你对这个百里挑一的小家伙有点儿犯糊涂。往日我可没见你这么上过心。」

===Original===
“Fact is,” said Beale, with an air of great candor, “it’s ’is cleverness does me. It ain’t as I’m silly about ’im﻿—but ’e’s that clever.”

“I ’ope ’e’s clever enough to do wot ’e’s told. Keep ’is mug shut﻿—that’s all.”

“He’s clever enough for hanythink,” said Beale, “and close as wax. ’E’s got a silver toy ’idden away somewhere﻿—it only pops for a bob﻿—and d’you think ’e’ll tell me where it’s stowed? Not ’im, and us such pals as never was, and ’is jaw wagging all day long. But ’e’s never let it out.”

“Oh, stow it!” said the other impatiently; “I don’t want to ’ear no more about ’im. If ’e’s straight ’e’ll do for me, and if he ain’t I’ll do for ’im. See? An’ now you and me’ll have a word or two particler, and settle up about this ’ere job. I got the plan drawed out. It’s a easy job as ever I see. Seems to me Tuesday’s as good a day as any. Tip-topper﻿—Sir Edward Talbot, that’s ’im﻿—’e’s in furrin parts for ’is ’ealth, ’e is. Comes ’ome end o’ next month. Little surprise for ’im, eh? You’ll ’ave to train it. Abrams ’e’ll be there Monday. And see ’ere﻿ ﻿…” He sank his voice to a whisper.

===Chinese===
「实话跟你说吧，」比尔一脸十分坦荡的神气说，「我就是服他的机灵。倒不是我昏了头稀罕他——可他实在是机灵。」

「我盼着他机灵得能听话就行。把嘴闭紧——就这么回事。」

「他机灵着呢，什么都难不倒，」比尔说，「嘴又严得像蜡封的。他有个银做的小玩意儿，藏在什么地方——当了也就值一先令——你当他肯告诉我搁哪儿了？才不呢。咱们俩这么好的交情，他的嘴又整天叭叭说个不停，可这桩事就是不漏。」

「行了，别提了！」那人不耐烦地说；「他的事我不想再听了。他要是走正道，就归我使唤；不走正道，我就废了他。懂吗？眼下咱俩得单说几句要紧的，把这桩活计定下来。图我都画好了。这活儿是我见过最容易的一桩。依我看，礼拜二就是个好日子。那位顶儿尖儿的人物——爱德华·塔尔博特爵士（Sir Edward Talbot），就是他——眼下在国外养身体呢，他。下个月底才回来。到时给他个小意外，啊？你得坐火车去。艾布拉姆斯（Abrams）礼拜一就到那儿。还有，听着……」他把嗓音压成了耳语。

===Original===
When Dickie came back, without mushrooms, the red-whiskered man was gone.

“See that bloke just now?” said Mr. Beale.

“Yuss,” said Dickie.

“Well, you never see ’im. If anyone arsts you if you ever see ’im, you never set eyes on ’im in all your born﻿—not to remember ’im. Might a passed ’im in a crowd﻿—see?”

“Yuss,” said Dickie again.

===Chinese===
迪基回来了，蘑菇自然没有，红络腮胡的男人已经走了。

「方才那人你瞧见了？」比尔先生问。

「瞧见了，」迪基说。

「那你就是没瞧见。往后谁要是问起你见过他没有，你这辈子压根儿就没见过他这号人——连想都想不起来。顶多算在人堆里打他跟前走过一回——懂吗？」

「懂了，」迪基又说。

===Original===
“ ’Tasn’t been ’arf a panto neither! Us two on the road,” Mr. Beale went on.

“Not ’arf!”

“Well, now we’re a-goin’ in the train like dooks﻿—an’ after that we’re a-goin’ to ’ave a rare old beano. I give you *my* word!”

Dickie was full of questions, but Mr. Beale had no answers for them. “You jes’ wait;” “hold on a bit;” “them as lives longest sees most”﻿—these were the sort of remarks which were all that Dickie could get out of him.

===Chinese===
「这一趟日子过得也真跟台上的戏一样热闹！咱爷儿俩一路同行，」比尔先生接着说。

「可不嘛！」

「得，往后咱们就要像公爵似的坐火车了——坐完火车，还要好好乐上一场呢。我拿*我*的人格担保！」

迪基满肚子的问题，比尔先生却一概不答。「你就等着吧」「再忍一忍」「活得长的见得多」——迪基从他嘴里掏出来的，无非是这一类的话。

===Original===
It was not the next day, which was a Saturday, that they took the train like dukes. Nor was it Sunday, on which they took a rest and washed their shirts, according to Mr. Beale’s rule of life.

They took the train on Monday, and it landed them in a very bright town by the sea. Its pavements were of red brick and its houses of white stone, and its bow-windows and balconies were green, and Dickie thought it was the prettiest town in the world. They did not stay there, but walked out across the downs, where the skylarks were singing, and on a dip of the downs came upon great stone walls and towers very strong and gray.

“What’s that there?” said Dickie.

“It’s a carstle﻿—like wot the King’s got at Windsor.”

“Is it a king as lives ’ere, then?” Dickie asked.

===Chinese===
他们并没有在第二天，也就是礼拜六，像公爵似的坐上火车。礼拜天也没动身；按比尔先生的处世规矩，礼拜天要歇息，还要洗衬衫。

他们是礼拜一上的火车，火车把他们拉到一座非常明丽的海边小城。城里的人行道铺着红砖，房屋是白石砌的，凸肚窗和阳台都漆成绿色，迪基觉得这是天底下最美的小城。他们没有停留，而是穿出城去，走过云雀啼啭的草坡旷野；在草坡一处低洼的地方，迎面出现了又坚固又灰白的高大石墙和塔楼。

「那是什么？」迪基问。

「那是一座城堡——就跟国王在温莎（Windsor）住的那种一样。」

「那住在这儿的是位国王喽？」迪基问。

===Original===
“No! Nobody don’t live ’ere, mate,” said Mr. Beale. “It’s a ruin, this is. Only howls and rats lives in ruins.”

“Did anyone ever live in it?”

“I shouldn’t wonder,” said Mr. Beale indifferently. “Yes, course they must ’ave, come to think of it. But you learned all that at school. It’s what they call ’ist’ry.”

Dickie, after some reflection, said, “D’jever ’ear of Here Ward?”

“I knowed a Jake Ward wunst.”

===Chinese===
「不是！这儿谁也不住，伙计，」比尔先生说。「这是一座废墟。废墟里就只住猫头鹰和老鼠。」

「从前有人在这里住过吗？」

「那还用说，」比尔先生心不在焉地说。「有，当然住过，仔细想想是准有的。不过这些你上学都学过。就是人家说的什么『历史』。」

迪基想了想，说：「你听说过『赫里·沃德』（Here Ward）没有？」

「从前认得一个杰克·沃德（Jake Ward）。」

===Original===
“Here Ward the Wake. He ain’t a bloke you’d know﻿—*’e’s* in ’istry. Tell you if you like.”

The tale of Hereward the Wake lasted till the jolting perambulator came to anchor in a hollow place among thick furze bushes. The bare, thick stems of the furze held it up like a roof over their heads as they sat. It was like a little furze house.

Next morning Mr. Beale shaved, a thing he had not done since they left London. Dickie held the mug and the soap. It was great fun, and, afterwards, Mr. Beale looked quite different. That was great fun too. And he got quite a different set of clothes out of his bundles, and put them on. And that was the greatest fun of all.

===Chinese===
「是『觉醒者』赫里·沃德（Hereward the Wake）。这号人物你不会认得——他可是进了*史书*的。你要听，我就讲给你。」

觉醒者赫里沃德的故事一直讲到咯噔咯噔的婴儿车像船抛锚似的，在密密荆豆丛中的一处洼地里停稳。他们坐着的时候，荆豆光秃秃的粗枝在头顶交搭起来，像给撑了个屋顶，俨然一座小小的荆豆屋。

第二天早晨，比尔先生刮了脸——打从离开伦敦，这还是头一回。迪基替他端着杯子、捧着肥皂。这真有趣；刮完以后，比尔先生看起来全变了个人，这也有趣极了。接着他又从包袱里翻出一套全然不同的衣裳穿在身上。那才是最有趣的一件事。

===Original===
“Now, then,” he said, “we’re a-goin’ to lay low ’ere all d’y, we are. And then come evening we’re a-goin’ to ’ave our beano. That red’eaded chap wot you never see ’e’ll lift you up to a window what’s got bars to it, and you’ll creep through, you being so little, and you’ll go soft’s a mouse the way I’ll show you, and undo the side-door. There’s a key and a chain and a bottom bolt. The top bolt’s cut through, and all the others is oiled. That won’t frighten you, will it?”

“No,” said Dickie. “What should it frighten me for?”

“Well, it’s like this,” said Mr. Beale a little embarrassed. “Suppose you was to get pinched?”

“What ’ud pinch me? A dawg?”

“There won’t be no dawg. A man, or a lady, or somebody in the ’ouse. Supposen they was to nab you﻿—what ’ud you say?”

===Chinese===
「听着，」他说，「今天一整天，咱们就在这儿猫着，猫他一整天。到了晚上，咱们就去乐上一场。那个你从没见过的红头发家伙，会把你举到一扇带铁栏的窗户跟前，你人小，能从铁栏中间爬进去，再照我教你的法子，轻得像只耗子似的走过去，把侧门打开。那门上有钥匙、有链子、还有一道下插销。顶上那道插销已经锯断了，其余的都上了油。这不会叫你害怕吧？」

「不怕，」迪基说。「这有什么可怕的？」

「是这么回事，」比尔先生有点儿发窘。「万一你叫人逮住了呢？」

「什么会逮我？狗吗？」

「不会有什么狗。是个人，或是位太太，或是宅子里随便什么人。假如他们把你逮住了——你怎么说？」

===Original===
Dickie was watching his face carefully.

“Whatever you tells me to say,” he said.

The man slapped his leg gently.

“If that ain’t the nipper all over! Well, if they was to nab you, you just say what I tells you to. And then, first chance you get, you slip away from ’em and go to the station. An’ if they comes arter you, you say you’re a-goin’ to your father at Dover. And first chance you get you slip off, and you come to that ’ouse where you and me slep’ at Gravesend. I’ve got the dibs for yer ticket done up in this ’ere belt I’m a-goin’ to put on you. But don’t you let on to anyone it’s Gravesend you’re a-coming to. See?”

“An’ if I don’t get pinched?”

===Chinese===
迪基仔细地观察着他的脸色。

「你教我说什么，我就说什么，」他说。

那汉子轻轻一拍大腿。

「瞧瞧，这才叫小家伙！听着，要是他们把你逮住，你就照我教的说。然后，一有机会，你就从他们手里溜走，上火车站去。要是他们追上来，你就说你是上多佛尔（Dover）找你爸爸去。再一有机会你就溜出来，上格雷夫森德（Gravesend）去，到那座你和我一块儿睡过觉的房子去。你车票的钱我给你裹在这条腰带里，一会儿就给你系上。可千万别对任何人露口风，说你是上格雷夫森德去的。懂吗？」

「那要是没人逮我呢？」

===Original===
“Then you just opens the door and me and that redheaded bloke we comes in.”

“What for?” asked Dickie.

“To look for some tools ’e mislaid there a year ago when ’e was on a plumbing job﻿—and they won’t let ’im ’ave them back, not by fair means, they won’t. That’s what for.”

“Rats!” said Dickie briefly. “I ain’t a baby. It’s burgling, that’s what it is.”

“You’ll a jolly sight too fond of calling names,” said Beale anxiously. “Never mind what it is. You be a good boy, matey, and do what you’re told. That’s what you do. You know ’ow to stick it on if you’re pinched. If you ain’t you just lay low till we comes out with the﻿ ﻿… the plumber’s tools. See?”

===Chinese===
「那你就把门打开，我和那个红头发家伙就进去。」

「进去干什么？」迪基问。

「去找他一年前在这儿干水管活儿时落下的几件工具——人家不肯还给他，好说好商量也不肯，就是不还。就为这个。」

「瞎说！」迪基干脆地说。「我又不是小娃娃。这是入室偷盗，就是那么回事。」

「你这孩子就是嘴上太没遮拦，什么都敢叫破，」比尔发愁地说。「别管它叫什么。你做个乖孩子，好伙计，叫你干什么就干什么，这就成了。你被逮住了，知道该怎么演。没被逮住呢，你就安安静静猫着，等我们拿着那……那水管匠的工具出来。明白吗？」

===Original===
“And if I’m nabbed, what is it I am to say?”

“You must let on as a strange chap collared you on the road, a strange chap with a black beard and a red ’ankercher, and give you a licking if you didn’t go and climb in at the window. Say you lost your father in the town, and this chap said he knew where ’e was, and if you see me you don’t know me. Nor yet that redheaded chap wot you never see.” He looked down at the small, earnest face turned up to his own. “You *are* a little nipper,” he said affectionately. “I don’t know as I ever noticed before quite wot a little ’un you was. Think you can stick it? You shan’t go without you wants to, matey. There!”

“It’s splendid!” said Dickie; “it is an adventure for a bold knight. I shall feel like Here Ward when he dressed in the potter’s clothes and went to see King William.”

He spoke in the book voice.

“There you go,” said Mr. Beale, “but don’t you go and talk to ’em like that if they pinches you; they’d never let you loose again. Think they’d got a marquis in disguise, so they would.”

===Chinese===
「要是我被逮住了，我该说什么来着？」

「你就说，路上有个怪汉子揪住了你，一个黑胡子、系红手巾的怪汉子，说你不从窗户爬进去就揍你。就说你进城时把爸爸弄丢了，这汉子说他知道爸爸在哪儿。还说，我你要是见了，就说认不得我。那个你从没见过的红头发家伙，也照样认不得。」他低头看着那张仰起来对着自己的、认真专注的小脸。「你真是个了不起的小家伙，」他亲昵地说，「我先前还真没留意，你原来这么小。你觉得自己撑得住吗？你要不愿意，就不许去，好伙计。就这么说定了！」

「太棒了！」迪基说；「这是一场只有勇敢的骑士才配的冒险。我要像赫里·沃德那样，他穿上陶工的衣裳，去见威廉国王（King William）。」

他用的是念书的腔调。

「瞧你，又来了，」比尔先生说，「不过万一叫他们逮住，可别拿这种腔调跟他们讲话，不然他们再也不放你走了。非得把你当成一位微服出游的侯爵不可。」

===Original===
Dickie thought all day about this great adventure. He did not tell Mr. Beale so, but he was very proud of being so trusted. If you come to think of it, burgling must be a very exciting profession. And Dickie had no idea that it was wrong. It seemed to him a wholly delightful and sporting amusement.

While he was exploring the fox-runs among the thick stems of the grass Mr. Beale lay at full length and pondered.

“I don’t more’n ’arf like it,” he said to himself. “Ho yuss. I know that’s wot I got him for﻿—all right. But ’e’s such a jolly little nipper. I wouldn’t like anything to ’appen to ’im, so I wouldn’t.”

Dickie took his boots off and went to sleep as usual, and in the middle of the night Mr. Beale woke him up and said, “It’s time.”

===Chinese===
迪基一整天都在想着这场大冒险。这话他没对比尔先生讲，但被人这样信任，他心里非常自豪。你仔细想想就会明白，入室行盗准是一行极其刺激的营生。迪基根本不知道那是错的。在他看来，这纯粹是一种有趣而够气派的消遣。

迪基在草丛的长梗之间沿着狐狸蹚出的小径钻来钻去时，比尔先生直挺挺地躺着，心里盘算。

「我心里可不是顶情愿，」他自言自语。「哼，是啊。我知道当初弄他就是为的这个——行，没问题。可他是个多招人疼的小家伙呀。我可不乐意他出个什么事儿，真的不乐意。」

迪基照例脱了靴子睡觉。半夜里，比尔先生把他叫醒，说：「到时候了。」

===Original===
There was no moon that night, and it was very, very dark. Mr. Beale carried Dickie on his back for what seemed a very long way along dark roads, under dark trees, and over dark meadows. A dark bush divided itself into two parts and one part came surprisingly towards them. It turned out to be the red-whiskered man, and presently from a ditch another man came. And they all climbed a chill, damp park-fence, and crept along among trees and shrubs along the inside of a high park wall. Dickie, still on Mr. Beale’s shoulders, was astonished to find how quietly this big, clumsy-looking man could move.

Through openings in the trees and bushes Dickie could see the wide park, like a spread shadow, dotted with trees that were like shadows too. And on the other side of it the white face of a great house showed only a little paler than the trees about it. There were no lights in the house.

They got quite close to it before the shelter of the trees ended, for a little wood lay between the wall and the house.

Dickie’s heart was beating very fast. Quite soon, now, his part in the adventure would begin.

===Chinese===
那天夜里没有月亮，天黑得不能再黑。比尔先生背着迪基，沿着黑魆魆的道路，穿过黑魆魆的树木，跨过黑魆魆的草地，走了仿佛很长很长的一段。一团黑影忽然分作两半，其中一半竟朝着他们迎面而来——原来是红络腮胡的男人；不多会儿，沟里又钻出另一个汉子。随后他们一齐翻过一道又冷又潮的庄园栅栏，沿着高高的庄园墙内侧，在树木灌木间悄悄前行。迪基还伏在比尔先生肩上，惊讶地发现这个大块头、看上去笨手笨脚的汉子，走起路来竟这样没有一点声响。

从树木灌木的隙缝间望出去，迪基看得见宽阔的庄园，像一摊铺开的影子，星星点点长着的树也像影子。在庄园的那一头，一座大宅的白色正面只比四下的树色淡那么一点点。宅子里没有一点灯火。

等到藏身的树影到了头，他们已经离宅子很近了，因为墙和宅子之间隔着一片小树林。

迪基的心跳得飞快。很快，很快，这场冒险里他那一角戏就要开演了。

===Original===
“ ’Ere﻿—catch ’old,” Mr. Beale was saying, and the red-whiskered man took Dickie in his arms, and went forward. The other two crouched in the wood.

Dickie felt himself lifted, and caught at the windowsill with his hands. It was a damp night and smelled of earth and dead leaves. The windowsill was of stone, very cold. Dickie knew exactly what to do. Mr. Beale had explained it over and over again all day. He settled himself on the broad window-ledge and held on to the iron window-bars while the red-whiskered man took out a pane of glass, with treacle and a handkerchief, so that there should be no noise of breaking or falling glass. Then Dickie put his hand through and unfastened the window, which opened like a cupboard door. Then he put his feet through the narrow space between two bars and slid through. He hung inside with his hands holding the bars, till his foot found the table that he had been told to expect just below, and he got from that to the floor.

“Now I must remember exactly which way to go,” he told himself. But he did not need to remember what he had been told. For quite certainly, and most oddly, he *knew* exactly where the door was, and when he had crept to it and got it open he found that he now knew quite well which way to turn and what passages to go along to get to that little side-door that he was to open for the three men. It was exactly as though he had been there before, in a dream. He went as quietly as a mouse, creeping on hands and knee, the lame foot dragging quietly behind him.

I will not pretend that he was not frightened. He was, very. But he was more brave than he was frightened, which is the essence of bravery, after all. He found it difficult to breathe quietly, and his heart beat so loudly that he felt almost sure that if any people were awake in the house they would hear it, even upstairs in their beds. But he got to the little side-door, and feeling with sensitive, quick fingers found the well-oiled bolt, and shot it back. Then the chain﻿—holding the loose loop of it in his hand so that it should not rattle, he slipped its ball from the socket. Only the turning of the key remained, and Dickie accomplished that with both hands, for it was a big key, kneeling on his one sound knee. Then very gently he turned the handle, and pulled﻿—and the door opened, and he crept from behind it and felt the cool, sweet air of the night on his face.

It seemed to him that he had never known what silence was before﻿—or darkness. For the door opened into a close box arbor, and no sky could be seen, or any shapes of things. Dickie felt himself almost bursting with pride. What an adventure! And he had carried out his part of it perfectly. He had done exactly what he had been told to do, and he had done it well. He stood there, on his one useful foot, clinging to the edge of the door, and it was not until something touched him that he knew that Mr. Beale and the other men were creeping through the door that he had opened.

===Chinese===
「来——接住了，」比尔先生正说着，红络腮胡的男人便把迪基抱进怀里，朝前走去。另外两个汉子蹲在林子里。

迪基觉得自己被举了起来，双手一把攀住窗台。夜是潮的，有泥土和枯叶的气味。窗台是石头的，冰凉。迪基清清楚楚地知道该做什么——比尔先生一整天翻来覆去地交代过。他先在宽宽的窗槛上坐稳，一手抓住铁窗栏；红络腮胡的男人用糖浆和一方手帕起下一块窗玻璃，一点打碎或落地的声响都没有。接着迪基把手伸进去，拨开了窗闩，那窗像橱柜门似的开着。然后他把两只脚从两根铁栏之间那道窄缝里伸过去，身子一滑钻了进去。他双手抓着窗栏，悬在里头，直到脚尖碰到事先说好就在窗下的一张桌子，再从桌子上落到地板。

「现在我得好好记住该往哪边走，」他对自己说。可他根本用不着去记别人教的话。因为千真万确、也最最奇怪的是，他*知道*门在哪儿；等他悄悄爬过去把门打开，他发现自己清清楚楚知道该往哪边拐、该顺着哪几条走廊走，才能到那扇他要去替三个汉子打开的小侧门。简直就像他从前在梦里来过这里。他轻得像只耗子，手和膝盖着地爬着走，那条瘸腿在身后无声地拖行。

我不想装作说他不害怕。他怕，怕得很。可他心里的勇敢压过了害怕——而勇敢的本质，说到底就在这里。他觉得自己难以平稳地呼吸，心跳得咚咚响，他几乎断定，宅子里但凡有人醒着，哪怕睡在楼上的床上，也听得见。可他还是到了那扇小侧门前，灵敏敏捷的手指一摸，就摸到了上足油的插销，把它拨了回去。接着是链子——他把松脱的链环攥在手心里不叫它响，把链球从窝里褪了出来。只剩下拧钥匙了，迪基单跪在好腿那条膝上，用两只手一齐拧开了那把大钥匙。然后他极轻极轻地转动门把，一拉——门开了，他从门后闪出来，夜里的凉气又清又甜，扑在脸上。

他觉得，自己从前根本不知道什么叫寂静——也不知道什么叫黑暗。因为门开出去是一座密不透风的匣式凉亭，看不见天，也看不见任何东西的影子。迪基几乎骄傲得要炸开了。多么了不起的冒险！而且他把分内的事完成得十全十美。教他做什么，他就做了什么，做得漂漂亮亮。他站在那儿，凭着唯一那只管用的脚，攀着门边；直到有什么东西碰了碰他，他才知道比尔先生和其余的人，正从他打开的门里悄悄鱼贯而入。

===Original===
And at that touch a most odd feeling came to Dickie﻿—the last feeling he would have expected﻿—a feeling of pride mixed with a feeling of shame. Pride in his own cleverness, and another kind of pride that made that cleverness seem shameful. He had a feeling, very queer and very strong, that he, Dickie, was not the sort of person to open doors for the letting in of burglars. He felt as you would feel if you suddenly found your hands covered with filth, not good honest dirt, but slimy filth, and would not understand how you could have let it get there.

He caught at the third shape that brushed by him.

“Father,” he whispered, “don’t do it. Go back, and I’ll fasten it all up again. Oh! don’t, father.”

“Shut your mug!” whispered the red-whiskered man. Dickie knew his voice even in that velvet-black darkness. “Shut your mug, or I’ll give you what for!”

“Don’t, father,” said Dickie, and said it all the more for that threat.

===Chinese===
就是这一碰，一种极古怪的感觉涌上了迪基的心头——那是他怎么也料不到的感觉——一种自豪混着羞耻的感觉。自豪的是自己的机灵；可另有一种自豪，恰恰叫这机灵显得可耻。他有一种非常奇怪又非常强烈的感受：他迪基，不是那种替贼开门、放贼进来的人。这感觉就像你忽然发现自己的两手沾满了污秽——不是堂堂正正的干净泥土，而是黏糊糊的龌龊——而且不明白它们是怎么爬上你的手来的。

第三个黑影从他身边擦过时，他一把抓住了它。

「爸爸，」他悄声说，「别干了吧。回去吧，我把门再照原样关好拴牢。哦！别去，爸爸。」

「闭嘴！」红络腮胡的男人低声喝道。哪怕在那种天鹅绒似的黑夜里，迪基也听得出他的嗓门。「闭嘴，不然给你好看！」

「别去吧，爸爸，」迪基说；正因为这句恐吓，他反倒把这话越说越响。

===Original===
“I can’t go back on my pals, matey,” said Mr. Beale; “you see that, don’t yer?”

Dickie did see. The adventure was begun: it was impossible to stop. It was helped and had to be eaten, as they say in Norfolk. He crouched behind the open door, and heard the soft pad-pad of the three men’s feet on the stones of the passage grow fainter and fainter. They had woolen socks over their boots, which made their footsteps sound no louder than those of padded pussy-feet. Then the soft rustle-pad died away, and it was perfectly quiet, perfectly dark. Dickie was tired; it was long past his proper bedtime, and the exertion of being so extra clever had been very tiring. He was almost asleep when a crack like thunder brought him stark, staring awake﻿—there was a noise of feet on the stairs, boots, a blundering, hurried rush. People came rushing past him. There was another sharp thunder sound and a flash like lightning, only much smaller. Someone tripped and fell; there was a clatter like pails, and something hard and smooth hit him on the knee. Then another hurried presence dashed past him into the quiet night. Another﻿—No! there was a woman’s voice.

“Edward, you shan’t! Let them go! You shan’t﻿—no!”

===Chinese===
「我不能背着伙计们打退堂鼓，好伙计，」比尔先生说；「这个理儿你明白，对不对？」

迪基确实明白。冒险已经开了头，再收不住了。正如诺福克人说的：菜既然盛上来了，就非吃下去不可。他蜷在敞开的门后，听着三个汉子的脚步在走廊石板上轻柔的橐橐声一点一点远下去。他们的靴子外头套着毛线袜，走起路来不过像垫了脚掌的猫爪。随后那轻轻的窸窣橐橐声消失干净，四下里静透了，也黑透了。迪基累了；早就过了他平常上床的钟点，而「格外机灵」原是件很费力气的事。他几乎要睡着了，忽然一声炸雷似的脆响把他惊得直挺挺地瞪着眼醒过来——楼梯上响起脚步声，是靴子，跌跌撞撞、慌慌张张的一阵乱跑。有人从他身边冲过去。又是一声尖锐的炸响，还有一道闪电似的光，只是小得多。有人绊了一跤摔倒，一阵水桶落地似的哐当乱响，随后一样又硬又滑的东西磕在他的膝盖上。接着又一个急匆匆的人影从他身边冲出去，冲进静静的夜里。又是一个——不！那是个女人的声音。

「爱德华，不行！让他们跑！不许追——不行！」

===Original===
And suddenly there was a light that made one wink and blink. A tall lady in white, carrying a lamp, swept down the stairs and caught at a man who sprang into being out of the darkness into the lamplight.

“Take the lamp,” she said, and thrust it on him. Then with unbelievable quickness she bolted and chained the door, locked it, and, turning, saw Dickie.

“What’s this?” she said. “Oh, Edward, quick﻿—here’s one of them!﻿ ﻿… Why﻿—it’s a child﻿—”

Some more people were coming down the stairs, with candles and excited voices. Their clothes were oddly bright. Dickie had never seen dressing-gowns before. They moved in a very odd way, and then began to go round and round like tops.

The next thing that Dickie remembers is being in a room that seemed full of people and lights and wonderful furniture, with someone holding a glass to his lips, a little glass, that smelled of public-houses, very nasty.

===Chinese===
忽然亮起一道光，晃得人直眨眼。一位穿白衣的高个子太太提着一盏灯，一阵风似的掠下楼梯，一把揪住一个男人——那男人方才还藏在黑暗里，灯光一亮，就凭空现出了身形。

「拿着灯，」她说着把灯塞给他。随即以难以置信的利落，插上插销、挂好链子、锁上锁，一转身，看见了迪基。

「这是怎么回事？」她说。「哦，爱德华，快来——这儿有一个！……咦——是个孩子——」

又有几个人从楼梯上下来，端着蜡烛，声音激动。他们的衣裳亮得出奇。迪基以前从没见过睡袍。他们走动的样子也怪极了，随后竟像陀螺似的打起转来。

迪基记得的下一件事，就是自己已经在一间屋子里了，那屋子仿佛满是人和灯，满是稀奇的家具；有人把一只杯子凑到他嘴边，一只小杯子，一股酒馆里的气味，难喝极了。

===Original===
“No!” said Dickie, turning away his head.

“Better?” asked a lady; and Dickie was astonished to find that he was on her lap.

“Yes, thank you,” he said, and tried to sit up, but lay back again because that was so much more pleasant. He had had no idea that anyone’s lap could be so comfortable.

“Now, young man,” said a stern voice that was not a lady’s, “just you tell us how you came here, and who put you up to it.”

“I got in,” said Dickie feebly, “through the butler’s pantry window,” and as he said it he wondered how he had known that it was the butler’s pantry. It is certain that no one had told him.

===Chinese===
「不！」迪基把头扭开。

「好些了吗？」一位太太问；迪基吃惊地发现自己正坐在她的膝头上。

「好些了，谢谢您，」他说。他想坐起来，可又躺了回去，因为躺着舒服得多。他从来不知道，人的膝头竟有这样舒坦。

「好啦，小伙子，」一个严厉的、可不是太太的声音说，「你就说说你是怎么进来的，是谁指使你干的。」

「我是从，」迪基有气无力地说，「管家餐具室的窗户进来的。」话一出口，他自己也纳闷起来：怎么会知道那是管家餐具室呢？可以肯定，没有人对他说过。

===Original===
“What for?” asked the voice, which Dickie now perceived came from a gentleman in rumpled hair and a very loose pink flannel suit, with cordy things on it such as soldiers have.

“To let﻿—” Dickie stopped. This was the moment he had been so carefully prepared for. He must think what he was saying.

“Yes,” said the lady gently, “it’s all right﻿—poor little chap, don’t be frightened﻿—nobody wants to hurt you!”

“I’m not frightened,” said Dickie﻿—“not now.”

“To let﻿—?” reminded the lady, persuasively.

===Chinese===
「进来干什么？」那声音问。迪基这才看出来，声音来自一位头发蓬乱的先生，穿着一身松垮垮的粉红法兰绒衣裳，上面缀着军人系的那一种带绳。

「为了让——」迪基收住了话头。这正是他反复演练、专等此刻的那个关口。他得想想自己在说什么。

「说吧，」太太温声说，「不要紧——可怜的小家伙，别怕——没人要伤害你！」

「我不怕，」迪基说——「现在不怕了。」

「为了让——？」太太温言劝诱地提醒。

===Original===
“To let the man in.”

“What man?”

“I dunno.”

“There were three or four of them,” said the gentleman in pink; “four or five﻿—”

“What man, dear?” the lady asked again.

===Chinese===
「为了让那个男人进来。」

「什么男人？」

「不知道。」

「他们有三四个，」穿粉红衣裳的先生说；「四五个——」

「什么男人，好孩子？」太太又问。

===Original===
“The man as said ’e knew w’ere my farver was,” said Dickie, remembering what he had been told to say; “so I went along of ’im, an’ then in the wood ’e said ’e’d give me a dressing down if I didn’t get through the winder and open the door; ’e said ’e’d left some tools ’ere and you wouldn’t let ’im ’ave them.”

“You see,” said the lady, “the child didn’t know. He’s perfectly innocent.” And she kissed Dickie’s hair very softly and kindly.

Dickie did not understand then why he suddenly felt as though he were going to choke. His head felt as though it were going to burst. His ears grew very hot, and his hands and feet very cold.

“I know’d right enough,” he said suddenly and hoarsely; “an’ I needn’t agone if I ’adn’t wanted to.”

“He’s feverish,” said the lady, “he doesn’t know what he’s saying. Look how flushed he is.”

===Chinese===
「就是那个说他知道我爸爸在哪儿的男人，」迪基说，心里记着人家教他的话；「我就跟他走了一路，后来在树林里他说，我要是不从窗户爬进去把门打开，就揍我；他说他有几件工具落在这儿，你们不肯还给他。」

「你们看，」太太说，「这孩子什么都不懂。他完全是无辜的。」她轻轻地、慈爱地吻了吻迪基的头发。

迪基当时不明白，自己为什么会忽然觉得透不过气来。他觉得脑袋像要炸开。耳朵烫得厉害，手脚却冰凉冰凉。

「我心里明白着呢，」他忽然哑着嗓子说；「我要不是自己乐意，根本就不会来。」

「他在发烧，」太太说，「他说的是胡话。瞧他脸红成什么样了。」

===Original===
“I wanted to,” said Dickie; “I thought it ’ud be a lark. And it was too.”

He expected to be shaken and put down. He wondered where his crutch was. Mr. Beale had had it under his arm. How could he get to Gravesend without a crutch? But he wasn’t shaken or put down; instead, the lady gathered him up in her arms and stood up, holding him.

“I shall put him to bed,” she said; “you shan’t ask him any more questions tonight. There’s time enough in the morning.”

She carried Dickie out of the drawing-room and away from the other people to a big room with blue walls and blue and gray curtains and beautiful furniture. There was a high four-post bed with blue silk curtains and more pillows than Dickie had ever seen before. The lady washed him with sweet-smelling water in a big basin with blue and gold flowers on it, dressed him in a lace-trimmed nightgown, which must have been her own, for it was much too big for any little boy.

Then she put him into the soft, warm bed that was like a giant’s pillow, tucked him up and kissed him. Dickie put thin arms round her neck.

===Chinese===
「我是愿意的，」迪基说；「我原当它是场好玩的把戏。它也真是。」

他等着挨一顿摇晃，等着被放下来。他惦记拐杖在哪儿。比尔先生一直把它夹在胳膊底下。没有拐杖，他怎么走到格雷夫森德去呢？可是没人摇晃他，也没人放下他；太太反倒把他拢进怀里，抱着他站起身来。

「我带他去睡，」她说；「今晚谁也不许再问他话。明天早晨有的是工夫。」

她抱着迪基走出客厅，离开那些人，进了一间大屋：蓝墙，蓝灰两色的帘幕，精美的家具。屋里有一张高高的四柱大床，挂着蓝绸帐，枕头多得迪基从没见过。太太用一只描着蓝金花卉的大盆，盛着香喷喷的水给他洗了身子，又给他换上一件镶花边的睡袍——那想必是她自己的，因为哪个小男孩也穿不下这么大的。

然后她把他放进那张柔软温暖的床里，那床像一个巨人的枕头。她掖好被子，亲了亲他。迪基伸出细细的胳膊，搂住她的脖子。

===Original===
“I do like you,” he said, “but I want farver.”

“Where is he? No, you must tell me that in the morning. Drink up this milk”﻿—she had it ready in a glass that sparkled in a pattern﻿—“and then go sound asleep. Everything will be all right, dear.”

“May Heavens,” said Dickie, sleepily, “bless you, generous Bean Factress!”

===Chinese===
「我真喜欢你，」他说，「可是我想爸爸。」

「他在哪儿？不，这个明早再告诉我。把这杯牛奶喝光」——她已经把奶备好，盛在一只刻着花纹、晶晶发亮的玻璃杯里——「然后甜甜地睡吧。一切都会好好的，宝贝。」

「愿上天，」迪基睡意蒙眬地说，「保佑您，慷慨的恩人婆婆！」

===Original===
---

“A most extraordinary child,” said the lady, returning to her husband. “I can’t think who it is that he reminds me of. Where are the others?”

“I packed them off to bed. There’s nothing to be done,” said her husband. “We ought to have gone after those men.”

“They didn’t get anything,” she said.

“No﻿—dropped it all when I fired. Come on, let’s turn in. Poor Eleanor, you must be worn out.”

===Chinese===
---

「真是个顶出奇的孩子，」太太回到丈夫身边说。「我怎么也想不起来，他到底像谁。其他人呢？」

「我都把他们赶去睡了。眼下没什么可做的了，」她丈夫说。「本来该追上那几个人才是。」

「他们什么也没拿着，」她说。

「可不是——我一开枪，他们就全撂下了。走吧，睡去。可怜的埃莉诺（Eleanor），你一定累坏了。」

===Original===
“Edward,” said the lady, “I wish we could adopt that little boy. I’m sure he comes of good people﻿—he’s been kidnapped or something.”

“Don’t be a dear silly one!” said Sir Edward.

---

That night Dickie slept in sheets of the finest linen, scented with lavender. He was sunk downily among pillows, and over him lay a down quilt covered with blue-flowered satin. On the footboard of the great bed was carved a shield and a great dog on it.

Dickie’s clothes lay, a dusty, forlorn little heap, in a stately tapestry-covered chair. And he slept, and dreamed of Mr. Beale, and the little house among the furze, and the bed with the green curtains.

===Chinese===
「爱德华，」太太说，「我倒真想收养那个小男孩。我敢说他出身于好人家的——多半是给拐走了，要不就是怎么一回事。」

「别犯傻了，我的好人儿！」爱德华爵士说。

---

那一夜，迪基睡在最细最细的亚麻被单里，带着薰衣草的香气。他软乎乎地陷在枕头中间，身上盖着一床鸭绒被，被面是蓝花的缎子。大床的床尾板上雕着一面盾牌，盾牌上刻着一条大狗。

迪基的衣裳堆在一把铺着织锦套子的高背椅上，一小堆，灰扑扑的，孤零零的。他睡着了，梦见比尔先生，梦见荆豆丛中的小房子，梦见那张挂着绿帐子的床。

## VI Buried Treasure / 六 埋藏的宝藏

===Original===
And then, as he stood there in the sunshine, he suddenly knew.

Having succeeded in dreaming once again the dream which he had so longed to dream, Dickie Harding looked out of the window of the dream-house in Deptford into the dream-garden with its cut yew-trees and box avenues and bowling-greens, and perceived without doubt that this was no dream, but real﻿—as real as the other Deptford where he had sown Artistic Bird Seed and gathered moonflowers and reaped the silver seeds of magic, for it *was* magic. Dickie was sure of it now. He had not lived in the time of the First James, be sure, without hearing magic talked of. And it seemed quite plain to him that if this that had happened to him was not magic, then there never was and never would be any magic to happen to anyone. He turned from the window and looked at the tapestry-hung room﻿—the big bed, the pleasant, wrinkled face of the nurse﻿—and he knew that all this was as real as anything that had happened to him in that other life where he was a little lame boy who took the road with a dirty tramp for father, and lay in the bed with green curtains.

“Was thy friend well, in thy dream?” the nurse asked.

===Chinese===
就在这时，他站在阳光里，忽然明白了。

迪基·哈丁（Dickie Harding）终于又一次做成了那个他渴望已久的梦。他从德普特福德（Deptford）那座梦中之屋的窗口望向梦中花园，园里有修剪齐整的紫杉、黄杨夹道的小径和滚木草坪；他毫无疑问地认定：这不是梦，是真的——同另一个德普特福德一样真实。在那另一个德普特福德里，他撒过「艺术鸟食」，收过月光花，收获过魔法的银籽——因为那确确实实是魔法。如今迪基是笃定了。他既然在詹姆士一世的时代住过，总归听人说起过魔法。而且在他看来再明白不过：要是发生在他身上的这桩事不是魔法，那么世上就从来不曾有过、将来也永远不会有什么魔法落到什么人头上。他从窗前转过身，打量这间挂满壁毯的屋子——那张大床，保姆那张和气而多皱的脸——他知道，这一切都同他另一种生活里经历过的任何事一样真实；在那种生活里，他是个瘸腿的小男孩，跟着一个脏兮兮的流浪汉当爹，一起流浪赶路，睡在那张挂着绿帐幔的床上。

「梦中你那位朋友，可安好？」保姆问。

===Original===
“Yes, oh, yes,” said Dickie, “and I carved boxes in my dream, and sold them, and I want to learn a lot more things, so that when I go back again﻿—I mean when I dream that dream again﻿—I shall be able to earn more money.”

“ ’Tis shame that one of thy name should have to work for money,” said the nurse.

“It *isn’t* my name there,” said Dickie; “and old Sebastian told me everyone ought to do some duty to his country, or he wasn’t worth his meat and ale. And you don’t know how good it is having money that you’ve *earned yourself*.”

“I ought to,” she said; “I’ve earned mine long enough. Now haste and dress﻿—and then breakfast and thy fencing lesson.”

===Chinese===
「好，哦，好得很，」迪基说，「我在梦里刻了盒子，还卖了出去。我想再学好多好多本事，这样等我再回去的时候——我是说，等我再做那个梦的时候——我就能挣更多的钱了。」

「你这样姓氏的人，居然要为钱去干活，真是不像话，」保姆说。

「在那边，那可不是我的姓，」迪基说；「再说，老塞巴斯蒂安（Sebastian）告诉过我，人人都该为自己的国家尽一份力，不然就不配吃他那份饭、喝他那份麦酒。你不知道，自己挣来的钱花着有多痛快。」

「我该知道的，」她说；「我的钱我挣得够久啦。快去穿衣裳——然后吃早饭，上你的剑术课。」

===Original===
When the fencing lesson was over, Dickie hesitated. He wanted, of course, to hurry off to Sebastian and to go on learning how to make a galleon. But also he wanted to learn some trade that he could teach Beale at Deptford, and he knew, quite as surely as any master craftsman could have known it, that nothing which required delicate handling, such as woodcarving or the making of toy boats, could ever be mastered by Beale. But Beale was certainly fond of dogs. Dickie remembered how little True had cuddled up to him and nestled inside his coat when he lay down to sleep under the newspapers and the bits of sacking in Lavender Terrace, Rosemary Lane.

So Dickie went his way to the kennels to talk to the kennelman. He had been there before with Master Roger Fry, his fencing master, but he had never spoken to the kennelman. And when he got to the kennels he knocked on the door of the kennelman’s house and called out, “What ho! within there!” just as people do in old plays. And the door was thrown open by a man in a complete suit of leather, and when Dickie looked in that man’s face he saw that it was the face of the man who had lived next door in Lavender Terrace, Rosemary Lane﻿—the man who dug up the garden for the parrot seed.

“Why,” said Dickie, “it’s you!”

===Chinese===
剑术课下了以后，迪基犹豫了一下。他自然想赶紧跑到塞巴斯蒂安那儿去，接着学造大帆船；可他也想学一门手艺，将来好教给德普特福德的比尔（Beale）。而且他心里有数，知道得同任何一位匠师一样确凿：凡是手上要求精细的活计，比如木雕，比如做玩具小船，比尔是一辈子也学不会的。可是比尔确实喜欢狗。迪基还记得小狗特鲁（True）偎到他身上、钻进他大衣里去的样子——那时他正睡在迷迭香巷薰衣草台街（Lavender Terrace, Rosemary Lane）的报纸和麻袋片底下。

于是迪基往犬舍去，想同犬舍管理员谈谈。他先前跟剑术师父罗杰·弗莱（Roger Fry）师傅来过这里，却从没跟犬舍管理员说过话。到了犬舍，他敲了敲犬舍管理员住房的门，像老戏里的人那样喊了一声：「喂——！屋里有人吗！」门砰地开了，开门的是个从头到脚一身皮衣的男人；迪基一望那张脸，就认出这是从前住在迷迭香巷薰衣草台街隔壁的那个男人——那个为了种「鹦鹉食」把园子刨了个底朝天的男人。

「咦，」迪基说，「是你呀！」

===Original===
“Who would it be but me, little master?” the man asked with a respectful salute, and Dickie perceived that though this man had the face of the Man Next Door, he had not the Man Next Door’s memories.

“Do you live here?” he asked cautiously﻿—“always, I mean.”

“Where else should I live?” the man asked, “that have served my lord, your father, all my time, boy and man, and know every hair of every dog my lord owns.”

Dickie thought that was a good deal to know﻿—and so it was.

===Chinese===
「不是我又能是谁呢，小少爷？」那人恭恭敬敬地行了个礼说。迪基随即看出，这人虽然长着「隔壁那位先生」的脸，却没有「隔壁那位先生」的记忆。

「你住在这儿？」他小心地问——「我是说，一直住在这儿。」

「不在这儿还能住在哪儿？」那人说，「我打小到大一直伺候你家老爷——你父亲大人，从孩子当到大人，我家老爷养的每条狗，狗身上每一根毛我都认得。」

迪基觉得，这可真算得上知道得多了——事实上也确实如此。

===Original===
He stayed an hour at the kennels and came away knowing very much more about dogs than he did before, though some of the things he learned would surprise a modern veterinary surgeon very much indeed. But the dogs seemed well and happy, though they were doctored with herb tea instead of stuff from the chemist’s, and the charms that were said over them to make them swift and strong certainly did not make them any the less strong and swift.

When Dickie had learned as much about dogs as he felt he could bear for that day, he felt free to go down to the dockyard and go on learning how ships were built. Sebastian looked up at the voice and ceased the blows with which his axe was smoothing a great tree trunk that was to be a mast, and smiled in answer to his smile.

“Oh, what a long time since I have seen thee!” Dickie cried.

===Chinese===
他在犬舍待了一个钟头，走的时候比先前多懂了好多狗经，虽然他学到的有些东西，准会让现代的兽医大吃一惊。可那些狗看着又健康又快活，尽管喂的是草药汤，而不是药铺里的药；而人们念在它们身上、要叫它们又快又壮的那些咒语，倒也确实没有让它们少壮一分、慢上一步。

迪基觉得，那一天装得下的狗经他已经学够了，这才放开脚步下船坞去，接着学造船。塞巴斯蒂安听见声音抬起头，停住了斧头——他正用斧子削平一根要做桅杆的大树干——微笑着回应迪基的微笑。

「哦，好久好久没见着你啦！」迪基喊道。

===Original===
And Sebastian, gently mocking him, answered, “A great while indeed﻿—two whole long days. And those thou’st spent merrymaking in the King’s water pageant. Two days﻿—a great while, a great, great while.”

“I want you to teach me everything you know,” said Dickie, picking up an awl and feeling its point.

“Have patience with me,” laughed Sebastian; “I will teach thee all thou canst learn, but not all in one while. Little by little, slow and sure.”

“You must not think,” said Dickie, “that it’s only play, and that I do not need to learn because I am my father’s son.”

===Chinese===
塞巴斯蒂安温存地打趣他，答道：「可不是嘛——整整两天呢，长着呢。还是拿去参加国王的水上游船会寻开心了。两天——好久哇，好久好久哇。」

「我要你把你会的全教给我，」迪基说着，拾起一把锥子，摸了摸它的尖。

「耐着性子吧，」塞巴斯蒂安笑了；「你能学多少，我就教多少，可不能一口气全给你。一点一点来，慢工出细活。」

「你可别以为，」迪基说，「这只是玩玩，也别以为我是老爷家的儿子，就用不着学了。」

===Original===
“Should I think so?” Sebastian asked; “I that have sailed with Captain Drake and Captain Raleigh, and seen how a gentleman venturer needs to turn his hand to every guess craft? If thou’s so pleased to learn as Sebastian is to teach, then he’ll be as quick to teach as thou to learn. And so to work!”

He fetched out from the shed the ribs of the little galleon that he and Dickie had begun to put together, and the two set to work on it. It was a happy day. And one happiness was to all the other happinesses of that day as the sun is to little stars﻿—and that happiness was the happiness of being once more a little boy who did not need to use a crutch.

And now the beautiful spacious life opened once more for Dickie, and he learned many things and found the days all good and happy and all the nights white and peaceful, in the big house and the beautiful garden on the slopes above Deptford. And the nights had no dreams in them, and in the days Dickie lived gaily and worthily, the life of the son of a great and noble house, and now he had no prickings of conscience about Beale, left alone in the little house in Deptford. Because one day he said to his nurse﻿—

===Chinese===
「我该这么想吗？」塞巴斯蒂安反问；「我可是跟德雷克船长、雷利船长出过海的，亲眼见过绅士冒险家样样船上的活儿都得拿得起来。你要是学得欢喜，赛过塞巴斯蒂安教得欢喜，那他教起来就会同你学起来一样快。来，干活吧！」

他从棚子里搬出他和迪基已经动手拼装的那只小大帆船的肋骨，两人一齐干了起来。这是快活的一天。这一天一切别的快乐，同其中一样快乐相比，就像小星星衬着太阳——那一样快乐，就是自己又做回了一个用不着拄拐杖的小男孩。

如今，这宽广美好的生活重新向迪基敞开了。他学着许多东西，只觉得日子天天又好又快乐，夜夜又洁白又安宁，住的是德普特福德上方山坡上的大宅和美丽的花园。夜里没有梦；白天，迪基过得又快活又堂堂正正，过的是伟大而高贵的世家的儿子该过的日子。而且他再不为比良心不安了——比尔孤零零地留在德普特福德那座小屋里。因为有一天，他对保姆说——

===Original===
“How long did it take me to dream that dream about making the boxes and earning the money in the ugly place I told you of?”

“Dreams about that place,” she answered him, “take none of *our* time here. And dreams about this place take none of what is time in that other place.”

“But my dream endured all night,” objected Dickie.

“Not so,” said the nurse, smiling between her white cap frills. “It was *after* the dream that sleep came﻿—a whole good nightful of it.”

===Chinese===
「我跟你说过的那个丑地方，我做那个刻盒子、挣钱的梦，花了多长时间？」

「关于那地方的梦，」她回答他，「一点也不占咱们这儿的时间。关于这地方的梦，也一点不占另一个世界里头的时间。」

「可我的梦做了个整夜呀，」迪基不服。

「不是的，」保姆说，白帽檐的褶边间漾出笑来。「是梦做完了，觉才来——满满当当一整夜的好觉。」

===Original===
So Dickie felt that for Beale no time at all had passed, and that when he went back﻿—which he meant to do﻿—he would get back to Deptford at the same instant as he left it. Which is the essence of this particular kind of white magic. And thus it happened that when he did go back to Mr. Beale he went because his heart called him, and not for any other reason at all.

Days and weeks and months went by and it was autumn, and the apples were ripe on the trees, and the grapes ripe on the garden walls and trellises. And then came a day when all the servants seemed suddenly to go mad﻿—a great rushing madness of mops and brooms and dusters and pails and everything in the house already perfectly clean was cleaned anew, and everything that was already polished was polished freshly, and when Dickie had been turned out of three rooms one after the other, had tumbled over a pail and had a dishcloth pinned to his doublet by an angry cook, he sought out the nurse, very busy in the linen-room, and asked her what all the fuss was about.

“It can’t be a spring-cleaning,” he said, “because it’s the wrong time of year.”

===Chinese===
这样，迪基就明白，对比尔来说，连一丁点时间也不曾过去；等他回去——他是一定要回去的——他会在离开的那一刹那回到德普特福德。这正是这一路白魔法顶要紧的奥妙。所以后来他当真回到比尔先生身边去的时候，是因为心里叫他回去，而不是为任何别的缘故。

日子、礼拜、月份一茬茬过去，秋天到了，树上的苹果熟了，园墙和棚架上的葡萄也熟了。后来有一天，满宅的仆人忽然像是都疯了——一场拖着拖把、笤帚、掸子、水桶来去匆匆的疯狂：家里本来干干净净的东西都要再洗刷一遍，本来擦得锃亮的东西都要重新擦亮。迪基接连被赶出三个房间，绊倒过一只水桶，还被一个恼火的厨娘把擦碗布别在他的紧身短上衣上，这才找到正在布巾房里忙着活的保姆，问她这一场大忙乱到底为的什么。

「总不会是春季大扫除吧，」他说，「眼下不是那个季节呀。」

===Original===
“Never say I did not tell thee,” she answered, unfolding a great embroidered cupboard cloth and holding it up critically. “Tomorrow thy father and mother come home, and thy baby-brother, and today sennight thy little cousins come to visit thee.”

“How perfectly glorious!” said Dickie. “But you *didn’t* tell me.”

“If I didn’t ’twas because you never asked.”

“I﻿—I didn’t dare to,” he said dreamily; “I was so afraid. You see, I’ve never seen them.”

===Chinese===
「往后可别说我不曾告诉过你，」她回答，抖开一方绣花的大柜布，举起来端详。「明天你父亲母亲就到家了，还有你那小弟弟；下周的今天，你的两位小表亲也来看你。」

「那可太好了，太好了！」迪基说。「可你确实没告诉过我。」

「我要没告诉，那是因为你从没问过。」

「我——我不敢问，」他恍恍惚惚地说；「我怕呀。你知道，我从没见过他们。」

===Original===
“Afraid?” she said, laying away the folded cloth and taking out another from the deep press, oaken, with smooth-worn, brown iron hinges and lock; “never seen thy father and mother, forsooth!”

“Perhaps it was the fever,” said Dickie, feeling rather deceitful. “You said it made me forget things. I don’t remember them. Not at all, I don’t.”

“Do not say that to them,” the nurse said, looking at him very gravely.

===Chinese===
「怕？」她说，把叠好的布收进那口深柜，又从柜里取出另一方——那是口橡木大柜，棕铁铰链和锁扣都磨得溜光——「居然没见过你的父亲母亲——瞧你说的！」

「兴许是那场热病闹的，」迪基说，自觉颇有几分欺瞒。「你说过，热病害我忘了好多事。我不记得他们。一点儿也不记得。」

「这话可别对他们讲，」保姆说，神色非常严肃地看着他。

===Original===
“I won’t. Unless they ask me,” he added. “Oh, nurse, let me do something too. What can I do to help?”

“Thou canst gather such flowers as are left in the garden to make a nosegay for thy mother’s room; and set them in order in fair water. And bid thy tutor teach thee a welcome song to say to them when they come in.”

Gathering the flowers and arranging them was pleasant and easy. Asking so intimate a favor from the sour-faced tutor whom he so much disliked was neither easy nor pleasant. But Dickie did it. And the tutor was delighted to set him to learn a particularly hard and uninteresting piece of poetry, beginning﻿—

===Chinese===
「不会讲的。除非他们问我，」他补了一句。「哦，保姆，也让我做点什么吧。我能帮上什么忙？」

「你可以把园子里剩下的花采来，给你母亲的房间扎一束鲜花，插在清水里摆好。再让家庭教师教你一首迎宾诗，等他们进来的时候念给他们听。」

采花、插花，是又痛快又容易的事。向自己那么讨厌的、绷着脸的家庭教师提出这么亲近的请求，却既不容易，也不痛快。可迪基还是开了口。而那位教师满心欢喜地给他布置了一首又难又乏味的诗去背，开头是——

===Original===
> “Happy is he

 Who, to sweet home retired,

 Shuns glory so admired

 And to himself lives free;

 While he who strives with pride to climb the skies

 Falls down with foul disgrace before he dies.”

===Chinese===
> 「有福的是他——

> 安然退守甜蜜的家，

> 避开人人艳羡的荣华，

> 独自过逍遥自在的日子；

> 那傲气十足、硬要攀上青天的人，

> 未及身死，先跌得声名狼藉。」

===Original===
Dickie could not help thinking that the father and mother who were to be his in this beautiful world might have preferred something simpler and more affectionate from their little boy than this difficult piece whose last verse was the only one which seemed to Dickie to mean anything in particular. In this verse Dickie was made to remark that he hoped people would say of him, “He died a good old man,” which he did *not* hope, and indeed had never so much as thought of. The poetry, he decided, would have been nicer if it had been more about his father and mother and less about fame and trees and burdens. He felt this so much that he tried to write a poem himself, and got as far as﻿—

===Chinese===
迪基忍不住想，在这个美丽世界里将要做他父母的这两位，从他们的小儿子嘴里听一点更简单、更亲热的话，总比这首难懂的诗强；这首诗里，在他看来多少还有点意思的只有最后一段。那一段里，迪基奉命表示，他盼望人们将来这样说起他：「他是个行善的好老人，寿终正寝。」——这话他其实并不盼望，而且压根儿连想也没想过。他认定，这诗要是多说些他的父亲母亲、少说些名声、树木和重担，本会更好些。这感觉太强烈了，以至他自己试笔写起诗来，写到——

===Original===
> “They say there is no other

 Can take the place of mother.

 I say there is no one I’d rather

 See than my father.”

===Chinese===
> 「人都说，世上再没有谁，

> 能替得了母亲。

> 我说，要说顶想见的，

> 头一个就是我父亲。」

===Original===
But he could not think of any more to say, and besides, he had a haunting idea that the first two lines﻿—which were quite the best﻿—were not his own makeup. So he abandoned the writing of poetry, deciding that it was not his line, and painfully learned the dismal verses appointed by his tutor.

But he never got them said. When the bustle of arrival had calmed a little, Dickie, his heart beating very fast indeed, found himself led by his tutor into the presence of the finest gentleman and the dearest lady he had ever beheld. The tutor gave him a little push so that he had to go forward two steps and to stand alone on the best carpet, which had been spread in their honor, and hissed in a savage whisper﻿—

“Recite your song of welcome.”

===Chinese===
可是往下他想不出再说什么了；再说，他心里总有个挥之不去的疑影，觉得头两行——恰恰是顶好的两行——不是他自个儿想出来的。于是他放弃了写诗，认定这不是他的行当，忍着苦把教师指定的那些沉闷诗句背了下去。

可他到底没能把诗背出来。迎接的忙乱稍稍平息之后，迪基发现自己已被教师引到他所见过的最俊美的绅士和最亲爱的夫人面前，心怦怦跳得飞快。教师在他背后轻轻一推，他只好朝前走了两步，独自站在那条专为迎接他们铺开的顶好地毯上，教师压着嗓子、狠声狠气地嘘道——

「念你的迎宾诗。」

===Original===
“ ‘Happy the man,’ ” began Dickie, in tones of gloom, and tremblingly pronounced the first lines of that unpleasing poem.

But he had not got to “strive with pride” before the dear lady caught him in her arms, exclaiming, “Bless my dear son! how he has grown!” and the fine gentleman thumped him on the back, and bade him “bear himself like a gentleman’s son, and not like a queasy square-toes.” And they both laughed, and he cried a little, and the tutor seemed to be blotted out, and there they were, all three as jolly as if they had known each other all their lives. And a stout young nurse brought the baby, and Dickie loved it and felt certain it loved him, though it only said, “Goo ga goo,” exactly as your baby-brother does now, and got hold of Dickie’s hair and pulled it and would not let go.

===Chinese===
「『有福的人，』」迪基阴沉沉地起了头，抖抖索索地念出那首讨人厌的诗的头几行。

可还没念到「硬要攀上青天」，亲爱的夫人就把他搂进怀里，喊着：「保佑我亲爱的儿子！他长这么高啦！」俊美的绅士在他背上砰砰捶了两拳，嘱咐他「要有绅士家儿子的样子，别像个娇气古板的老夫子」。两人都笑了，他掉了点眼泪，教师仿佛被抹掉了，剩下他们三个，亲亲热热，就像彼此认识了一辈子。一个壮实的年轻保姆抱来了小娃娃，迪基喜欢他，也认定他喜欢自己，虽然他只会「咕——嘎——咕」，跟如今你的小弟弟一模一样；他还一把抓住迪基的头发又扯又揪，怎么也不撒手。

===Original===
There was a glorious dinner, and Dickie waited on this new father of his, changed his plate, and poured wine out of a silver jug into the silver cup that my lord drank from. And after dinner the dear lady-mother must go all over the house to see everything, because she had been so long away, and Dickie walked in the garden among the ripe apples and grapes with his father’s hand on his shoulder, the happiest, proudest boy in all Deptford﻿—or in all Kent either.

His father asked what he had learned, and Dickie told, dwelling, perhaps, more on the riding, and the fencing, and the bowls, and the music than on the sour-faced tutor’s side of the business.

“But I’ve learned a lot of Greek and Latin, too,” he added in a hurry, “and poetry and things like that.”

===Chinese===
那顿饭吃得很气派。迪基伺候这位新父亲用饭，替他换餐盘，从银壶里斟酒，斟进老爷喝酒的那只银杯。饭后，亲爱的夫人母亲非要把整座宅子看个遍不可，因为她离家太久了；迪基同父亲在园子里苹果熟、葡萄熟的枝叶间散步，父亲的手搭在他的肩上——他是整个德普特福德、不，整个肯特郡，最快活、最得意的男孩。

父亲问他学了些什么，迪基一一说了，也许骑马、剑术、滚木球和音乐讲得多些，绷着脸的教师那一摊子讲得少些。

「不过希腊文、拉丁文我也学了不少，」他赶忙补上，「还有诗，还有那一类的东西。」

===Original===
“I fear,” said the father, “thou dost not love thy book.”

“I do, sir; yet I love my sports better,” said Dickie, and looked up to meet the fond, proud look of eyes as blue as his own.

“Thou’rt a good, modest lad,” said his father when they began their third round of the garden, “not once to ask for what I promised thee.”

===Chinese===
「我看，」父亲说，「你不怎么爱你的书啊。」

「爱的，先生；不过我更爱我的武艺。」迪基说，抬起头，迎住一双同他自己的眼睛一样蓝的眼睛，那里面盛满慈爱又骄傲的神气。

「你是个又好又谦逊的孩子，」父亲说，这时他们已在花园里绕到第三圈了，「我答应你的东西，你居然一回也没有讨。」

===Original===
Dickie could not stand this. “I might have asked,” he said presently, “but I have forgot what the promise was﻿—the fever﻿—”

“Ay, ay, poor lad! And of a high truth, too! Owned he had forgot! Come, jog that poor peaked remembrance.”

Dickie could hardly believe the beautiful hope that whispered in his ear.

===Chinese===
这一下迪基可受不住了。「我原本要问的，」他隔了一会儿说，「可我把答应的是什么给忘了——热病——」

「唉，唉，可怜的孩子！还着实诚实得很哪！自己就承认忘了！来，摇一摇你那可怜巴巴的小尖记性。」

那在耳边低语的美好指望，迪基简直不敢相信。

===Original===
“I almost think I remember,” he said. “Father﻿—did you promise﻿—?”

“I promised, if thou wast a good lad and biddable and constant at thy book and thy manly exercises, to give thee, so soon as thou should’st have learned to ride him﻿—”

“A little horse?” said Dickie breathlessly; “oh, father, not a little horse?” It was good to hear one’s father laugh that big, jolly laugh﻿—to feel one’s father’s arm laid like that across one’s shoulders.

===Chinese===
「我好像有点记得了，」他说。「父亲——你是不是答应过——？」

「我答应过，只要你做个好孩子，听话，用功念书，练好武艺，就等你一学会骑它——」

「小马吗？」迪基喘着气说；「哦，父亲，不会是一匹小马吧？」听自己的父亲发出那么响亮爽朗的大笑，觉出父亲的胳膊那样搭在自己肩上，真是好极了。

===Original===
The little horse turned round to look at them from his stall in the big stables. It was really rather a big horse.

What colored horse would you choose﻿—if a horse were to be yours for the choosing? Dickie would have chosen a gray, and a gray it was.

“What is his name?” Dickie asked, when he had admired the gray’s every point, had had him saddled, and had ridden him proudly round the pasture in his father’s sight.

===Chinese===
那匹「小马」在大马厩的栏里转过头来看他们。它其实是一匹相当大的马。

要是有一匹马随你挑毛色，你会挑什么颜色？迪基本会挑一匹灰马，而他得到的正是一匹灰马。

「它叫什么名字？」迪基问。这时他已经把灰马浑身上下夸了个遍，叫人给它备好了鞍，在父亲眼前神气十足地绕着草地骑了一圈。

===Original===
“We call him Rocinante,” said his father, “because he is so fat,” and he laughed, but Dickie did not understand the joke. He had not read *Don Quixote*, as you, no doubt, have.

“I should like,” said Dickie, sitting square on the gray, “to call him Crutch. May I?”

“*Crutch?*” the father repeated.

===Chinese===
「我们叫它罗西南特（Rocinante），」父亲说，「因为它长得太肥。」他笑了，迪基却不明白这个笑话。他还没读过《堂吉诃德》，不像你，想必是读过的。

「我想，」迪基端端正正坐在灰马上，「叫它『拐棍』。行吗？」

「拐棍？」父亲重复了一遍。

===Original===
“Because his paces are so easy,” Dickie explained. He got off the horse very quickly and came to his father. “I mean even a lame boy could ride him. Oh! father, I am so happy!” he said, and burrowed his nose in a velvet doublet, and perhaps snivelled a little. “I am so glad I am not lame.”

“Fancy-full as ever,” said his father; “come, come! Thou’rt weak yet from the fever. Be a man. Remember of what blood thou art. And thy mother﻿—she also hath a gift for thee﻿—from thy grandfather. Hast thou forgotten that? It hangs to the book learning. A reward﻿—and thou hast earned it.”

“I’ve forgotten that, too,” said Dickie. “You aren’t vexed because I forget? I can’t help it, father.”

===Chinese===
「因为它步子特别稳当，」迪基解释。他很快翻身下马，跑到父亲跟前。「我是说，连瘸腿的孩子也能骑它。哦！父亲，我太快活了！」他说着，把鼻子埋进那件天鹅绒紧身上衣里，兴许还抽了抽鼻子。「我真高兴，我不是瘸子。」

「又是满脑子的想入非非，」父亲说，「好了好了！你还病弱着呢。拿出男子汉的样子来。记住你是什么血统。你母亲——她也有件东西给你——你外祖父给的。这个你也忘了？那同读书是一路的。是给你的奖赏——你也挣来了。」

「那个我也忘了，」迪基说。「我忘事，你不恼吧？我实在没办法，父亲。」

===Original===
“That I’ll warrant thou cannot. Come, now, to thy mother. My little son! The Earl of Scilly chid me but this summer for sparing the rod and spoiling the child. But thy growth in all things bears out in what I answered him. I said: ‘The boys of our house, my lord, take that pride in it that they learn of their own free will what many an earl’s son must be driven to with rods.’ He took me. His own son is little better than an idiot, and naught but the rod to blame for it, I verily believe.”

They found the lady-mother and her babe by a little fire in a wide hearth.

“Our son comes to claim the guerdon of learning,” the father said. And the lady stood up with the babe in her arms.

===Chinese===
「这个我敢担保——你是真没办法。来，这会儿找你母亲去。我的小儿啊！锡利伯爵（the Earl of Scilly）今年夏天还责备我心软，舍不得动戒尺，惯坏了孩子。可我在样样事情上的长进，都证明我当时答他的话不假。我说：『我那位大人，咱们家这一门的男孩儿自有这份志气：人家多少伯爵家的少爷得用戒尺赶着才肯学的东西，他们自家情愿就学了去。』他认了这话。他自己的儿子跟傻子也差不离，依我看，除了戒尺也怪不得别的。」

他们找到夫人和她的小娃娃，就在宽大的壁炉边一小堆火旁。

「我们的儿子来讨他读书的奖赏了，」父亲说。夫人抱着娃娃站起身来。

===Original===
“Call the nurse to take him,” she said. But Dickie held out his arms.

“Oh, mother,” he said, and it was the first time in all his life that he had spoken that word to anyone. “Mother, do let me hold him.”

A warm, stiff bundle was put into his careful arms, and his little brother instantly caught at his hair. It hurt, but Dickie liked it.

===Chinese===
「叫保姆来抱他，」她说。迪基却伸出了胳膊。

「哦，母亲，」他说——这是他生平头一回对人说这个字眼。「母亲，让我抱抱他吧。」

一个暖乎乎、硬挺挺的小包被放进他小心翼翼的怀里，小弟弟立刻伸手去抓他的头发。揪得生疼，可迪基喜欢。

===Original===
The lady went to one of the carved cabinets and with a bright key from a very bright bunch unlocked one of the heavy panelled doors. She drew out of the darkness within a dull-colored leather bag embroidered in gold thread and crimson silk.

“He has forgot,” said Sir Richard in an undertone, “what it was that the grandfather promised him. Though he has well earned the same. ’Tis the fever.”

The mother put the bag in Dickie’s hands.

===Chinese===
夫人走到一只雕花柜前，从一大串亮闪闪的钥匙里拣出一把，开了柜上沉重的一扇嵌板门，从里头的暗处取出一只颜色发暗的皮袋，袋上用金线和深红丝线绣着花。

「他忘了，」理查德爵士（Sir Richard）低声说，「忘了外祖父答应给他的是什么。虽说这东西他是实实在在挣来的。是那场热病闹的。」

母亲把袋子放到迪基手里。

===Original===
“Count it out,” she said, taking her babe from him; and Dickie untied the leathern string, and poured out on to the polished long table what the bag held. Twenty gold pieces.

“And all with the image of our late dear Queen,” said the mother; “the image of that incomparable virgin Majesty whose example is a beacon for all time to all virtuous ladies.”

“Ah, yes, indeed,” said the father; “put them up in the bag, boy. They are thine own to thee, to spend as thou wilt.”

===Chinese===
「倒出来数数，」她说，把娃娃从他怀里抱了回去；迪基解开皮绳，把袋里的东西哗啦啦倒在擦得光亮的长桌上。二十枚金币。

「枚枚都铸着咱们已故的可爱女王的御容，」母亲说；「那位无与伦比的童贞女王的御容，她的典范是万世贤淑妇女的明灯。」

「啊，是啊，正是，」父亲说；「装回袋里去吧，孩子。这钱是你的，随你想怎么花就怎么花。」

===Original===
“Not unwisely,” said the mother gently.

“As he wills,” the father firmly said; “wisely or unwisely. As he wills. And none,” he added, “shall ask how they be spent.”

The lady frowned; she was a careful housewife, and twenty gold pieces were a large sum.

===Chinese===
「要花得不失明智，」母亲温言道。

「随他怎么花，」父亲斩钉截铁地说；「明智也好，不明智也好。随他。而且，」他加了一句，「谁也不许问这些钱是怎么花的。」

夫人皱了皱眉；她是个会当家的人，二十枚金币可不是小数目。

===Original===
“I will not waste it,” said Dickie. “Mother, you may trust me not to waste it.”

It was the happiest moment of his life to Dickie. The little horse﻿—the gold pieces﻿ ﻿… Yes, but much more, the sudden, good, safe feeling of father and mother and little brother; of a place where he belonged, where he loved and was loved. And by his equals. For he felt that, as far as a child can be the equal of grown people, he was the equal of these. And Beale was not his equal, either in the graces of the body or in the inner treasures of mind and heart. And hitherto he had loved only Beale; had only, so far as he could remember, been loved by Beale and by that shadowy father, his “Daddy,” who had died in hospital, and dying, had given him the rattle, his Tinkler, that was Harding’s Luck. And in the very heart of that happiest moment came, like a sharp dagger prick, the thought of Beale. What wonders could be done for Beale with those twenty-five gold sovereigns? For Dickie thought of them just as sovereigns﻿—and so they were.

===Chinese===
「我不会乱花的，」迪基说。「母亲，你可以放心，我不乱花。」

对迪基来说，这是他一生最快活的时刻。小马——金币……不错，可更要紧的，是父亲、母亲和小弟弟忽然带来的那种安安稳稳的好感觉；是有了一个属于自己的地方，在那里他爱人，也被人爱。而且是同与他平等的人在一起。因为他觉得，就一个孩子所能同大人平等的程度而言，他同这些人正是平等的。而比尔却算不上同他平等——论身子的灵巧不算，论心思和心灵里头的宝藏也不算。在此以前，他爱过的只有比尔；爱过他的，就他所记得的，也只有比尔，还有那位影影绰绰的父亲，他的「爹地」——死在医院里，临死把那只摇铃留给了他，那便是他的叮铃（Tinkler），那便是哈丁的好运气。就在这最快活的时刻的心尖上，比尔这个念头像匕首尖一样刺了进来。有这二十五枚索维林金币，能为比尔做成多少好事啊！因为迪基把它们只当作索维林金币来想——而它们也的确是索维林。

===Original===
And as these people who loved him, who were his own, drew nearer and nearer to his heart﻿—his heart, quickened by love of them, felt itself drawn more and more to Mr. Beale. Mr. Beale, the tramp, who had been kind to him when no one else was. Mr. Beale, the tramp and housebreaker.

So when the nurse took him, tired with new happinesses, to that beautiful tapestried room of his, he roused himself from his good soft sleepiness to say﻿—

“Nurse, you know a lot of things, don’t you?”

===Chinese===
而这些爱他的人、这些他自己的亲人，越是贴近他的心，他的心——被对他们的爱激醒了的这颗心——就越是牵向比尔先生。比尔先生，那个流浪汉，在谁都不肯待见他的时候对他好的流浪汉。比尔先生，那个流浪汉兼撬门贼。

于是，保姆带着被一桩桩新喜事累乏了的他回到那间挂着美丽壁毯的屋子时，他从香甜绵软的困意里振作起来，说——

「保姆，你懂得的事儿多，对不对？」

===Original===
“I know what I know,” she answered, undoing buttons with speed and authority.

“You know that other dream of mine﻿—that dream of mine, I mean, the dream of a dreadful place?”

“And then?”

“Could I take anything out of this dream﻿—I mean out of this time into the other one?”

===Chinese===
「我知道我知道的事，」她回答，麻利而专断地解着他的扣子。

「你知道我那另一个梦——我是说，我的那个梦，那个可怕地方的梦？」

「那又怎么样？」

「我能不能从这个梦里带点什么出去——我是说，从这个时代带到那个时代去？」

===Original===
“You could, but you must bring it back when you come again. And you could bring things thence. Certain things: your rattle, your moonseeds, your seal.”

He stared at her.

“You *do* know things,” he said; “but I want to take things there and leave them there.”

===Chinese===
「可以是可以，可你再来的时候，得把带去的带回来。从那边你也可以带东西过来。有几样东西：你的摇铃，你的月籽，你的图章。」

他直瞪瞪地望着她。

「你可真懂事儿，」他说；「可我想把东西带到那边去，留在那边。」

===Original===
She knitted thoughtful brows.

“There’s three hundred thick years between now and then,” she said. “Oh, yes, I know. And if you held it in your hand, you’d lose it like as not in some of the years you go through. Money’s mortal heavy and travels slow. Slower than the soul of you, my lamb. Someone would have time to see it and snatch it and hold to it.”

“Isn’t there any way?” Dickie asked, insisting to himself that he wasn’t sleepy.

===Chinese===
她蹙起眉头，思忖起来。

「从如今到那时，隔着厚厚的三百年，」她说。「哦，是啊，我知道。你要是把钱攥在手里带过去，多半会在你穿过的某几年里弄丢。钱死沉死沉，走得慢。比你的魂儿走得慢，我的小羊羔。有的是人有工夫瞧见它、一把抢去、攥住不放。」

「就没有一点法子吗？」迪基问，一边自己对自己咬定不困。

===Original===
“There’s the way of everything﻿—the earth,” she said; “bury it, and lie down on the spot where it’s buried, and then, when you get back into the other dream, the kind, thick earth will have hid your secret, and you can dig it up again. It will be there﻿ ﻿… unless other hands have dug there in the three hundred years. You must take your chance of that.”

“Will you help me?” Dickie asked. “I shall need to dig it very deep if I am to cheat three hundred years. And suppose,” he added, struck by a sudden and unpleasing thought, “there’s a house built on the place. I should be mixed up with the house. Two things can’t be in the same place at the same time. My tutor told me that. And the house would be so much stronger than me﻿—it would get the best of it, and where should I be then?”

===Chinese===
「有个法子，是万物通用的——就是大地，」她说；「把它埋了，人就躺在埋东西的地方睡觉，等你回到另一个梦里，好心的厚土地早就把你的秘密守住了，你到那边再把它挖出来。它会在那儿……除非这三百年里另有别的手在那儿挖过。这个风险，你只能自己担。」

「你帮我，行吗？」迪基问。「要骗过三百年，我得埋得很深很深才行。再说，」他忽然想到一桩不痛快的事，「要是那地方盖起了房子呢？我就会同房子搅在一块儿。两样东西不能同时占同一个地方。这是我的教师告诉我的。而房子比我要强得多——它准赢，那我上哪儿去呢？」

===Original===
“I’ll ask where thou’d be,” was the very surprising answer. “I’ll ask someone who knows. But it’ll take time﻿—put thy money in the great press, and I’ll keep the key. And next Friday as ever is, come your little cousins.”

They came. It was more difficult with them than it was with the grownups to conceal the fact that he had not always been the Dickie he was now; but it was not so difficult as you might suppose. It was no harder than not talking about the dreams you had last night.

===Chinese===
「你去哪儿了，我会替你问的，」回答来得非常出人意外。「我去问个明白人。不过这得费些时日——先把你的钱放进那口大衣柜，钥匙我来保管。还有，下个星期五，一准儿，你的小表亲们就到了。」

他们来了。同这两个孩子在一起，要瞒住「他并不是从来就是如今这个迪基」这件事，比同大人们在一起更难；可也没有你想的那么难。这同不去谈论你昨晚做的梦，差不多一样容易。

===Original===
And now he had indeed a full life: head-work, bodily exercises, work, home life, and joyous hours of play with two children who understood play as the poor little, dirty Deptford children do not and cannot understand it.

He lived and learned, and felt more and more that this was the life to which he really belonged. And days and weeks and months went by and nothing happened, and that is the happiest thing that can happen to anyone who is already happy.

Then one night the nurse said﻿—

“I have asked. You are to bury your treasure under the window of the solar parlor, and lie down and sleep on it. You’ll take no harm, and when you’re asleep I will say the right words, and you’ll wake under the same skies and not under a built house, like as you feared.”

===Chinese===
如今他的日子过得十足饱满：动脑子的功课、身体的操练、活计、家庭生活，还有同两个孩子一道玩耍的快活时光——那两个孩子懂得玩是什么，德普特福德那些又穷又脏的小家伙却不懂得，也无从懂得。

他生活着，学习着，越来越觉得这才是他真正归属的生活。日子、礼拜、月份一茬茬过去，什么也没有发生——对一个已经幸福的人来说，这是世上顶幸福的事了。

后来一天夜里，保姆说——

「我问过了。你把你的宝贝埋在阳光厅的窗下，就躺在上面睡。你不会受伤害；你睡着以后，我会念那几句该念的话，你就会在原来的天空下醒来，不会在盖起了房子的地方醒来，省得像你担心的那样。」

===Original===
She wrapped him in a warm cloth mantle of her own, when she took him from his bed that night after all the family were asleep, and put on his shoes and led him to the hole she had secretly dug in below the window. They had put his embroidered leather bag of gold in a little wrought-iron coffer that Sebastian had given him, and the nurse had tightly fastened the join of lid and box with wax and resin. The box was wrapped in a silk scarf, and the whole packet put into a big earthenware jar with a lid, and the join of lid and jar was smeared with resin and covered with clay. The nurse had shown him how to do all this.

“Against the earth spirits and the three hundred years,” she said.

===Chinese===
那天夜里，全家人都睡熟了，她把他从床上抱起来，用自己一件暖和的布斗篷把他裹好，替他穿上鞋，领他到她在窗下偷偷挖好的坑边。他们已经把他那只装金子的绣花皮袋装进塞巴斯蒂安送他的一只小锻铁箱，保姆用蜡和树脂把箱盖同箱子的接缝封得紧紧的。箱子裹上一条丝巾，整个一包放进一只有盖的大陶瓮，瓮盖同瓮口的接缝抹上树脂，再糊上泥。这些法子都是保姆教给他的。

「防地里的精怪，也防那三百年，」她说。

===Original===
Now she lifted the jar into the hole, and together they filled the hole with earth, treading it in with their feet.

“And when you would return,” said the nurse, “you know the way.”

“Do I?”

“You lay the rattle, the seal, and the moonseeds as before, and listen to the voices.”

===Chinese===
这时她把陶瓮放进坑里，两人一齐用土把坑填满，再用脚把土踩实。

「往后你要回来的时候，」保姆说，「路你是知道的。」

「我知道？」

「照老样子摆好摇铃、图章和月籽，听那些声音。」

===Original===
And then Dickie lay down in the cloth cloak, and the nurse sat by him and held his hand till he fell asleep. It was June now, and the scent of the roses was very sweet, and the nightingales kept him awake awhile. But the sky overhead was an old friend of his, and as he lay he could see the shining of the dew among the grass blades of the lawn. It was pleasant to lie again in the bed with the green curtains.

When he awoke there was his old friend the starry sky, and for a moment he wondered. Then he remembered. He raised himself on his elbow. There were houses all about﻿—little houses with lights in some of the windows. A broken paling was quite close to him. There was no grass near, only rough trampled earth; the smell all about him was not of roses, but of dustbins, and there were no nightingales﻿—but far away he could hear that restless roar that is the voice of London, and near at hand the foolish song and unsteady footfall of a man going home from the Cat and Whistle. He scratched a cross on the hard ground with a broken bit of a plate to mark the spot, got up and crept on hands and knees to the house, climbed in and found the room where Beale lay asleep.

===Chinese===
于是迪基裹着布斗篷躺下，保姆坐在旁边，握着他的手，直到他睡着。这时候是六月，玫瑰的香气甜得很，夜莺叫他有一阵子睡不着。可头顶的天空是他的一位老朋友；他躺着，能望见草坪叶子上露水的闪光。很快又能躺回那张挂着绿帐幔的床上了，多好。

他醒来，头顶上还是那位老朋友——满天星斗。一时间他有些发怔。随后他想起来了。他撑起一只胳膊肘。四下里全是房子——一些小房子，有几扇窗里亮着灯。一截破栅栏就在他跟前。近处没有草，只有踩得板结的粗土；四下里闻到的不是玫瑰，是垃圾桶的气味；夜莺也没有了——可是远远地，他听得见那永不停歇的轰鸣，那是伦敦的声音；近处，是一个从「猫与哨子」（Cat and Whistle）酒馆回家的人，胡乱哼着歌，深一脚浅一脚地走。他用一块碎瓷片在硬地上划了个十字，标明地方，爬起身，手脚并用地爬到房子跟前，翻了进去，找到比尔躺着睡觉的房间。

---

===Original===
“Father,” said Dickie, next morning, as Mr. Beale stretched and grunted and rubbed sleepy eyes with his unwashed fists in the cold daylight that filled the front room of 15, Lavender Terrace, Rosemary Lane. “You got to take this house﻿—that’s what you got to do; you remember.”

“Can’t say I do,” said Beale, scratching his head; “but if the nipper says so, it *is* so. Let’s go and get a mug and a doorstep, and then we’ll see.”

“You get it﻿—if you’re hungry,” said Dickie. “I’d rather wait here in case anybody else was to take the house. You go and see ’im now. ’E’ll think you’re a man in reg’lar work by your being up so early.”

===Chinese===
「爹，」第二天早晨迪基说。这时比尔先生正伸着懒腰，哼哼着，用没洗的拳头揉着惺忪的睡眼，清冷的晨光灌满了迷迭香巷薰衣草台街 15 号的前屋。「这房子你非租下来不可——就是这么回事；你记得的。」

「说不上记得，」比尔挠着头说；「不过小家伙既然这么说，那就是这么回事。先去弄杯茶、弄块面包片垫垫，然后瞧着办。」

「你去弄——要是你饿的话，」迪基说。「我情愿在这儿等，省得房子叫别人抢了去。你现在就去见他。你起得这么早，他会当你是个有正经营生的人。」

===Original===
“P’raps,” said Beale thoughtfully, running his hand over the rustling stubble of his two days’ beard﻿—“p’raps I’d best get a wash and brush-up first, eh? It might be worth it in the end. I’ll ’ave to go to the doss to get our pram and things, any’ow.”

The landlord of the desired house really thought Mr. Beale a quite respectable working man, and Mr. Beale accounted for their lack of furniture by saying, quite truthfully, that he and his nipper had come up from Gravesend, doing a bit of work on the way.

“I could,” he added, quite untruthfully, “give you the gentleman I worked with for me reference﻿—Talbott, ’is name is﻿—a bald man with a squint and red ears﻿—but p’raps this’ll do as well.” He pulled out of one pocket all their money﻿—two pounds eighteen shillings﻿—except six pennies which he had put in the other pocket to rattle. He rattled them now. “I’m anxious,” he said, confidentially, “to get settled on account of the nipper. I don’t deceive you; we ’oofed it up, not to waste our little bit, and he’s a hoppy chap.”

===Chinese===
「兴许，」比尔沉吟着说，手掌在两天没刮的、沙沙作响的胡茬上抹了一把——「兴许我顶好先洗把脸、拾掇拾掇，嗯？到头来总归值得。反正我还得回那家破客栈，把咱们的婴儿车和家什取来。」
