“You can’t be or you couldn’t have come after me right into the cave in the middle of the night. Come on. Stand close together and I’ll spread out the moonseeds.”

So Dickie said, and they stood, and he spread the moonseeds out, and he wished to be with the party of men who were hiding the treasure. But before he spread out the seeds he took certain other things in his left hand and held them closely. And instantly they were.

They were standing very close together, all three of them, in a niche in a narrow, dark passage, and men went by them carrying heavy chests, and great sacks of leather, and bundles tied up in straw and in handkerchiefs. The men had long hair and the kind of clothes you know were worn when Charles the First was King. And the children wore the dresses of that time and the boys had little swords at their sides. When the last bundle had been carried, the last chest set down with a dump on the stone floor of some room beyond, the children heard a door shut and a key turned, and then the men came back all together along the passage, and the children followed them. Presently torchlight gave way to daylight as they came out into the open air. But they had to come on hands and knees, for the path sloped steeply up and the opening was very low. The chests must have been pushed or pulled through. They could never have been carried.

The children turned and looked at the opening. It was in the courtyard wall, the courtyard that was now a smooth grass lawn and not the rough, daisied grass plot dotted with heaps of broken stone and masonry that they were used to see. And as they looked two men picked up a great stone and staggered forward with it and laid it on the stone floor of the secret passage just where it ended at the edge of the grass. Then another stone and another. The stones fitted into their places like bits of a Chinese puzzle. There was mortar or cement at their edges, and when the last stone was replaced no one could tell those stones from the other stones that formed the wall. Only the grass in front of them was trampled and broken.

“Fetch food and break it about,” said the man who seemed to be in command, “that it may look as though the men had eaten here. And trample the grass at other places. I give the Roundhead dogs another hour to break down our last defense. Children, go to your mother. This is no place for you.”

They knew the way. They had seen it in the picture. Edred and Elfrida turned to go. But Dickie whispered, “Don’t wait for me. I’ve something yet to do.”

===Chinese===
「画里头有许多兵，」埃德雷德说，「还在打呢。」

「我不怕兵，」埃尔弗里达抢着说，「你可是什么都不怕的，埃德雷德——你自己知道你不怕。」

「你不能怕，你不然怎么会在半夜里一路追我追进洞里来。来吧。站紧挨些，我来把月籽摊开。」

迪基这么说；他们就站好了，他把月籽摊开，心里许愿要同那些藏匿宝藏的人待在一处。可在摊开月籽之前，他左手先拿了另一样东西，紧紧攥着。刹那间，他们果真到了。

三个人紧紧挨着，立在一道又窄又暗的走廊的一个壁龛里；汉子们从他们身边经过，扛着沉重的木箱，扛着大皮的口袋，还有用稻草和手帕裹扎起来的包袱。那些汉子留着长发，穿的是查理一世在位时你熟知的那种衣裳。孩子们穿的也是那个时代的衣裳，两个男孩腰间还挂着小小的剑。末一只包袱扛过去了，末一只箱子咚的一声搁进里屋的石板地了，孩子们听见一扇门关上，一把钥匙拧过，随后那些汉子一齐顺着走廊折回来，孩子们跟在后面走。不多时，火把的光让给了天光，他们钻出洞口，到了露天。只是他们不得不手脚并用爬出来，因为小径陡陡地向上斜，洞口又矮。那些箱子想必当初是推着拽着弄进来的，扛是万万扛不进的。

孩子们回身打量那洞口。洞口开在庭院墙里——那庭院如今是一方平平整整的草坪，再不是他们看惯的那一片粗陋的、点着雏菊、东一堆西一堆碎砖残石蒿草般的空地了。他们正看着，两个汉子抬起一块大石头，摇摇晃晃抬上前，搁在密道石板的地上，正搁在密道尽头、草地边缘的地方。接着又是一块，再一块。石头嵌进各自的位置，像中国拼图的一块块碎板。石头边缘抹了灰浆或水泥；末一块石头归了位，谁也认不出这些石头同砌墙的别的石头有什么两样。只是他们面前的草地叫人践踏得乱七八糟。

「取些吃食来，掰碎了撒在这儿，」那个像是在发号施令的汉子说，「好叫人看着像士兵们在这儿吃过饭。再把别处的草地也踩踏一遍。我给圆颅党（Roundhead）的狗们再一个钟头工夫，去攻破我们这最后一道防线。孩子们，找你们母亲去。这儿不是你们待的地方。」

他们认得路。他们在画里见过。埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达转身要走。可迪基悄声说：「别等我。我还有一件事要办。」

===Original===
And when the soldiers had gone to get food and strew it about, as they had been told to do, Dickie crept up to the stones that had been removed, from which he had never taken his eyes, knelt down and scratched on one of the stones with one of the big nails he had brought in his hand. It blunted over and he took another, hiding in the chapel doorway when the men came back with the food.

“Every man to his post and God save us all!” cried the captain when the food was spread. They clattered off—they were in their armor now—and Dickie knelt down again and went on scratching with the nail.

The air was full of shouting, and the sound of guns, and the clash of armor, and a shattering sound like a giant mallet striking a giant drum—a sound that came and came again at five-minute intervals—and the shrieks of wounded men. Dickie pressed up the grass to cover the marks he had made on the stone, so low as to be almost underground and quite hidden by the grass roots.

Then he brushed the stone dust from his hands and stood up.

The treasure was found and its hiding-place marked. Now he would find Edred and Elfrida, and they would go back. Whether he was Lord of Arden or no, it was he and no other who had restored the fallen fortunes of that noble house.

===Chinese===
士兵们奉命去取吃食、四下撒布的时候，迪基便爬到那几块挪开过的石头跟前——他的眼睛始终没离开过那里——双膝跪下，用他带来握在手里的粗铁钉，在其中一块石头上刻画起来。钉尖磨秃了，他就换一根；汉子们捧着吃食回来时，他躲进小礼拜堂的门洞里。

吃食一摊开，队长便高喊：「人人各守岗位，愿上帝保佑我们大家！」他们哐哐啷啷地跑去了——这会儿都披挂了甲胄——迪基重新跪下，接着用铁钉刻画。

空气里满是呐喊，是枪炮声，是甲叶的铿锵，还有一种震天裂地的声响，像巨人抡着大槌擂一面巨鼓——那声音每隔五分钟就回来一遍——还有伤兵的惨叫。迪基把草往石头上按了又按，盖住他在石上刻下的痕记；那道痕低得几乎贴着地皮，让草根掩得严严实实。

然后他掸净手上的石粉，站起身来。

宝藏寻着了，藏处也标下了。如今他要去寻埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达，一同回去。他是不是阿登勋爵且不论，叫那户贵族人家中兴复业、家道重振的，是他，不是别人。

===Original===
He turned to go the way his cousins had gone. He could see the men-at-arms crowding in the archway of the great gate tower. From a window to his right a lady leaned, pale with terror, and with her were Edred and Elfrida—he could just see their white faces. He made for the door below that window. But it was too late. That dull, thudding sound came again, and this time it was followed by a great crash and a great shouting. The blue sky showed through the archway where the tall gates had been and under the arch was a mass of men shouting, screaming, struggling, and the gleam of steel and the scarlet of brave blood.

Dickie forgot all about the door below the window, forgot all about his cousins, forgot that he had found the treasure and that it was now his business to get himself and the others safely back to their own times. He only saw the house he loved broken into by men he hated; he saw the men he loved spending their blood like water to defend that house.

He drew the little sword that hung at his side and shouting “An Arden! an Arden!” he rushed towards the swaying, staggering melee. He reached it just as the leader of the attacking party had hewn his way through the Arden men and taken his first step on the flagged path of the courtyard. The first step was his last. He stopped, a big, burly fellow in a leathern coat and steel round cap, and looked, bewildered, at the little figure coming at him with all the fire and courage of the Ardens burning in his blue eyes. The big man laughed, and as he laughed Dickie lunged with his sword—the way his tutor had taught him—and the little sword—no tailor’s ornament to a Court dress, but a piece of true steel—went straight and true up into the heart of that big rebel. The man fell, wrenching the blade from Dickie’s hand.

A shout of fury went up from the enemy. A shout of pride and triumph from the Arden men. Men struggled and fought all about him. Next moment Dickie’s hands were tied with a handkerchief, and he stood there breathless and trembling with pride.

“I have killed a man,” he said; “I have killed a man for the King and for Arden.”

===Chinese===
他转身要走堂兄妹们走过的那条路。他望得见大队甲士正涌进大门楼那道拱洞。右边一扇窗里倚着一位夫人，吓得脸色煞白；跟她在一起的还有埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达——他刚好能望见那两张白脸。他朝那扇窗底下的门奔去。可是迟了。那沉闷的、砰砰的撞城声又响起来，这一回，接着而来的是一声巨响的崩裂和一阵鼎沸的呐喊。高大的城门原本所在的那道拱洞里透出了蓝天；拱洞底下挤满了人，喊的，叫的，扭打的，还有钢铁的寒光和壮士热血的猩红。

窗下的门、堂兄妹，迪基统统忘在了脑后；寻着宝藏、如今该把自己和同伴们平安送回自己的时代——这一层，他也忘了。他只看见他心爱的宅子叫他恨的人破门而入；只看见他爱的人正在像泼水一样抛洒热血，保卫那座宅子。

他拔出挂在腰间的小剑，喊着「阿登在此！阿登在此！」朝那摇摇晃晃、跌跌撞撞的混战冲了过去。他冲到跟前时，恰是攻城的头目一路砍杀、穿过阿登家的甲士，在庭院的石板路上迈出第一步的当口。这第一步，也是他的最后一步。他站住了——一条穿皮外衣、戴钢圆盔的大汉，怔怔地望着朝自己扑来的那个小小身影，那双蓝眼睛里燃着阿登家满腔的火与胆气。大汉笑了；就在他笑的时候，迪基挺剑刺出——用的是导师教的架势——那柄小剑，可不是裁缝钉在朝服上做样子的饰物，而是一段真正的精钢，又直又准，扎进了那个大个子叛党的心窝。那汉子倒下了，把剑身从迪基手里拧了去。

敌阵里腾起一声怒吼。阿登家的兵勇里腾起一声自豪与得胜的欢呼。四面八方的人扭作一团，厮杀不休。转瞬间，迪基的两手给一方手帕捆住了，他站在那里，屏着气，激动得直打颤。

「我杀了一个人，」他说；「为了国王，为了阿登，我杀了一个人。」

===Original===
They shut him up in the fuel shed and locked the door. Pride and anger filled him. He could think of nothing but that one good thrust for the good cause. But presently he remembered.

He had brought his cousins here—he must get them back safely. But how? On a quiet evening on the road Beale had taught him how to untie hands tied behind the back. He remembered the lesson now and set to work—but it was slow work. And all the time he was thinking, thinking. How could he get out? He knew the fuel shed well enough. The door was strong, there was a beech bar outside. But it was not roofed with tile or lead, as the rest of the Castle was. And Dickie knew something about thatch. Not for nothing had he watched the men thatching the oast-house by the Medway. When his hands were free he stood up and felt for the pins that fasten the thatch.

Suddenly his hands fell by his side. Even if he got out, how could he find his cousins? He would only be found by the rebels and be locked away more securely. He lay down on the floor, lay quite still there. It was despair. This was the end of all his cleverness. He had brought Edred and Elfrida into danger, and he could not get them back again. His anger had led him to defy the Roundheads, and to gratify his hate of them he had sacrificed those two who trusted him. He lay there a long time, and if he cried a little it was very dark in the fuel house, and there was no one to see him.

He was not crying, however, but thinking, thinking, thinking, and trying to find some way out, when he heard a little scratch, scratching on the corner of the shed. He sat up and listened. The scratching went on. He held his breath. Could it be that someone was trying to get in to help him? Nonsense, of course it was only a rat. Next moment a voice spoke so close to him that he started and all but cried out.

===Chinese===
他们把他关进柴棚，锁上了门。他心里满是骄傲，也满是愤怒。他满脑子只剩那一剑——为正义的缘由刺出的好剑。可不多时他想起来了。

是他把堂兄妹带到这儿来的——他必须把他们平安带回去。可是怎么带呢？路上一个清静的晚上，比尔教过他怎么解开反绑在背后的手。这会儿他记起了那一课，便动手解起来——只是解得很慢。他一边解，一边想呀想呀。他怎么出去呢？柴棚他是再熟不过的。门很结实，外头上着一根山毛榉的门闩。可这柴棚的顶子，不像城堡其他屋顶那样盖着瓦，也不包着铅，而是苫的草。苫草顶的门道，迪基是懂的。他在梅德韦河（the Medway）边看人给烘花屋（oast-house）苫顶，那不是白看的。两手一得空，他站起来，摸索着去寻那些钉草用的木钉。

忽然他的两手垂落下来。就算出去了，他又上哪儿去找堂兄妹呢？他只会叫叛党发现，关得更严实。他躺倒在地板上，一动不动地躺着。这是绝望。他一身机灵，到此统统算数。他把埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达带进了险境，却再也没法子把他们带回去。是他的怒气引着他去顶撞圆颅党人；为着痛快一时、泄一泄对他们的恨，他把信任他的那两个孩子搭了进去。他在那儿躺了很久很久；就算他掉了几滴泪也不要紧，柴棚里黑得很，谁也看不见他。

其实他并没有哭，他是在想，想，想，想找出一条活路，这时候，他听见棚子角落里响起窸窸窣窣轻轻的搔刮。他坐起来听。搔刮声继续着。他屏住了呼吸。会不会有人正设法进来救他？胡说，当然不过是一只耗子。可就在下一刻，一个声音贴着他响起来，惊得他浑身一哆嗦，险些叫出声来。

===Original===
“Bide where you be, lad, bide still; ’tis only me—old Mouldiwarp of Arden. You be a bold lad, by my faith, so you be. Never an Arden better. Never an Arden of them all.”

“Oh, Mouldiwarp, dear Mouldiwarp, do help me! I led them into this—help me to get them back safe. Do, do, do!”

“So I will, den—dere ain’t no reason in getting all of a fluster. It ain’t fitten for a lad as ’as faced death same’s what you ’ave,” said the voice. “I’ve made a liddle tunnel for ’e—so I ’ave—’ere in dis ’ere corner—you come caten wise crose the floor and you’ll feel it. You crawl down it, and outside you be sure enough.”

Dickie went towards the voice, and sure enough, as the voice said, there was a hole in the ground, just big enough, it seemed, for him to crawl down on hands and knees.

“I’ll go afore,” said the Mouldiwarp, “you come arter. Dere’s naught to be afeared on, Lord Arden.”

“Am I really Lord Arden?” said Dickie, pausing.

“Sure’s I’m alive you be,” the mole answered; “yer uncle’ll tell it you with all de lawyer’s reasons tomorrow morning as sure’s sure. Come along, den. Dere ain’t no time to lose.”

So Dickie went down on his hands and knees, and crept down the mole tunnel of soft, sweet-smelling earth, and then along, and then up—and there they were in the courtyard. There, too, were Edred and Elfrida.

The three children hugged each other, and then turned to the Mouldiwarp.

“How can we get home?”

“The old way,” he said; and from the sky above a swan carriage suddenly swooped. “In with you,” said the Mouldiwarp; “swan carriages can take you from one time to another just as well as one place to another. But we don’t often use ’em—’cause why? swans is dat contrary dey won’t go invisible not for no magic, dey won’t. So everybody can see ’em. Still we can’t pick nor choose when it’s danger like dis ’ere. In with you. Be off with you. This is the last you’ll see o’ me. Be off afore the soldiers sees you.”

===Chinese===
「待在原地，孩子，待着别动；是我呢——阿登家的老莫迪瓦普（Mouldiwarp）。你是个有胆气的好小子，我对天说，你就是。阿登家数你顶好。一家人里数一数二。」

「哦，莫迪瓦普，亲爱的莫迪瓦普，快救救我！是我领他们闯进来的——帮我把他们平平安安带回去吧。求你，求你，求你！」

「我会的，那还用说——急得乱了方寸可没个道理。像你这样直面过死神的愣小子，犯不上失体统，」那声音说。「我给你掘了一条小地道——掘好了的——就在这个角落里——你猫着腰、贴着地皮摸过来，就能摸着它。你顺着它爬出去，外头保管太平无事。」

迪基朝着声音摸过去；果然，一如那声音说的，地上有一个洞，大小恰够他手脚并用地钻下去。

「我在前头走，」莫迪瓦普说，「你跟着来。没什么可害怕的，阿登勋爵。」

「我果真是阿登勋爵吗？」迪基停住问。

「千真万确，我活着一天就作一天保，」那只鼹鼠答道；「明天一大早，你叔父自会把律师们的那些道理讲给你听，板钉钉一样确凿。走吧，那还用说。耽搁不得了。」

于是迪基手脚并用，钻进那甜软芳香的泥土里鼹鼠掘出的地道，先往下爬，再往前走，然后往上爬——转眼他们已在庭院里了。埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达也在那儿。

三个孩子抱作一团，随后一齐转向莫迪瓦普。

「我们怎么回家呢？」

「老法子，」他说；话音未落，天上忽然俯冲下一辆天鹅车。「进去吧，」莫迪瓦普说；「天鹅车送你们从一个时代到另一个时代，同从一个地方到另一个地方一样便当。只是我们不大常用它——为什么？天鹅性子忒别扭，任凭你什么魔法，它也不肯隐形，就是不肯。于是谁都能看得见它们。可是像这样的险境，由不得我们挑拣。进去吧。快去吧。这是我最后一回见你们了。趁士兵们还没瞧见，快走。」

===Original===
They squeezed into the swan carriage, all three. The white wings spread and the whole equipage rose into the air unseen by anyone but a Roundhead sentinel, who with great presence of mind gave the alarm, and was kicked for his pains, because when the guard turned out there was nothing to be seen.

The swans flew far too fast for the children to see where they were going, and when the swans began to flap more slowly so that the children could have seen if there had been anything to see, there was nothing to be seen, because it was quite dark. And the air was very cold. But presently a light showed ahead, and next moment there they were in the cave, and stepped out of the carriage on the exact spot where Dickie had set out the moonseeds and Tinkler and the white seal.

The swan carriage went back up the cave with a swish and rustle of wings, and the children went down the hill as quickly as they could—which was not very quickly because of Dickie’s poor lame foot. The boy who had killed a Cromwell’s man with his little sword had not been lame.

Arrived in the courtyard, Dickie proudly led the way and stooped to examine the stones near the ruined arch that had been the chapel door. Alas! there was not a sign of the inscription which Dickie had scratched on the stone when the Roundheads were battering at the gates of Arden Castle.

Then Edred said, “Aha!” in a tone of triumph.

“*I* took notice, too,” he explained. “It’s the fifth stone from the chapel door under the little window with the Arden arms carved over it. There’s no other window with that over it. I’ll get the cold chisel.”

He got it, and when he came back Dickie was on his knees by the wall, and he had dug with his hands and uncovered the stone where he had scratched with the nails. And there was the mark—19. R.D. 08. Only the nail had slipped once or twice while he was doing the 9, so that it looked much more like a five—15. R.D. 08.

“There,” he said, “that’s what I scratched!”

===Chinese===
三个人都挤进了天鹅车。白翼一张，整个车驾腾空而起，谁也没有瞧见——只除了一个圆颅党哨兵。那哨兵机灵得很，当即发出警报，却为此白吃了一脚：卫队闻声赶到，天上什么也没有。

天鹅飞得太快，孩子们根本看不见正飞向哪里；后来天鹅扑翅慢了下来，孩子们本该看得见了，却又什么也看不见，因为天已黑透。空气又冷得厉害。可是不多时，前方现出一点光，转眼间他们已在洞里，下了车，落脚的地方不多不少，正是迪基摊开月籽、叮铃（Tinkler）和白图章的那一小块地。

天鹅车扇着翅膀，飒飒作响，回身往洞穴深处去了；孩子们尽快往山下走——其实快不了，因为迪基那条可怜的瘸腿。方才用小剑杀了一个克伦威尔部下的那个男孩，是不瘸的。

回到庭院，迪基神气十足地领着头，弯下腰去查看小礼拜堂门那道残拱附近的石头。哎呀！圆颅党攻打阿登城堡大门的时候，他刻在石头上的那行字，连一点痕迹也没有了。

这时埃德雷德得意洋洋地「啊哈！」了一声。

「*我*也留了心，」他解释道。「在小礼拜堂门旁、刻着阿登家纹章的小窗底下，从门数过去第五块石头。别处再没有第二个窗，顶上刻着这个。我去取錾子。」

錾子取来了；他回来的时候，迪基正跪在墙边，用手刨开土，把当年他用铁钉刻画的那块石头露了出来。痕记果然在那里——19. R.D. 08。只可惜刻那个「9」的时候，铁钉滑了一两回，看上去倒更像一个「五」——15. R.D. 08。

「喏，」他说，「这就是我刻的！」

===Original===
“That?” said Edred. “Why, that’s always been there. We found that when we were digging about, trying to find the treasure. Quite at the beginning, didn’t we, Elf?”

And Elfrida agreed that this was so.

“Well, I scratched it, anyway,” said Dickie. “Now, then, let me go ahead with the chisel.”

Edred let him: he knew how clever Dickie was with his hands, for had he not made a work-box for Elfrida and a tool-chest for Edred, both with lids that fitted?

Dickie got the point of the chisel between the stones and pried and pressed—here and there, and at the other end—till the stone moved forward a little at a time, and they were able to get hold of it, and drag it out. Behind was darkness, a hollow—Dickie plunged his arm in.

“I can feel the door,” he said; “it’s all right.”

“Let’s fetch father,” suggested Elfrida; “he *will* enjoy it so.”

So he was fetched. Elfrida burst into the library where her father was busy with many lawyers’ letters and papers, and also with the lawyer himself, a stout, jolly-looking gentleman in a tweed suit, not a bit like the long, lean, disagreeable, black-coated lawyers you read about in books.

“Please, daddy,” she cried, “we’ve found the treasure. Come and look.”

“What treasure? and how often have I told you not to interrupt me when I am busy?”

“Oh, well,” said Elfrida, “I only thought it would amuse you, daddy. We’ve found a bricked-up place, and there’s a door behind, and I’m almost sure it’s where they hid the treasure when Cromwell’s wicked men took the Castle.”

===Chinese===
「那个？」埃德雷德说。「嘿，那早就有了。我们当初四处乱刨、寻宝藏的时候，就发现了它。开头头一天就发现了，是不是，艾尔夫（Elf）？」

埃尔弗里达说正是这样。

「反正是我刻的，横竖是了，」迪基说。「好，这回让我拿錾子来干。」

埃德雷德依了他：他知道迪基的手有多巧，不然，他给埃尔弗里达做的那只针线盒、给埃德雷德做的那口工具箱，盖子怎会都严丝合缝？

迪基把錾尖插进两块石头中间，又撬又压——这边，那边，再到另一头——石头一点一点地朝前挪，末了他们能抓住它，把它拖了出来。后头是黑黢黢的一个洞——迪基把胳膊探了进去。

「我摸着门了，」他说；「没问题。」

「去请爸爸来吧，」埃尔弗里达提议；「他*一定*乐坏了。」

于是把父亲请了来。埃尔弗里达一头闯进书房——她父亲正在那里忙着一大堆律师的信件文件，还有律师本人：一个胖墩墩、一团和气的先生，穿一身花呢套装，同你在书上读到的那种又高又瘦、叫人厌烦的黑袍律师全然两样。

「求求你，爸爸，」她喊道，「我们找着宝藏啦。快来瞧瞧。」

「什么宝藏？我同你说过多少回，我忙着的时候不许打岔？」

「哦，好啦，」埃尔弗里达说，「我只想着这能给你解闷儿嘛，爸爸。我们找着一处用砖封死的地方，后头有一扇门，我差不多敢肯定，就是克伦威尔的坏家伙们夺了城堡的时候，他们藏宝藏的地方。」

===Original===
“There is a legend to that effect,” said Elfrida’s father to the lawyer, who was looking interested. “You must forgive us if our family enthusiasms obliterate our manners. You have not said good morning to Mr. Roscoe, Elfrida.”

“Good morning, Mr. Roscoe,” said Elfrida cheerfully. “I thought it was the engineer’s day and not the lawyer’s. I beg your pardon, you wouldn’t mind me bursting in if you knew how very important the treasure is to the fortunes of our house.”

The lawyer laughed. “I am deeply interested in buried treasure. It would be a great treat to me if Lord Arden would allow me to assist in the search for it.”

“There’s no search *now*,” said Elfrida, “because it’s found. We’ve been searching for ages. Oh, daddy, do come—you’ll be sorry afterwards if you don’t.”

“If Mr. Roscoe doesn’t mind, then,” said her father indulgently. And the two followed Elfrida, believing that they were just going to be kind and to take part in some childish game of make-believe. Their feelings were very different when they peeped through the hole, where Dickie and Edred had removed two more stones, and saw the dusty gray of the wooden door beyond. Very soon all the stones were out, and the door was disclosed.

The lock plate bore the arms of Arden, and the door was not to be shaken.

“We must get a locksmith,” said Lord Arden.

“The big key with the arms on it!” cried Elfrida; “one of those in the iron box. Mightn’t that—?” One flew to fetch it.

A good deal of oil and more patience were needed before the key consented to turn in the lock, but it did turn—and the low passage was disclosed. It hardly seemed a passage at all, so thick and low hung the curtain of dusty cobwebs. But with brooms and lanterns and much sneezing and choking, the whole party got through to the door of the treasure room. And the other key unlocked that. And there in real fact was the treasure just as the children had seen it—the chests and the boxes and the leathern sacks and the bundles done up in straw and in handkerchiefs.

===Chinese===
「民间确实有这么一类的传说，」埃尔弗里达的父亲对那位律师说——律师正听得兴致勃勃。「我们要是家兴一起、礼数全忘了，还请你包涵。埃尔弗里达，你还没向罗斯科先生（Mr. Roscoe）道早安呢。」

「罗斯科先生，早安，」埃尔弗里达欢欢喜喜地说。「我还当今天是工程师的日子，不是律师的日子呢。对不住；您要是知道这笔宝藏对我们全家的家运有多要紧，我这样闯进来，您绝不会见怪的。」

律师笑了。「埋藏的宝藏，我是深感兴趣的。阿登勋爵若是肯让我帮着寻访，那对我真是天大的乐事。」

「如今*不用*寻访了，」埃尔弗里达说，「因为已经寻着了。我们寻了多少年月了。哦，爸爸，快去吧——您要不走，回头要后悔的。」

「只要罗斯科先生不介意，」她父亲溺爱地说。于是两人跟着埃尔弗里达去了，满心以为不过是发发善心，陪孩子们做一场小孩子装模作样的游戏。等他们凑近那个洞口往里一瞧——迪基和埃德雷德又挪开了两块石头——望见里头木质门那蒙尘的灰暗，两人的心情可就大不一样了。很快，石头全部搬空，那扇门露了形。

锁牌上铸着阿登家的纹章，那门是摇撼不动的。

「得请个锁匠来，」阿登勋爵说。

「有家徽的那把大钥匙！」埃尔弗里达喊道；「铁盒里收着的那几把当中的一把。岂不正好——？」有人飞奔去取了。

上了好些油，又添了加倍多的耐心，那钥匙才肯在锁里转动；到底转动了——一条低矮的夹道显现出来。说是夹道，简直不像夹道，蒙尘的蛛网结成一道又厚又低的帘幕。可是一行人拿了笤帚、提了灯笼，喷嚏连连、呛咳不止，总算穿过夹道，到了藏宝室门前。另一把钥匙又开了它。果不其然，宝藏就在那里，同孩子们见过的分毫不差——木箱、匣子、皮口袋，还有用稻草和手帕裹扎的包袱。

===Original===
The lawyer, who had come on a bicycle, went off on it, at racing speed, to tell the Bank at Cliffville to come and fetch the treasure, and to bring police to watch over it till it should be safe in the Bank vaults.

“And I’m child enough,” he said before he went, “as well as cautious enough, to beg you not to bring any of it out till I come back, and not to leave guarding the entrance till the police are here.”

So when the treasure at last saw the light of day it saw it under the eyes of policemen and Bank managers and all the servants and all the family and the Beales and True, and half the village beside, who had got wind of the strange happenings at the Castle and had crowded in through the now undefended gate.

It was a glorious treasure—gold and silver plate, jewels and beautiful armor, along with a pile of old parchments which Mr. Roscoe said were worth more than all the rest put together, for they were the title-deeds of great estates.

“And now,” cried Beale, “let’s ’ave a cheer for Lord Arden. Long may ’e enjoy ’is find, says I! ’Ip, ’ip, ’ooray!”

The cheers went up, given with a good heart.

“I thank you all,” said the father of Edred and Elfrida. “I thank you all from my heart. And you may be sure that you shall share in this good fortune. The old lands are in the market. They will be bought back. And every house on Arden land shall be made sound and weather-tight and comfortable. The Castle will be restored—almost certainly. And the fortunes of Arden’s tenantry will be the fortunes of Arden Castle.”

===Chinese===
那位律师是骑脚踏车来的，这会儿蹬上车，赛跑似的飞驰而去，去叫克利夫维尔（Cliffville）的银行来把宝藏运走，并带上警察来看守，直到宝藏安然进了银行的地下金库。

「我这个人呢，」他临走前说，「胆子比孩子大，心思倒也够缜密，我要求你们：我回来之前，一件也别往外搬；警察没到，谁也别离开进口去守着。」

于是，宝藏重见天日的那一刻，围着看的，有警察，有银行的经理们，有全体仆人，有全家上下，有比尔一家（the Beales）和特鲁（True），还有半个村子的人——他们听说城堡里出了稀奇事，早已从那扇如今无人把守的大门蜂拥而入。

好一笔堂皇的宝藏——金银器皿，珠宝，精美的甲胄，还有一大摞旧羊皮文书；据罗斯科先生说，那些文书比其余的一切加起来还值钱，因为它们是大片田产的地契。

「如今，」比尔喊道，「咱们给阿登勋爵喝一声彩吧。我说的：愿他享用他寻着的宝贝，长长久久！希普，希普，乌拉！」

欢呼声腾起，出自真心实意。

「我谢谢诸位，」埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达的父亲说。「我打心底里谢谢诸位。你们只管放心，这份好运自有你们一份。祖上的田产正在市面上出卖。我们要一一买回来。阿登地界上的每一所房子，都要修得结实、御得住风雨、住得舒坦。城堡要修复——差不多是肯定的了。阿登佃户们的家运，就是阿登城堡的家运。」
===Original===
Another cheer went up. But the speaker raised his hand, and silence waited his next words.

“I have something else to tell you,” he said, “and as well now as later. This gentleman, Mr. Roscoe, my solicitor, has this morning brought me news that I am not Lord Arden!”

Loud murmurs of dissatisfaction from the crowd.

“I have no claim to the title,” he went on grimly; “my father was a younger son—the real heir was kidnapped, and supposed to be dead, so I inherited. It is the grandson of that kidnapped heir who is Lord Arden. I know his whole history. I know what he has done, to do honor to himself and to help others.” (“Hear, hear” from Beale.) “I know all his life, and I am proud that he is the head of our house. He will do for you, when he is of age, all that I would have done. And in the meantime I am his guardian. This is Lord Arden,” he said, throwing his arm round the shoulders of Dickie, little lame Dickie, who stood there leaning on his crutch, pale as death. “This is Lord Arden, come to his own. Cheer for him, men, as you never cheered before. Three cheers for Richard Lord Arden!”

===Chinese===
又一阵欢呼腾起。可是演讲的人举起了手，一屋子人静下来，等他接下去的话。

「我还有一件事要告诉你们，」他说，「现在说，同往后说是一样。这位先生——罗斯科先生，我的事务律师——今天早晨给我带来了消息：我不是阿登勋爵！」

人群里响起一片不平的嘟哝。

「我无权承袭这个名号，」他板着脸接下去；「我的父亲是个幼子——真正的继承人当年叫人拐走，都以为死了，家业便传到了我手里。如今真正的阿登勋爵，是那位被拐走的继承人的孙子。他的整个身世我都知道。他做了些什么——为他自己增光，也扶助旁人——我都知道。」「说得对，说得对。」比尔应声。「他这一辈子的事我全知道，他做了我们这一家门的首领，我引以为荣。等他成年，我原本要为你们做的每一件事，他都会替你们做到。而在那之前，我是他的监护人。这一位，就是阿登勋爵，」他说着，一胳膊搂住迪基的肩——小小的、瘸腿的迪基，拄着拐杖站在那儿，脸白得像死人。「这一位，就是阿登勋爵，如今归返本家。乡亲们，给他喝彩吧，像从来没有喝过彩那样喝彩。为理查德·阿登勋爵，三呼万岁！」

## XII The End / 第十二章 终局

===Original===
What a triumph for little lame Dickie of Deptford!
---
You think, perhaps, that he was happy as well as proud, for proud he certainly was, with those words and those cheers ringing in his ears. He had just done the best he could, and tried to help Beale and the dogs, and the man who had thought himself to be Lord Arden had said, “I am proud that he should be the head of our house,” and all the Arden folk had cheered. It was worth having lived for.
The unselfish kindness and affection of the man he had displaced, the love of his little cousins, the devotion of Beale, the fact that he was Lord of Arden, and would soon be lord of all the old acres﻿—the knowledge that now he would learn all he chose to learn and hold in his hand some day the destinies of these village folk, all loyal to the name of Arden, the thought of all that he could be and do﻿—all these things, you think, should have made him happy.

===Chinese===
德普特福德（Deptford）的瘸腿小迪基（Dickie），这是何等的荣耀啊！
---
你也许会想，他不但骄傲，而且快活；骄傲是千真万确的——那些话、那些欢呼还在他耳边回响。他刚刚竭尽所能，去帮比尔（Beale）和那群狗，而那位原本自以为是阿登勋爵（Lord Arden）的先生说了：「由他来做我们家族的当家人，我骄傲。」于是全体阿登的人都为他欢呼。一生为了这个而活，也就值了。
他挤掉了的那个人待他无私而恩厚；他的小堂弟堂妹们疼他；比尔对他忠心耿耿；他身为阿登勋爵，不久便要成为全部古老田产的主人；他知道，从此自己想学什么就能学什么，有朝一日还能把这些村人的命运握在自己手里，而他们都忠心于阿登这个名号；再加上对自己将来能成为什么、能成就什么的全部憧憬——你想，这一切本该让他快活的。

===Original===
They would have made him happy, but for one thing. All this was won at the expense of those whom he loved best﻿—the children who were his dear cousins and playfellows, the man, their father, who had moved heaven and earth to establish Dickie’s claim to the title, and had been content quietly to stand aside and give up title, castle, lands, and treasure to the little cripple from Deptford.
Dickie thought of that, and almost only of that, in the days that followed.
The life he had led in that dreamworld, when James the First was King, seemed to him now a very little thing compared with the present glory, of being the head of the house of Arden, of being the Providence, the loving overlord of all these good peasant folk, who loved his name.
Yet the thought of those days when he was plain Richard Arden, son of Sir Richard Arden, living in the beautiful house at Deptford, fretted at all his joy in his present state. That, and the thought of all he owed to him who had been Lord of Arden until he came, with his lame foot and his heirship, fretted his soul as rust frets steel. These people had received him, loved him, been kind to him when he was only a tramp boy. And he was repaying them by taking away from them priceless possessions. For so he esteemed the lordship of Arden and the old lands and the old Castle.

===Chinese===
这一切本该让他快活的，只是有一样东西从中作梗。这一切，全是以他最爱的人为代价换来的——那几个孩子，是他亲爱的堂弟堂妹，也是他的玩伴；还有那个人，他们的父亲——为了证实迪基对这爵位的权利，他曾经上天入地，随后又心甘情愿地悄悄退到一旁，把爵位、城堡、田产和宝藏，统统让给了德普特福德来的小瘸子。
在此后的日子里，迪基想着这件事，而且几乎满心只想着这件事。
如今，比起眼前的荣光——做阿登家族的当家人，做这些善良乡民爱戴的守护天意、满怀仁爱的庇主——他当年在那个梦境之国里、詹姆斯一世在位时过的生活，就显得微末极了。
可是一想到往昔——想到自己那时只是一个平平常常的理查德·阿登（Richard Arden），是理查德·阿登爵士的儿子，住在德普特福德那座美丽的宅子里——他眼下境况中的全部欢乐便被搅得不安。这心事，加上一想到自己欠下那个人的一切——在他拖着瘸腿、带着继承权到来之前，那人是阿登的领主——他的灵魂便受着侵蚀，像钢让锈蚀了。当年他不过是个流浪儿，这些人接待了他，疼爱他，待他好。而他的报答，却是把无价的家当从他们手里拿走。因为在他看来，阿登的领主权位、古老的田地、古老的城堡，正是这样的无价之宝。

===Original===
Suppose he gave them up﻿—the priceless possessions? Suppose he went away to that sure retreat that was still left him﻿—the past? It was a sacrifice. To give up the here and now, for the far off, the almost forgotten. All that happy other life, that had once held all for which he cared, seemed thin and dreamlike beside the vivid glories of the life here, now. Yet he remembered how once that life, in King James’s time, had seemed the best thing in the world, and how he had chosen to come back from it, to help a helpless middle-aged ne’er-do-weel of a tramp﻿—Beale. Well, he had helped Beale. He had done what he set out to do. For Beale’s sake he had given up the beautiful life for the sordid life. And Beale was a new man, a man that Dickie had made. Surely now he could give up one beautiful life for another﻿—for the sake of these, his flesh and blood, who had so readily, so kindly, so generously set him in the place that had been theirs?
More and more it came home to Dickie that this was what he had to do. To go back to the times when James the First was King, and never to return to these times at all. It would be very bitter﻿—it would be like leaving home never to return. It was exile. Well, was Richard Lord Arden to be afraid of exile﻿—or of anything else? He must not just disappear either, or they would search and search for him, and never know that he was gone forever. He must slip away, and let the father of Edred and Elfrida be, as he had been, Lord Arden. He must make it appear that he, Richard Lord Arden, was dead. He thought over this very carefully. But if he seemed to be dead, Edred and Elfrida would be very unhappy. Well, they should not be unhappy. He would tell them. And then they would know that he had behaved well, and as an Arden should. Don’t be hard on him for longing for just this “little human praise.” There are very few of us who can do without it; who can bear not to let someone, very near and dear, know that we have behaved rather decently on those occasions when that is what we have done.
It took Dickie a long time to think out all this, clearly, and with no mistakes. But at last his mind was made up.

===Chinese===
假如他把这些无价的家当放弃了，会怎样？假如他逃回到那处依然留给他的安稳退身之地——过去——去，又会怎样？那是一种牺牲。舍下此时此地的一切，去换那遥远的、几乎已被忘却的一切。那一段快乐的另一种生活，曾经盛着他所珍视的一切，如今在这边、在眼下这鲜明灿烂的生活映照之下，却显得单薄缥缈，像一场梦。可他记得，当年那段詹姆斯国王时代的生活，曾像是天底下最好的东西，而他是为了帮一个孤苦无依、人到中年还一事无成的流浪汉——比尔——才甘愿从那样的生活里回来的。好了，他帮了比尔。他要做的事做成了。为了比尔，他把美好的生活换成了污浊困顿的生活。而比尔如今是个新人，是迪基造就的人。那么如今，为了这些亲骨肉——他们那么爽快、那么和善、那么慷慨地把原本属于自己的位置让给了他——他自然也可以舍下一种美好生活，去换另一种美好生活，不是吗？
迪基越来越真切地明白到，这就是他非做不可的事。回到詹姆斯一世在位的年代去，从此再也不回到这个年代来。那会十分苦楚——就像离家而去，永不回来。那是流放。可是，理查德·阿登勋爵难道会怕流放——会怕任何别的什么吗？他也不能只是凭空消失，否则他们会找呀找呀，永远不知道他已一去不返。他必须悄悄走开，让埃德雷德（Edred）和埃尔弗里达（Elfrida）的父亲像从前那样做阿登勋爵。他必须造成一种假象，让人以为他理查德·阿登勋爵已经死了。这件事他想了又想，极其仔细。可是，他若是显得死了，埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达会非常难过。那么，就不该让他们难过。他要告诉他们。这样他们就会知道，他行事端正，不愧是阿登家的人。不要因为他渴求这一点点「小小的人间褒奖」就苛责他。我们当中很少有人离了它还能活；很少有人能忍受不让某个至亲至爱的人知道：在那些我们确实做得体面的场合，我们做得体面。
迪基花了很长时间，才把这一切想得清清楚楚、不出差错。但他终于拿定了主意。

===Original===
And then he asked Edred and Elfrida to come up to the cave with him, because he had something to tell them. When they were all there, sitting on the smooth sand by the underground stream, Dickie said﻿—
“Look here. I’m not going on being Lord Arden.”
“You can’t help it,” said Edred.

===Chinese===
于是他请埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达陪他一起到洞穴里去，说他有话要告诉他们。等大家都到了，坐在地下暗河旁平滑的沙地上，迪基说道——
「听我说。我不打算继续做阿登勋爵了。」
「这可由不得你，」埃德雷德说。

===Original===
“Yes, I can. You know how I went and lived in King James’s time. Well, I’m going there again﻿—for good.”
“You shan’t,” said Elfrida. “I’ll tell father.”
“I’ve thought of all that,” Dickie said, “and I’m going to ask the Mouldiwarps to make it so that you *can’t* tell. I can’t stay here and feel that I’m turning you and your father out. And think what Edred did for me, in this very cave. No, my mind’s made up.”

===Chinese===
「我由得了自己。你知道我是怎么到詹姆斯国王的时代去住过一段的。好，我要再去那边——永远住下去。」
「不许你去，」埃尔弗里达说。「我要告诉爸爸。」
「这些我都想过了，」迪基说，「我要去求诸位莫德瓦普（Mouldiwarps），把事情安排成你们*没法*告诉任何人。我不能留在这儿，心里总掂着是我把你们和你爸爸往外赶。再想想埃德雷德为我做过的事，就在这个洞穴里。不行，我的主意打定了。」

===Original===
It was, and they could not shake it.
“But we shan’t ever see you again.”
Dickie admitted that this was so.
“And oh, Dickie,” said Elfrida, with deep concern, “you won’t ever see us again either. Think of that. Whatever will you do without us?”

===Chinese===
主意是打定了，他们怎么也动摇不了。
「可我们再也见不着你了。」
迪基承认确实如此。
「还有啊，迪基，」埃尔弗里达深深担忧地说，「你也再见不着我们了呀。想想这个。没有我们，你到底怎么办呢？」

===Original===
“That,” said Dickie, “won’t be so bad as you think. The Elfrida and Edred who live in those times are as like you as two pins. No, they aren’t really! Oh, don’t make it any harder. I’ve got to do it.”
There was that in his voice which silenced and convinced them. They felt that he had, indeed, to do it.
“I could never be happy here﻿—never,” he went on; “but I shall be happy there. And you’ll never forget me, though there are one or two things I want you to forget. And I’m going now.”

===Chinese===
「那个嘛，」迪基说，「不像你想的那么糟。住在那个时代的埃尔弗里达和埃德雷德，跟你们俩像得就像两枚一模一样的别针。不，其实并不真是！哦，别把这件事弄得更难了。我非做不可。」
他话音里有一种东西，叫他们没了言语，也叫他们信了。他们感觉到，他的确非做不可。
「在这儿我永远不会快活——永远不会，」他接着说；「可在那边我会快活。你们永远不会忘了我，虽然有一两件事，我要你们忘掉。我现在就走了。」

===Original===
“Oh, not now; wait and think,” Elfrida implored.
“I’ve thought of nothing else for a month,” said Dickie, and began to lay out the moonseeds on the smooth sand.
“Now,” he said, when the pattern was complete, “I shall hold Tinkler and the white seal in my hand and take them with me. When I’ve gone, you can put the moonseeds in your pocket and go home. When they ask you where I am, say I am in the cave. They will come and find my clothes, and they’ll think I was bathing and got drowned.”

===Chinese===
「哦，别是现在；等一等，再想一想，」埃尔弗里达哀求道。
「这一个月我想的没有别的，」迪基说着，开始在平滑的沙面上摆开月籽（moonseeds）。
「好了，」图案摆齐的时候，他说，「我要把叮铃（Tinkler）和白印章（white seal）握在手心里，带着它们一起走。我走了之后，你们把月籽装进口袋，回家去。他们要是问起我在哪儿，就说我在洞穴里。他们会来找，找到我的衣裳，就会当我是下河洗澡淹死了。」

===Original===
“I can’t bear it,” said Elfrida, bursting into sobs. “I can’t, and I won’t.”
“I shan’t be really dead, silly,” Richard told her. “We’re bound to meet again some day. People who love each other can’t help meeting again. Old nurse told me so, and she knows everything. Goodbye, Elfrida.” He kissed her. “Goodbye, Edred, old chap. I’d like to kiss you too, if you don’t mind. I know boys don’t, but in the times I’m going to men kiss each other. Raleigh and Drake did, you know.”
The boys kissed shyly and awkwardly.

===Chinese===
「我受不了，」埃尔弗里达说，呜咽一下子涌了上来。「我受不了，我也不依。」
「我又不是真死了，傻瓜，」理查德对她说。「我们总有一天一定要重逢的。彼此相爱的人，想不重逢都不成。老保姆就是这么跟我说的，她什么都知道。再见了，埃尔弗里达。」他吻了她。「再见，埃德雷德，老伙计。你若不介意，我也想亲亲你。我知道男孩儿们不兴这样，可在我要去的那种年代，男人跟男人是亲吻的。雷利和德雷克（Raleigh and Drake）就亲过，你知道的。」
两个男孩儿又羞又怯、笨手笨脚地吻了他。

===Original===
“And now, goodbye,” said Richard, and stepped inside the crossed triangles of moonseeds.
“I wish,” he said slowly, “oh, dear Mouldiwarps of Arden, grant me these last wishes. I wish Edred and Elfrida may never be able to tell what I have done. And I wish that in a year they may forget what I have done, and let them not be unhappy about me, because I shall be very happy. I know I shall,” he added doubtfully, and paused.
“Oh, Dickie, *don’t*,” the other children cried out together. He went on﻿—

===Chinese===
「那么，再见了，」理查德说，一脚踏进了交叠的月籽三角当中。
「我愿望，」他缓缓地说，「哦，阿登的亲爱的诸位莫德瓦普，准许我这些最后的愿望吧。我愿望埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达永远说不出我做过的事。我愿望一年之后他们忘掉我做过的事，也别为我难过，因为我会很快乐的。我知道我会的，」他有些迟疑地补上一句，顿住了。
「哦，迪基，*别许了*，」另外两个孩子一齐喊出声来。他接着往下说——

===Original===
“I wish my uncle may restore the Castle, and take care of the poor people so that there *aren’t* any poor people, and everyone’s comfortable, just as I meant to do.”
He took off his cap and coat and flung them outside the circle, his boots too.
“I wish I may go back to James the First’s time, and live out my life there, and do honor in my life and death to the house of Arden.”

===Chinese===
「我愿望我的叔叔修复城堡，照拂穷人，好叫穷人*再*没有了，人人都过得舒舒坦坦，就像我本打算做的那样。」
他摘下帽子，脱下外衣，抛到圈子外面，靴子也一并抛了出去。
「我愿望我回到詹姆斯一世的年代去，在那里度过我的一生，生前死后，都为阿登家族增光。」

===Original===
The children blinked. Dickie and Tinkler and the white seal were gone, and only the empty ring of moonseeds lay on the sand.
---
“Shocking bathing fatality,” the newspapers said. “Lord Arden drowned. The body not yet recovered.”
It never was recovered, of course. Elfrida and Edred said nothing. No wonder, their elders said. The shock was too great and too sudden.

===Chinese===
两个孩子眨了眨眼。迪基、叮铃和白印章都不见了，沙地上只剩一圈空空的月籽。
---
「一桩骇人的游泳惨案，」报纸上这样说。「阿登勋爵溺毙。尸体尚未寻获。」
尸体当然永远没有寻获。埃尔弗里达和埃德雷德一声不响。这不奇怪，大人们说。打击太大，又来得太突然。

===Original===
The father of Edred and Elfrida is Lord Arden now. He has done all that Dickie would have done. He has made Arden the happiest and most prosperous village in England, and the stream beside which Dickie bade farewell to his cousins flows, a broad moat round the waters of the Castle, restored now to all its own splendor.
There is a tablet in the church which tells of the death by drowning of Richard, Sixteenth Lord Arden. The children read it every Sunday for a year, and knew that it did not tell the truth. But by the time the moonseeds had grown and flowered and shed their seeds in the Castle garden they ceased to know this, and talked often, sadly and fondly, of dear cousin Dickie who was drowned. And at the same time they ceased to remember that they had ever been out of their own time into the past, so that if they were to read this book they would think it all nonsense and makeup, and not in the least recognize the story as their own.
But whatever else is forgotten, Dickie is remembered. And he who gave up his life here for the sake of those he loved will live as long as life shall beat in the hearts of those who loved him.

===Chinese===
如今，埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达的父亲做了阿登勋爵。迪基本来会做的，他都做了。他把阿登治理成了英格兰最快乐、最兴旺的村庄；那条迪基曾与堂弟堂妹们在水边道别的溪流，如今流成了一道宽阔的护城河，环绕着城堡的水面；城堡已恢复了自己全部的昔日光彩。
教堂里有一块石牌，记载着理查德——第十六代阿登勋爵——溺水身故的始末。整整一年，孩子们每个礼拜天都去读它，心里明白它说的不是实话。可是等月籽在城堡园子里长起来、开了花、又落了籽，他们就不再明白这一点了，时常忧伤而眷恋地谈起那位亲爱的、淹死了的堂哥迪基。同时他们也记不起自己曾经离开自己的年代去过往昔，因此哪怕他们哪天读到这本书，也只会觉得满纸无稽，全是胡编乱造，丝毫认不出那故事写的就是他们自己。
可是无论别的什么会被忘掉，迪基总有人记得。这个为了所爱的人舍下此地生命的人，将活下去，活到爱他的人们心中还有生命在搏动的每一个日子。

===Original===
---
And Dickie himself. I see him in his ruff and cloak, with his little sword by his side, living out the life he has chosen in the old England when James the First was King. I see him growing in grace and favor, versed in book learning, expert in all noble sports and exercises. For Dickie is not lame now.
I see the roots of his being taking fast hold of his chosen life, and the life that he renounced receding, receding till he can hardly see it any more.
I see him, a tall youth, straight and strong, lending the old nurse his arm to walk in the trim, beautiful garden at Deptford. And I hear him say﻿—

===Chinese===
---
至于迪基本人。我看见他戴着轮状皱领，披着斗篷，胁下佩着一柄小小的剑，在詹姆斯一世为王的老英格兰，把他选定的那种生活一直过下去。我看见他在恩宠与眷顾中日渐长成，通晓书卷学问，一切高贵的技艺和运动无不精熟。因为迪基如今已经不瘸了。
我看见他生命的根须在他选定的生活里牢牢扎住，而他舍弃的那一种生活却步步退去，步步退去，直到他几乎再也望不见它。
我看见他，一个挺拔健壮的高个子青年，把胳膊伸给老保姆搀着，陪她在德普特福德那座整洁美丽的园子里散步。我听见他说——

===Original===
“When I was a little boy, nurse, I had mighty strange dreams﻿—of another life than this.”
“Forget them,” she says; “dreams go to the making of all proper men. But now thou art a man; forget the dreams of thy childhood, and play the man to the glory of God and of the house of Arden. And let thy dreams be of the life to come, compared to which all lives on earth are only dreams. And in that life all those who have loved shall meet and be together forevermore, in that life when all the dear and noble dreams of the earthly life shall at last and forever be something more than dreams.”

===Chinese===
「保姆，我还是个小男孩的时候，做过古怪透顶的梦——梦见的是另一种生活，不是这样的。」
「把它们忘了吧，」她说；「梦，能造就一切堂堂正正的男子汉。可如今你已是男子汉了；把童年的梦忘掉，拿出男子汉的样子来，归荣耀于上帝，归荣耀于阿登家族。让你的梦属于来生——比起那来生，尘世上的一切生命都只是梦。在那来生里，凡是相爱过的人都要重逢，永远相聚；在那来生里，尘世生活中一切亲爱而高贵的梦，到头来、到永远，都不再只是梦了。」

## III The Escape / 第三章 出逃

===Original===

When Lady Talbot leaned over the side of the big bed to awaken Dickie Harding she wished with all her heart that she had just such a little boy of her own; and when Dickie awoke and looked in her kind eyes he felt quite sure that if he had had a mother she would have been like this lady.

“Only about the face,” he told himself, “not the way she’s got up; nor yet her hair nor nuffink of that sort.”

“Did you sleep well?” she asked him, stroking his hair with extraordinary gentleness.

“A fair treat,” said he.

===Chinese===

塔尔博特夫人（Lady Talbot）俯下身来，靠在大床边上，唤醒迪基·哈丁（Dickie Harding）；她打心底里巴望，自己也能有这么一个小男孩。迪基醒来，望着她那双慈爱的眼睛，心里笃笃定定地想：他要是有一位妈妈，一定就是这位太太的模样。

「单是脸那一处像，」他心里说，「她那身行头可不像；头发也不像，那些统统都不像。」

「睡得好吗？」她问他，一面抚着他的头发，温柔得不像话。

「好得不得了，」他说。

===Original===

“Was your bed comfortable?”

“Ain’t it soft, neither,” he answered. “I don’t know as ever I felt of anythink quite as soft without it was the geese as ’angs up along the Broadway Christmas-time.”

“Why, the bed’s made of goose-feathers,” she said, and Dickie was delighted by the coincidence.

“ ’Ave you got e’er a little boy?” he asked, pursuing his first waking thought.

===Chinese===

「床睡着舒服吗？」

「软和得很哪，」他答道。「这么软和的东西，我还真没摸过第二回——除非是圣诞节的时候，沿百老汇街（Broadway）挂起来的那些大白鹅。」

「咦，这床就是拿鹅毛填的呀，」她说，迪基为这个巧劲儿乐开了花。

「您有一个小男孩没有？」他问，紧追着他醒来头一个念头不放。

===Original===

“No, dear; if I had I could lend you some of his clothes. As it is, we shall have to put you into your own.” She spoke as though she were sorry.

Dickie saw no matter for regret. “My father ’e bought me a little coat for when it was cold of a night lying out.”

“Lying out? Where?”

===Chinese===

「没有，宝贝；要是有，我就能借几件他的衣裳给你穿。如今呢，只好让你穿你自己的了。」她说得好像满心过意不去。

迪基看不出有什么可遗憾的。「我爸爸给我买过一件小外套，夜里睡在外头，天冷的时候穿的。」

「睡在外头？睡在哪儿呀？」

===Original===

“In the bed with the green curtains,” said Dickie. This led to Here Ward, and Dickie would willingly have told the whole story of that hero in full detail, but the lady said after breakfast, and now it was time for our bath. And sure enough there was a bath of steaming water before the fireplace, which was in quite another part of the room, so that Dickie had not noticed the cans being brought in by a maid in a pink print dress and white cap and apron.

“Come,” said the lady, turning back the bedclothes.

Somehow Dickie could not bear to let that lady see him crawl clumsily across the floor, as he had to do when he moved without his crutch. It was not because he thought she would make fun of him; perhaps it was because he knew she would not. And yet without his crutch, how else was he to get to that bath? And for no reason that he could have given he began to cry.

===Chinese===

「睡在挂着绿帐子的那张床上，」迪基说。这就扯到赫里·沃德身上去了；那位好汉的整个故事，迪基巴不得细细地讲上一遍，可是太太说，等吃过早饭再讲，而眼下正是咱们洗澡的时候。果然，壁炉前摆好了一大盆热气腾腾的水；壁炉是在这屋里顶另一头的地方，所以女仆提着热水罐进进出出，迪基压根儿没有留意——那女仆穿着粉红印花布的连衫裙，戴着白帽子，系着白围裙。

「来吧，」太太说着，掀开了被窝。

不知怎么，迪基就是受不了让这位太太看见他笨拙地在地板上爬——没有拐杖，他一动就得那么爬。倒不是怕她笑话他；也许恰恰是因为他知道她不会笑话。可是没有拐杖，不爬的话，他又怎么能到澡盆跟前去呢？他说不出个所以然，就哭起来了。

===Original===

The lady’s arms were round him in an instant.

“What is it, dear? Whatever is it?” she asked; and Dickie sobbed out﻿—

“I ain’t got my crutch, and I can’t go to that there barf without I got it. Anything ’ud do﻿—if ’twas only an old broom cut down to me ’eighth. I’m a cripple, they call it, you see. I can’t walk like wot you can.”

===Chinese===

一眨眼的工夫，太太的胳膊已经把他搂住了。

「怎么啦，宝贝？到底是什么事呀？」她问；迪基抽抽噎噎地说——

「我的拐杖不在跟前，没有它，那个澡我去不成。随便什么都行——哪怕一把旧扫帚，锯得跟我一般高。我是个瘸子，人家都这么叫的，您瞧。我不能像您那样走路。」

===Original===

She carried him to the bath. There was scented soap, there was a sponge, and a warm, fluffy towel.

“I ain’t had a barf since Gravesend,” said Dickie, and flushed at the indiscretion.

“Since *when*, dear?”

“Since Wednesday,” said Dickie anxiously.

===Chinese===

她抱着他到澡盆那边去。那儿有香皂，有海绵，还有一条又暖和又蓬软的浴巾。

「打从格雷夫森德（Gravesend）起，我就没洗过澡，」迪基说，话一出口自觉失言，臊得满脸通红。

「打*什么时候*起，宝贝？」

「打礼拜三起，」迪基忐忑地说。

===Original===

He and the lady had breakfast together in a big room with long windows that the sun shone in at, and, outside, a green garden. There were a lot of things to eat in silver dishes, and the very eggs had silver cups to sit in, and all the spoons and forks had dogs scratched on them like the one that was carved on the footboard of the bed upstairs. All except the little slender spoon that Dickie had to eat his egg with. And on that there was no dog, but something quite different.

“Why,” said he, his face brightening with joyous recognition, “my Tinkler’s got this on it﻿—just the very moral of it, so ’e ’as.”

Then he had to tell all about Tinkler, and the lady looked thoughtful and interested; and when the gentleman came in and kissed her, and said, How were we this morning, Dickie had to tell about Tinkler all over again; and then the lady said several things very quickly, beginning with, “I told you so, Edward,” and ending with “I knew he wasn’t a common child.”

Dickie missed the middle part of what she said because of the way his egg behaved, suddenly bursting all down one side and running over into the salt, which, of course, had to be stopped at all costs by some means or other. The tongue was the easiest.

===Chinese===

他和太太一起吃早饭，是在一间大屋里，长长的窗子，太阳照进来，窗外是一片绿茵茵的园子。吃的东西盛在银盘里，样样齐全，连鸡蛋都是坐在银蛋杯里的；所有的勺子和叉子上都錾着狗，跟楼上床尾板上雕的那只一个样。只有迪基吃蛋用的那把细长小勺不在其列。那上头没有狗，錾的是一样全然不同的东西。

「咦，」他说，欢喜的相认把小脸都照亮了，「我的叮铃（Tinkler）上头就有这个——一模一样，活脱脱就是它，真个的。」

于是他只好把叮铃的来历原原本本讲了一遍；太太听着，又像沉吟，又兴致勃勃。等那位先生进来，吻了她，问「咱们小家伙今儿早上怎么样哇」，迪基只好把叮铃的事从头再讲一遍；随后太太连珠炮似的说了好些话，起头一句是「我早跟你说过，爱德华」，收尾一句是「我就知道他不是个普通孩子」。

她话中间那一段，迪基没有听着，因为他那只鸡蛋忽然使起性子来，哗啦一下从一边裂到底，淌进了盐碟里。这当然得不管什么代价，想个法子止住才行。用舌头最容易。

===Original===

The gentleman laughed. “Weh! don’t eat the eggcup,” he said. “We shall want it again. Have another egg.”

But Dickie’s pride was hurt, and he wouldn’t. The gentleman must be very stupid, he thought, not to know the difference between licking and eating. And as if anybody could eat an eggcup, anyhow! He was glad when the gentleman went away.

After breakfast Dickie was measured for a crutch﻿—that is to say, a broom was held up beside him and a piece cut off its handle. Then the lady wrapped flannel around the hairy part of the broom and sewed black velvet over that. It was a beautiful crutch, and Dickie said so. Also he showed his gratitude by inviting the lady to look “ ’ow spry ’e was on ’is pins,” but she only looked a very little while, and then turned and gazed out of the window. So Dickie had a good look at the room and the furniture﻿—it was all different from anything he ever remembered seeing, and yet he couldn’t help thinking he had seen them before, these high-backed chairs covered with flowers, like on carpets; the carved bookcases with rows on rows of golden-beaded books; the bow-fronted, shining sideboard, with handles that shone like gold, and the corner cupboard with glass doors and china inside, red and blue and goldy. It was a very odd feeling. I don’t think that I can describe it better than by saying that he looked at all these things with a double pleasure﻿—the pleasure of looking at new and beautiful things, and the pleasure of seeing again things old and beautiful which he had not seen for a very long time.

His limping survey of the room ended at the windows, when the lady turned suddenly, knelt down, put her hand under his chin and looked into his eyes.

===Chinese===

先生笑了。「喂！别把蛋杯吃了，」他说。「待会儿还要用呢。再吃一个蛋。」

可迪基的自尊心受了伤，他不肯再吃。这先生准是很笨，他想，连舔和吃都分不清。再说了，谁又能把蛋杯吃了去呢！等到先生走了，他才高兴起来。

早饭过后，给迪基量了尺寸做拐杖——也就是说，把一把扫帚在他身旁比着竖起来，从扫帚柄上截下一段。然后太太在那截扫帚毛茸茸的地方裹上法兰绒，法兰绒外面再缝上黑丝绒。这真是一根漂亮的拐杖，迪基也这么说了。他还请太太瞧瞧「他这两条腿脚儿多利索」，以此表示感激；可太太只看了顶小的一会儿，就转过身去，望着窗外出了神。于是迪基把屋子和家具好好端详了一遍——样样都跟他记忆里见过的任何东西不同，可他又不由得觉得从前见过它们：这些蒙着花套子的高背椅，花色跟地毯上的一样；雕花的书柜，一排又一排金顶的书；凸肚面亮闪闪的餐具柜，柜把儿金晃晃的；还有角柜，玻璃门里头陈着红、蓝、金的瓷器。这感觉古怪极了。我想我顶好的说法是：他怀着双重的高兴看着这一切——一样是看着新鲜漂亮东西的高兴，一样是重见旧时漂亮东西的高兴，那些东西他已经很久很久没有见过了。

他一瘸一拐地把屋子看到窗前，正好太太忽然转过身来，屈下一膝，一只手托起他的下巴，望着他的眼睛。

===Original===

“Dickie,” she said, “how would you like to stay here and be *my* little boy?”

“I’d like it right enough,” said he, “only I got to go back to father.”

“But if father says you may?”

“ ’E won’t,” said Dickie, with certainty, “an’ besides, there’s Tinkler.”

===Chinese===

「迪基，」她说，「留下来做*我的*小男孩，你乐意不乐意？」

「那敢情好，」他说，「只是我得回到爸爸那儿去。」

「可要是爸爸说行呢？」

「他不会的，」迪基一口咬定，「再说，还有叮铃呢。」

===Original===

“Well, you’re to stay here and be my little boy till we find out where father is. We shall let the police know. They’re sure to find him.”

“The pleece!” Dickie cried in horror. “Why, father, ’e ain’t done nothing.”

“No, no, of course not,” said the lady in a hurry; “but the police know all sorts of things﻿—about where people are, I know, and what they’re doing﻿—even when they haven’t *done* anything.”

“The pleece knows a jolly sight too much,” said Dickie, in gloom.

And now all Dickie’s little soul was filled with one longing; all his little brain awake to one only thought: the police were to be set on the track of Beale, the man whom he called father; the man who had been kind to him, had wheeled him in a perambulator for miles and miles through enchanted country; the man who had bought him a little coat “to put on o’ nights if it was cold or wet”; the man who had shown him the wonderful world to which he awakens who has slept in the bed with the green curtains.

===Chinese===

「这样吧，在咱们打听出爸爸在哪儿之前，你就留在这儿做我的小男孩。我们要告诉警察。他们准能把他找到。」

「警察！」迪基吓得喊起来。「哎哟，爸爸，他可什么坏事也没干过呀。」

「不不，当然没有，」太太慌忙说；「可是警察样样事情都知道——我知道，人家在哪儿啦、在干什么啦——哪怕人家什么坏事也没*干*过。」

「警察知道得可太多啦，」迪基阴沉沉地说。

这一下，迪基整个小小的心灵只装得下一桩渴望；他整个小小的脑瓜只醒着一个念头：警察要循踪去追比尔（Beale）——他叫爸爸的那个人；那个待他好、推着婴儿车带他穿过迷人的乡野、一英里又一英里的人；那个给他买小外套、「夜里天冷天潮就穿上」的人；那个让他看见了奇妙世界的人——凡是睡过那张挂着绿帐子的床的人，一觉醒来就能到那个世界里去。

===Original===

The lady’s house was more beautiful than anything he had ever imagined﻿—yet not more beautiful than certain things that he almost imagined that he remembered. The lady was better than beautiful, she was dear. Her eyes were the eyes to which it is good to laugh﻿—her arms were the arms in which it is good to cry. The tree-dotted parkland was to Dickie the Land of Heart’s Desire.

But father﻿—Beale﻿—who had been kind, whom Dickie loved!﻿ ﻿…

The lady left him alone with a book, beautiful beyond his dreams﻿—three great volumes with pictures of things that had happened and been since the days of Hereward himself. The author’s charming name was Green, and recalled curtains and nights under the stars.

But even those beautiful pictures could not keep Dickie’s thoughts from Mr. Beale: “father” by adoption and love. If the police were set to find out “where he was and what he was doing?”﻿ ﻿… Somehow or other Dickie must get to Gravesend, to that house where there had been a bath, or something like it, in a pail, and where kindly tramp-people had toasted herrings and given apples to little boys who helped.

===Chinese===

太太的宅子比他想象过的任何东西都美——却又美不过他几乎觉得自己记得的某些东西。这位太太不止是美，她是可亲。她的眼睛，是让人对着笑才好的眼睛；她的怀抱，是让人在里头哭才好的怀抱。树木点点的庄园草地，在迪基眼里就是心愿之乡。

可是爸爸——比尔——那个待他那么好、迪基那么爱着的人哪！……

太太留他一个人看书。那书美得超出他的梦——三大卷，画着打从赫里沃德自己那个年代以来，人间发生过的、有过的种种事情。作者的名字很可爱，叫格林，叫人想起那顶绿帐子，想起星斗底下过夜的时辰。

可就连那些美丽的画片，也拴不住迪基对比尔先生的想念：那位靠疼爱收下的「爸爸」。要是警察奉命去查他「在哪儿、正干什么」……不管怎么样，迪基总得赶到格雷夫森德去，赶到那座房子——那里头曾在木桶里盛着洗澡水，或者差不离的什么；那里心肠好的流浪人烤过鲱鱼，把苹果赏给帮了忙的小男孩。

===Original===

He had helped then. And by all the laws of fair play there ought to be someone now to help him.

The beautiful book lay on the table before him, but he no longer saw it. He no longer cared for it. All he cared for was to find a friend who would help him. And he found one. And the friend who helped him was an enemy.

The smart, pink-frocked, white-capped, white-aproned maid, who, unseen by Dickie, had brought the bath-water and the bath, came in with a duster. She looked malevolently at Dickie.

“Shovin’ yourself in,” she said rudely.

===Chinese===

他那时候是帮了忙的。按一切公平的规矩，如今也该有人来帮帮他了。

美丽的书摊在他面前的桌子上，可他不再看见它了。他也不再稀罕它了。他一心想要的，只是找一个肯帮他的朋友。他也真找着一个。而这帮助他的朋友，原来是个冤家。

那个利落的侍女——粉红连衫裙、白帽子、白围裙——先前趁迪基不留神，把洗澡水和澡盆都送了进来的——拿着掸尘布进来了。她恶狠狠地瞪着迪基。

「上赶着往里挤，」她粗声粗气地说。

===Original===

“I ain’t,” said he.

“If she wants to make a fool of a kid, ain’t I got clever brothers and sisters?” inquired the maid, her chin in the air.

“Nobody says you ain’t, and nobody ain’t makin’ a fool of me,” said Dickie.

“Ho no. Course they ain’t,” the maid rejoined. “People comes ’ere without e’er a shirt to their backs and makes fools of their betters. That’s the way it is, ain’t it? Ain’t she arst you to stay and be ’er little boy?”

“Yes,” said Dickie.

“Ah, I thought she ’ad,” said the maid triumphantly; “and you’ll stay. But if I’m expected to call you Master Whatever-your-silly-name-is, I gives a month’s warning, so I tell you straight.”

===Chinese===

「我没有，」他说。

「太太要是想拿个娃娃寻开心，难道我就没有一帮机灵的兄弟姐妹？」女仆下巴翘得老高，反问。

「没人说你没有，也没人拿我寻开心，」迪基说。

「哼，可不是嘛。当然没有，」女仆回敬道。「有的人连件衬衫都没得穿，就上这儿来，倒把比他们体面的人耍得团团转。世道就是这样，不是吗？太太是不是求过你，要你留下做她的小男孩来着？」

「是，」迪基说。

「啊，我就知道她求过，」女仆得意洋洋地说；「你也会留下的。不过要是还得叫我管你叫『什么傻名字的少爷』，我就提前一个月辞工——我把话直说了。」

===Original===

“I don’t want to stay,” said Dickie﻿—“at least﻿—”

“Oh, tell me another,” said the girl impatiently, and left him, without having made the slightest use of the duster.

Dickie was taken for a drive in a little carriage drawn by a cream-colored pony with a long tail﻿—a perfect dream of a pony, and the lady allowed him to hold the reins. But even amid this delight he remembered to ask whether she had put the police on to father yet, and was relieved to hear that she had not.

It was Markham who was told to wash Dickie’s hands when the drive was over, and Markham was the enemy with the clever brothers and sisters.

“Wash ’em yourself,” she said among the soap and silver and marble and sponges. “It ain’t my work.”

===Chinese===

「我不想留下，」迪基说——「至少——」

「得了，别又来哄我，」姑娘不耐烦地说，丢下他走了，那掸尘布一下儿也没掸。

有人带迪基坐着小马车去兜风，拉车的是一匹奶油色的小马，尾巴长长的——一匹完美得像梦的小马，太太还让他握着缰绳。可就在这欢喜里，他还惦记着问，警察是不是已经派去追爸爸了；听说还没有，他心里一松。

兜风完了，被叫来给迪基洗手的是马卡姆（Markham），而马卡姆，就是那个有一帮机灵兄弟姐妹的冤家。

「你自己洗，」她说，站在香皂、银盆、大理石和海绵中间。「这不是我的活儿。」

===Original===

“You’d better,” said Dickie, “or the lady’ll know the difference. It ain’t my work neither, and I ain’t so used to washing as what you are, and that’s the truth.”

So she washed him, not very gently.

“It’s no use your getting your knife into me,” he said as the towel was plied. “I didn’t *arst* to come ’ere, did I?”

“No, you little thief!”

“Stow that!” said Dickie, and after a quick glance at his set lips she said, “Well, next door to, anyhow. I should be ashamed to show my face ’ere, if I was you, after last night. There, you’re dry now. Cut along down to the dining-room. The servants’ hall’s good enough for honest people as don’t break into houses.”

===Chinese===

「你还是给洗吧，」迪基说，「不然太太瞧得出差在哪儿。这也不是我的活儿，再说我不像你，洗手洗惯了的，这是实话。」

于是她给他洗了，下手可不大温柔。

「你存心跟我过不去，也没用，」毛巾一下一下地抹着，他说。「又不是我*求*着要来的，对不对？」

「可不是，你这个小贼！」

「少来这套！」迪基说。她飞快地瞟了一眼他抿紧的嘴唇，说：「呃，反正也差不离儿。换了是我，昨儿夜里干了那号事，今天可没脸在这儿见人。好啦，你干爽了。快跑下楼，上饭厅去。仆人饭厅招待的是正经人，正经人可不撬人家的房子。」

===Original===

All through that day of wonder, which included real roses that you could pick and smell and real gooseberries that you could gather and eat, as well as picture-books, a clockwork bear, a musical box, and a doll’s house almost as big as a small villa, an idea kept on hammering at the other side of a locked door in Dickie’s mind, and when he was in bed it got the door open and came out and looked at him. And he recognized it at once as a really useful idea.

“Markham will bring you some warm milk. Drink it up and sleep well, darling,” said the lady; and with the idea very near and plain he put his arms round her neck and hugged her.

“Goodbye,” he said; “you *are* good. I do love you.” The lady went away very pleased.

When Markham came with the milk Dickie said, “You want me gone, don’t you?”

===Chinese===

整整这个奇妙的日子——里头有真正摘得下来闻的玫瑰，真正摘得下来吃的醋栗，还有图画书、一只上发条的熊、一只音乐盒，和一座差不多赶得上小别墅的娃娃屋——一个念头始终隔着迪基心里一扇上了锁的门，咚咚地敲；等他上了床，那念头把门弄开了，走出来，望着他。他一眼就认出来，这是个真正管用的念头。

「马卡姆会给你送热牛奶来。喝完了好好睡，宝贝，」太太说；念头近在手边、清清楚楚，他搂住她的脖子，紧紧抱了抱她。

「再见，」他说；「您*真*是好。我是真心喜欢您。」太太欢欢喜喜地走了。

马卡姆端着牛奶来了，迪基说：「你想让我走，是不是？」

===Original===

Markham said she didn’t care.

“Well, but how am I to get away﻿—with my crutch?”

“Mean to say you’d cut and run if you was the same as me﻿—about the legs, I mean?”

“Yes,” said Dickie.

“And not nick anything?”

===Chinese===

马卡姆说她才不在乎。

「那好，可我怎么走得脱——拄着拐？」

「你的意思是，要是腿脚跟我一样，你就拔腿就跑——我是说腿？」

「是，」迪基说。

「而且什么都不顺走？」

===Original===

“Not a bloomin’ thing,” said he.

“Well,” said Markham, “you’ve got a spirit, I will say that.”

“You see,” said Dickie, “I wants to get back to farver.”

“Bless the child,” said Markham, quite affected by this.

“Why don’t you help me get out? Once I was outside the park I’d do all right.”

===Chinese===

「一样也不顺走，半点儿也不，」他说。

「行，」马卡姆说，「你这个人有骨气，这话我认。」

「你瞧，」迪基说，「我要回到爸爸那儿去。」

「这孩子哟，」马卡姆说，听得很有些动心。

「你干嘛不帮我出去？只要出了庄园的地界儿，我自己就有法子了。」

===Original===

“Much as my place is worth,” said Markham; “don’t you say another word getting me into trouble.”

But Dickie said a good many other words, and fell asleep quite satisfied with the last words that had fallen from Markham. These words were: “We’ll see.”

It was only just daylight when Markham woke him. She dressed him hurriedly, and carried him and his crutch down the back stairs and into that very butler’s pantry through whose window he had crept at the bidding of the red-haired man. No one else seemed to be about.

===Chinese===

「这可抵得上我全部的差事了，」马卡姆说；「你再多说一个字，就是存心害我倒霉。」

可迪基照样说了好多好多话，末了，马卡姆吐出的最后几个字叫他心满意足地睡着了。那几个字是：「再说吧。」

天刚刚放亮，马卡姆就把他叫醒了。她急急忙忙给他穿好衣裳，抱着他，带着他的拐杖，从后楼梯下去，进了那间管家餐具室——正是他听那红头发家伙吩咐、从窗户爬进来的那一间。四下里似乎再没有别人。

===Original===

“Now,” she said, “the gardener he has got a few hampers ready﻿—fruit and flowers and the like﻿—and he drives ’em to the station ’fore anyone’s up. They’d only go to waste if ’e wasn’t to sell ’em. See? An’ he’s a particular friend of mine; and he won’t mind an extry hamper more or less. So out with you. Joe,” she whispered, “you there?”

Joe, outside, whispered that he was. And Markham lifted Dickie to the window. As she did so she kissed him.

“Cheer﻿—oh, old chap!” she said. “I’m sorry I was so short. An’ you do want to get out of it, don’t you?”

“No error,” said Dickie; “an’ I’ll never split about him selling the vegetables and things.”

===Chinese===

「听着，」她说，「园丁备好了几只货筐——果子啦、鲜花啦什么的——赶在人家没起身就送去车站。他要不拿去卖，就全白糟蹋了。懂吗？他跟我交情不浅；多一筐少一筐，他不会计较的。所以你出去吧。乔（Joe），」她悄声说，「你在外头吗？」

乔在外头悄声应了声在。马卡姆把迪基举到窗口。举起来的时候，她亲了他一下。

「好走——哦，老伙计！」她说。「先前说话那么冲，对不住。你是真心想脱身，对不对？」

「错不了，」迪基说；「他卖菜卖花那些事儿，打死我也不说出去。」

===Original===

“You’re too sharp to live,” Markham declared; and next moment he was through the window, and Joe was laying him in a long hamper half-filled with straw that stood waiting.

“I’ll put you in the van along with the other hampers,” whispered Joe as he shut the lid. “Then when you’re in the train you just cut the string with this ’ere little knife I’ll make you a present of and out you gets. I’ll make it all right with the guard. He knows me. And he’ll put you down at whatever station you say.”

“Here, don’t forget ’is breakfast,” said Markham, reaching her arm through the window. It was a wonderful breakfast. Five cold rissoles, a lot of bread and butter, two slices of cake, and a bottle of milk. And it was fun eating agreeable and unusual things, lying down in the roomy hamper among the smooth straw. The jolting of the cart did not worry Dickie at all. He was used to the perambulator; and he ate as much as he wanted to eat, and when that was done he put the rest in his pocket and curled up comfortably in the straw, for there was still quite a lot left of what ordinary people consider night, and also there was quite a lot left of the sleepiness with which he had gone to bed at the end of the wonderful day. It was not only just body-sleepiness: the kind you get after a long walk or a long play day. It was mind-sleepiness﻿—Dickie had gone through so much in the last thirty-six hours that his poor little brain felt quite worn out. He fell asleep among the straw, fingering the clasp-knife in his pocket, and thinking how smartly he would cut the string when the time came.

And he slept for a very long time. Such a long time that when he did wake up there was no longer any need to cut the string of the hamper. Someone else had done that, and the lid of the basket was open, and three or four faces looked down at Dickie, and a girl’s voice said﻿—

===Chinese===

「你这机灵劲儿，都机灵得活不长啦，」马卡姆说；话音没落，他人已经出了窗，乔正把他放进一只等在那儿的长货筐，筐里垫着半筐稻草。

「我把你连筐搁上大车，跟别的筐搁作一堆，」乔合上筐盖，悄声说。「等你上了火车，就用这把小刀把绳子割断——这刀是送你的——你就出得来了。列车员那头我会打点好，他认得我。你说在哪个站下，他就把你放哪儿。」

「喂，别忘了他那份早饭，」马卡姆说着，把胳膊从窗口探进去。那真是一份了不起的早饭：五个冷炸肉饼，好些面包和黄油，两片蛋糕，外加一瓶牛奶。躺在宽宽敞敞的大货筐里，身子底下是滑溜溜的稻草，吃着这些香喷喷的稀罕东西，真是有趣。大车的颠簸一点也搅扰不着迪基——婴儿车他是坐惯了的；他痛快吃了个饱，剩下的都装进口袋，舒舒服服蜷进稻草里。因为平常人算作夜的这段光阴，这会儿还剩着好长一段；而那个奇妙的日子到头时他带去上床的那份睡意，也还剩着好大一团。那还不单是身子困——走了远路、疯玩了一天之后的那种困；那是脑筋困——这三十六个钟头里，迪基经过的事太多太多，可怜的小脑瓜实在累乏了。他在稻草里睡着了，手指头拨弄着兜里的折刀，心想到时候割绳子，一定要割得又利索又漂亮。

他睡了好久好久。久到醒来的时候，筐绳已经不用他割了。早有别人割过了，筐盖敞开着，三四个面孔低头望着迪基，一个姑娘的声音说——

===Original===

“Why, it’s a little boy! And a crutch﻿—oh, dear!” Dickie sat up. The little crutch, which was lying corner-wise above him in the hamper, jerked out and rattled on the floor.

“Well, I never did﻿—never!” said another voice. “Come out, dearie; don’t be frightened.”

“How kind people are!” Dickie thought, and reached his hands to slender white hands that were held out to him. A lady in black﻿—her figure was as slender as her hands﻿—drew him up, put her arms round him, and lifted him on to a black bentwood chair.

His eyes, turning swiftly here and there, showed him that he was in a shop﻿—a shop full of flowers and fruit.

===Chinese===

「哎呀，是个小男孩！还有一根拐杖——哎哟天哪！」迪基坐起来。那根小拐杖，方才在筐里斜横在他头顶上方，一下子蹦了出去，当啷啷落在地板上。

「哎哟，真是活见——活见鬼了！」另一个声音说。「出来吧，小乖乖；别害怕。」

「人们多好心呀！」迪基想，把自己的手伸向那双朝他伸来的纤细白手。一位黑衣太太——身段跟她的手一样纤细——把他拉了上来，搂住他，把他抱上一把黑色的曲木椅子。

他的眼睛滴溜溜地四下一转，看出自己是在一家铺子里——一家满放着鲜花和水果的铺子。

===Original===

“Mr. Rosenberg,” said the slender lady﻿—“oh, do come here, please! This extra hamper﻿—”

A dark, handsome, big-nosed man came towards them.

“It’s a dear little boy,” said the slender lady, who had a pale, kind face, dark eyes, and very red lips.

“It’th a practical joke, I shuppothe,” said the dark man. “Our gardening friend wanth a liththon: and I’ll thee he getth it.”

“It wasn’t his fault,” said Dickie, wriggling earnestly in his high chair; “it was my fault. I fell asleep.”

===Chinese===

「罗森贝格先生（Mr. Rosenberg），」苗条的太太说——「哦，请您快过来！这只多出来的货筐——」

一个黑脸膛、模样体面、大鼻子的男人朝他们走来。

「是个招人疼的小男孩，」苗条的太太说。她有一张白净和善的脸，深色的眼睛，嘴唇红得很。

「我看系个恶作剧，」那黑脸汉子咬着舌头说。「咱们那位种花的朋友系想要个教训：那我就成全他，叫他得着这个教训。」

「这不怪他，」迪基在高高的椅子上着急地扭着身子说；「怪我。我睡着了。」

===Original===

The girls crowded round him with questions and caresses.

“I ought to have cut the string in the train and told the guard﻿—he’s a friend of the gardener’s,” he said, “but I was asleep. I don’t know as ever I slep’ so sound afore. Like as if I’d had sleepy-stuff﻿—you know. Like they give me at the orspittle.”

I should not like to think that Markham had gone so far as to put “sleepy-stuff” in that bottle of milk; but I am afraid she was not very particular, and she may have thought it best to send Dickie to sleep so that he could not betray her or her gardener friend until he was very far away from both of them.

“But why,” asked the long-nosed gentleman﻿—“why put boyth in bathketth? Upthetting everybody like thith,” he added crossly.

===Chinese===

姑娘们把他团团围住，问长问短，又搂又摸。

「我本该在火车上就把绳子割断，告诉列车员——他跟园丁是朋友，」他说；「可我睡着了。我从前还没睡得这么死过。就像吃过蒙汗药似的——你们知道的。就像医院里给我吃的那种。」

我可不愿意往坏处想，说马卡姆居然在那瓶牛奶里搁了「蒙汗药」；不过我怕她这个人做事本就不大讲究，兴许她觉得，顶好的法子就是叫迪基睡死过去，这样，在离她和她那位园丁朋友都还近的时候，迪基就招不出她们俩来。

「可是为什么，」长鼻子先生问——「为什么把男娃装进筐子里？像这样搅得人人心烦，」他没好气地补了一句。

===Original===

“It was,” said Dickie slowly, “a sort of joke. I don’t want to go upsetting of people. If you’ll lift me down and give me me crutch I’ll ’ook it.”

But the young ladies would not hear of his hooking it.

“We may keep him, mayn’t we, Mr. Rosenberg?” they said; and he judged that Mr. Rosenberg was a kind man or they would not have dared to speak so to him; “let’s keep him till closing-time, and then one of us will see him home. He lives in London. He says so.”

Dickie had indeed murmured “words to this effect,” as policemen call it when they are not quite sure what people really *have* said.

“Ath you like,” said Mr. Rosenberg, “only you muthn’t let him interfere with bithneth; thath all.”

===Chinese===

「那算是，」迪基慢慢地说，「一种玩笑吧。我不想搅得人家不安。你们要肯把我抱下来，把我的拐杖给我，我就开溜。」

可小姐们说什么也不肯放他走。

「咱们把他留下，行不行，罗森贝格先生？」她们说；他由此断定罗森贝格先生是个好心人，不然她们不敢这样跟他说话；「把他留到打烊，到时我们推一个人送他回家。他住在伦敦。他自己说的。」

迪基确实咕哝过「大意如此的话」——警察们拿不准人家真正*说*过什么的时候，就是这么个叫法。

「随你们的便，」罗森贝格先生说，「只系别叫他搅了买卖；就系这么回事。」

===Original===

They took him away to the back of the shop. They were dear girls, and they were very nice to Dickie. They gave him grapes, and a banana, and some Marie biscuits, and they folded sacks for him to lie on.

And Dickie liked them and was grateful to them﻿—and watched his opportunity. Because, however kind people were, there was one thing he had to do﻿—to get back to the Gravesend lodging-house, as his “father” had told him to do.

The opportunity did not come till late in the afternoon, when one of the girls was boiling a kettle on a spirit-lamp, and one had gone out to get cakes in Dickie’s honor, which made him uncomfortable, but duty is duty, and over the Gravesend lodging-house the star of duty shone and beckoned. The third young lady and Mr. Rosenberg were engaged in animated explanations with a fair young gentleman about a basket of roses that had been ordered, and had not been sent.

===Chinese===

她们把他带到铺子后头去。她们是招人疼的姑娘，待迪基好得很。她们给他葡萄，给他一只香蕉、几块玛丽饼干，还给他把麻袋铺叠起来躺。

迪基喜欢她们，也感激她们——可他一直在守着机会。因为不论人家多好心，有一件事他非做不可——回到格雷夫森德那家客栈去，他的「爸爸」是这么嘱咐的。

机会直到傍晚才来。一个姑娘在酒精灯上坐着水壶；另一个为着迪基的面子，出门买点心去了——这叫他心里老大不安，可是职责是职责，职责之星在格雷夫森德那家客栈的上空闪耀，向他招手。第三位小姐和罗森贝格先生，正跟一位白净脸儿的年轻先生起劲地交涉，为的是一篮订了却没送出去的玫瑰。

===Original===

“Cath,” Mr. Rosenberg was saying﻿—“cath down enthureth thpeedy delivery.”

And the young lady was saying, “I am extremely sorry, sir; it was a misunderstanding.”

And to the music of their two voices Dickie edged along close to the grapes and melons, holding on to the shelf on which they lay so as not to attract attention by the tap-tapping of his crutch.

He passed silently and slowly between the rose-filled window and the heap of bananas that adorned the other side of the doorway, turned the corner, threw his arm over his crutch, and legged away for dear life down a sort of covered Arcade; turned its corner and found himself in a wilderness of baskets and carts and vegetables, threaded his way through them, in and out among the baskets, over fallen cabbage-leaves, under horses’ noses, found a quiet street, a still quieter archway, pulled out the knife﻿—however his adventure ended he was that knife to the good﻿—and prepared to cut the money out of the belt Mr. Beale had buckled round him.

And the belt was not there! Had he dropped it somewhere? Or had he and Markham, in the hurry of that twilight dressing, forgotten to put it on? He did not know. All he knew was that the belt was not on him, and that he was alone in London, without money, and that at Gravesend his father was waiting for him﻿—waiting, waiting. Dickie knew what it meant to wait.

He went out into the street, and asked the first good-natured-looking loafer he saw the way to Gravesend.

===Chinese===

「凯丝（Cath），」罗森贝格先生正说着——「凯丝，打发人送下去，『保证迅速送到』。」

那位小姐正说着——「非常抱歉，先生；这是一个误会。」

就在这两个人声音奏出的乐声里，迪基贴着葡萄和瓜悄悄往前挪，一手扒着搁它们的架子，免得拐杖笃笃的点地声招人注意。

他悄悄地、慢慢地，从摆满玫瑰的橱窗和装点着门口另一侧的那一大堆香蕉中间过去，一拐弯，把胳膊架在拐杖上，拔腿就没命地顺着一条带顶棚的拱廊跑；拐过拱廊的弯，发现自己置身在一片筐子、大车和蔬菜的荒野里，便在筐子中间钻进钻出，踩过掉落的白菜叶，从马鼻子底下穿过去，找到一条僻静的街，一处更僻静的过街门洞，掏出小刀——这场冒险不管怎么收场，这把刀总归是白赚的——准备把比尔先生扣在他腰上的那条腰带里的钱割出来。

可是腰带不在身上！是他掉在什么地方了？还是他和马卡姆在那蒙眬天色里匆匆忙忙穿衣裳，忘了给他系上？他不知道。他只晓得腰带没在他身上，他孤零零一个人在伦敦，身无分文，而在格雷夫森德，他的爸爸在等他——等着，等着。等待是什么滋味，迪基是懂的。

他走到街上，看见头一个面相和善的游手好闲的家伙，就问去格雷夫森德的路。

===Original===

“Way to your grandmother,” said the loafer; “don’t you come saucing of me.”

“But which is the way?” said Dickie.

The man looked hard at him and then pointed with a grimy thumb over his shoulder.

“It’s thirty mile if it’s a yard,” he said. “Got any chink?”

“I lost it,” said Dickie. “My farver’s there awaitin’ for me.”

===Chinese===

「去问你奶奶吧，」那闲汉说；「少跑来跟我耍贫嘴。」

「可到底是哪条道儿呀？」迪基说。

那汉子使劲打量了他一番，然后用一只污黑的拇指朝肩膀后头一指。

「少说也有三十英里，」他说。「身上有现钱吗？」

「钱丢了，」迪基说。「我爸爸在那儿等着我呢。」

===Original===

“Garn!” said the man; “you don’t kid me so easy.”

“I ain’t arstin’ you for anything except the way,” said Dickie.

“More you ain’t,” said the man, hesitated, and pulled his hand out of his pocket. “Ain’t kiddin’? Sure? Father at Gravesend? Take your Bible?”

“Yuss,” said Dickie.

“Then you take the first to the right and the first to the left, and you’ll get a blue bus as’ll take you to the Elephant. That’s a bit of the way. Then you arst again. And ’ere﻿—this’ll pay for the bus.” He held out coppers.

===Chinese===

「去你的吧！」那汉子说；「你想哄我，可没那么容易。」

「我不跟你讨别的，就讨条道儿，」迪基说。

「你才不讨呢，」那汉子说着，犹豫了一下，把手从口袋里抽出来。「不是哄人？真格的？爸爸在格雷夫森德？敢按着圣经起誓？」

「是，」迪基说。

「那你见着头一条街往右拐，再一条往左拐，就能搭上一辆蓝色的公共汽车，把你拉到大象城堡（the Elephant and Castle）去。那能顶一段路。到了再问。喏——这个够你坐车了。」他掏出几枚铜子儿。

===Original===

This practical kindness went to Dickie’s heart more than all the kisses of the young ladies in the flower-shop. The tears came into his eyes.

“Well, you *are* a pal, and no error,” he said. “Do the same for you some day,” he added.

The lounging man laughed.

“I’ll hold you to that, matey,” he said; “when you’re a-ridin’ in yer carriage an’ pair p’raps you’ll take me on ter be yer footman.”

===Chinese===

这份实实在在的好心，比花铺里那些小姐的亲吻更打动了迪基的心窝。他眼里涌上了泪水。

「嘿，你*真*够朋友，错不了，」他说。「哪天我也照样帮你，」他添了一句。

那个游游荡荡的汉子笑了。

「这话我可记下了，好伙计，」他说；「等你坐上马车、套着一对马，说不定倒要我给你当跟班呢。」

===Original===

“When I am, I will,” said Dickie, quite seriously. And then they both laughed.

The Elephant and Castle marks but a very short stage of the weary way between London and Gravesend. When he got out of the tram Dickie asked the way again, this time of a woman who was selling matches in the gutter. She pointed with the blue box she held in her hand.

“It’s a long way,” she said, in a tired voice; “nigh on thirty mile.”

===Chinese===

「到那天我就这么办，」迪基说得一本正经。随后两个人都笑了。

「大象城堡」不过是伦敦与格雷夫森德之间这条累人的长路上短短的一程。他下了电车，迪基又问路，这回问的是一个蹲在街沟里卖火柴的女人。她拿手里攥着的蓝色小匣子一指。

「远着呢，」她用疲惫的声音说；「将近三十英里呢。」

===Original===

“Thank you, missis,” said Dickie, and set out, quite simply, to walk those miles﻿—nearly thirty. The way lay down the Old Kent Road, and presently Dickie was in familiar surroundings. For the Old Kent Road leads into the New Cross Road, and that runs right through the yellow brick wilderness where Dickie’s aunt lived. He dared not follow the road through those well-known scenes. At any moment he might meet his aunt. And if he met his aunt﻿ ﻿… he preferred not to think of it.

Outside the Marquis of Granby stood a van, and the horses’ heads were turned away from London. If one could get a lift? Dickie looked anxiously to right and left, in front and behind. There were wooden boxes in the van, a lot of them, and on the canvas of the tilt was painted in fat, white letters﻿—

> Fry’s Tonic
> The Only Cure

===Chinese===

「谢谢您，太太，」迪基说，然后简简单单地上了路，要一步一步把那段路走完——将近三十英里。路顺着旧肯特路（the Old Kent Road）下去，不一会儿，迪基就置身在熟识的环境里了。因为旧肯特路通进纽克罗斯路（the New Cross Road），纽克罗斯路正穿过那片黄砖头的荒原，迪基的姨妈就住在那荒原里。他可不敢顺着大路穿过那些熟门熟路的地方。说不准哪一刻就会撞上姨妈。要是撞上了姨妈——他情愿不去想它。

「格兰比侯爵」酒馆（the Marquis of Granby）门外停着一辆大篷车，马头背着伦敦的方向。要是能搭一段车呢？迪基焦急地左右前后张望。车上装着木箱，好大的一堆，篷车的帆布上用肥肥的白字漆着——

> 弗里氏补药
> 唯一特效药

===Original===

There would be room on the top of the boxes﻿—they did not reach within two feet of the tilt.

Should he ask for a lift, when the carter came out of the Marquis? Or should he, if he could, climb up and hide on the boxes and take his chance of discovery on the lift? He laid a hand on the tailboard.

“Hi, Dickie!” said a voice surprisingly in his ear; “that you?”

Dickie owned that it was, with the feeling of a trapped wild animal, and turned and faced a boy of his own age, a schoolfellow﻿—the one, in fact, who had christened him “Dot-and-go-one.”

===Chinese===

木箱顶上还搁得下人——箱子顶离车篷还差着两英尺呢。

等赶车的从「侯爵」酒馆里出来，他该开口讨个搭车呢？还是只要他能行，就自己爬上去，藏身在木箱上头，冒着被发觉的危险搭这一程？他一只手已经搭上了车尾挡板。

「嘿，迪基！」一个声音出其不意地贴着他耳朵说；「是你吗？」

迪基认下了是自己，心里像一头被堵进陷阱的野兽；一转身，面对的是个跟他同岁的男孩，一位同学——说起来，正是给他起「笃一步」（Dot-and-go-one）这个绰号的那一位。

===Original===

“Oh, what a turn you give me!” he said; “thought you was my aunt. Don’t you let on you seen me.”

“Where you been?” asked the boy curiously.

“Oh, all about,” Dickie answered vaguely. “Don’t you tell me aunt.”

“Yer aunt? Don’t you know?” The boy was quite contemptuous with him for not knowing.

“Know? No. Know what?”

===Chinese===

「哦，你倒吓了我好一大跳！」他说；「我还当是我姨妈呢。你可别露口风，说你见过我。」

「你上哪儿去了？」男孩好奇地问。

「哦，到处走了走，」迪基含含糊糊地答。「别告诉我姨妈。」

「你姨妈？你不知道？」男孩见他连这都不知道，好生瞧不起他。

「知道？不知道。知道什么？」

===Original===

“She shot the moon﻿—old Hurle moved her; says he don’t remember where to. She give him a pint to forget’s what I say.”

“Who’s livin’ there now?” Dickie asked, interest in his aunt’s address swallowed up in a sudden desperate anxiety.

“No one don’t live there. It’s shut up to let apply Roberts 796 Broadway,” said the boy. “I say, what’ll you do?”

“I don’t know,” said Dickie, turning away from the van, which had abruptly become unimportant. “Which way you goin’?”

“Down home﻿—go past your old shop. Coming?”

===Chinese===

「她来了个连夜开溜，房钱都没结——老赫尔（Hurle）把她撵走的；说是他自己也想不起来她搬去了哪儿。要我说呀，是她塞给他一品脱酒，才叫他记不起来的。」

「如今谁住在那儿？」迪基问。他本来还惦着姨妈的去向，这会儿一股突然涌上来的、急得要命的焦灼把它整个儿吞没了。

「谁也不在那儿住。房子锁了招租，要租去问百老汇街 796 号的罗伯茨（Roberts），」男孩说。「哎，你打算怎么办？」

「不知道，」迪基说着，从大车旁边转过身去——那车忽然变得毫不相干了。「你往哪边走？」

「回家去——打你那家老铺子门前过。一块儿走不？」

===Original===

“No,” said Dickie. “So long﻿—see you again some day. I got to go this way.” And he went it.

All the same the twilight saw him creeping down the old road to the house whose backyard had held the rabbit-hutch, the garden where he had sowed the parrot food, and where the moonflowers had come up so white and beautiful. What a long time ago! It was only a month really, but all the same, what a long time!

The news of his aunt’s departure had changed everything. The steadfast desire to get to Gravesend, to find his father, had given way, at any rate for the moment, to a burning anxiety about Tinkler and the white stone. Had his aunt found them and taken them away? If she hadn’t and they were still there, would it not be wise to get them at once? Because of course someone else might take the house and find the treasures. Yes, it would certainly be wise to go tonight, to get in by the front window﻿—the catch had always been broken﻿—to find his treasures, or at any rate to make quite sure whether he had lost them or not.

No one noticed him as he came down the street, very close to the railings. There are so many boys in the streets in that part of the world. And the front window went up easily. He climbed in, dragging his crutch after him.

===Chinese===

「不走那边，」迪基说。「回见——往后总有再见面的一天。我得走这边。」他就走了这边。

尽管如此，暮色还是看见他顺着那条老路，朝那座后院里搁过兔笼的房子溜溜地走去——他播过鹦鹉食（parrot food）的园子就在那儿，月光花就是从那园子里长出来的，长得又白又美。那是多久以前的事啦！其实不过一个月，可不管怎么说，多久啦！

姨妈搬走的消息把一切全改变了。到格雷夫森德去、去找爸爸的那份铁定了的心愿，至少眼下这一时，已经让了位——让给对叮铃和白石头的那股火烧火燎的焦灼。姨妈是不是发现了它们、把它们拿走了？要是她没拿，东西还在那儿，立刻把它们取走，岂不是顶聪明的做法？因为当然啦，说不定别人就会住进这房子，把这些宝贝翻出来。对，今晚就去，那准是稳妥的：从前窗爬进去——那个插销从来就是坏的——把他的宝贝找回来，或者至少彻彻底底弄清楚，它们到底是丢了还是没丢。

他贴着栏杆溜下街去，没有人留意他。在那片地界儿，街上的孩子多的是。前窗轻轻松松就推上去了。他拖着拐杖，爬了进去。

===Original===

He got upstairs very quickly, on hands and knees, went straight to the loose board, dislodged it, felt in the hollow below. Oh, joy! His hands found the soft bundle of rags that he knew held Tinkler and the seal. He put them inside the front of his shirt and shuffled down. It was not too late to do a mile or two of the Gravesend road. But the moonflower﻿—he would like to have one more look at that.

He got out into the garden﻿—there stood the stalk of the flower very tall in the deepening dusk. He touched the stalk. It was dry and hard﻿—three or four little dry things fell from above and rattled on his head.

“Seeds, o’ course,” said Dickie, who knew more about seeds now than he had done when he saved the parrot seeds. One does not tramp the country for a month, at Dickie’s age, without learning something about seeds.

He got out the knife that should have cut the string of the basket in the train, opened it and cut the stalk of the moonflower, very carefully so that none of the seeds should be, and only a few were, lost. He crept into the house holding the stalk upright and steady as an acolyte carries a processional cross.

===Chinese===

他手脚并用地爬上楼，快得很，直奔那块松动的地板，把它撬开，往底下的空膛里摸索。哦，欢喜！他的手摸到了那团软软的破布包——他知道，里头包着叮铃和印章（the seal）。他把它们塞进衬衫前襟，趿趿地下了楼。这时候还来得及在格雷夫森德的路上走上三两英里。可是那株月光花——他想再多看它一眼。

他走到园子里——暮色越来越沉，那株花的花茎直挺挺地立着，好高。他摸了摸花茎。干的，硬的——上头掉下三四个小干东西，在他头顶上刷刷直响。

「是籽儿，这还用说，」迪基说。他如今懂得的籽儿上的学问，比他攒鹦鹉食那会儿多多了。像迪基这般年纪，在乡野间流浪上一个月，免不得要学下一些籽儿的道理。

他掏出那把本该在火车上割筐绳的小刀，打开来，把月光花的花茎割下来，小心得不能再小心，务要一颗籽也不掉——到底掉下去的，也不过寥寥几颗。他擎着花茎，笔直，平稳，像辅祭捧着游行的十字架那样，悄悄进了屋。

===Original===

The house was quite dark now, but a street lamp threw its light into the front room, bare, empty, and dusty. There was a torn newspaper on the floor. He spread a sheet of it out, kneeled by it and shook the moonflower head over it. The seeds came rattling out﻿—dozens and dozens of them. They were bigger than sunflower seeds and flatter and rounder, and they shone like silver, or like the pods of the plant we call honesty.

“Oh, beautiful, beautiful!” said Dickie, letting the smooth shapes slide through his fingers. Have you ever played with mother-of-pearl card counters? The seeds of the moonflower were like those.

He pulled out Tinkler and the seal and laid them on the heap of seeds. And then knew quite suddenly that he was too tired to travel any further that night.

===Chinese===

屋里这会儿全黑了，可是街灯把光照进前屋——屋里空落落的，一无所物，蒙着灰。地上有一张破报纸。他铺开一页，跪在旁边，把月光花的头颅朝报纸上抖。籽儿哗啦啦地落下来——一打又一打。它们比葵花籽大些，更扁些，也更圆些，亮得像银子，或者像我们唤作「银扇」（honesty）的那种植物的荚壳。

「哦，真美，真美！」迪基说，让那些光滑的小东西从指缝里滑过去。你玩过珠母贝的牌筹没有？月光花的籽儿就像那东西。

他掏出叮铃和印章，摆在那一堆籽儿上。随后忽然明白过来：他累透了，今晚再也走不动了。

===Original===

“I’ll doss here,” he said; “there’s plenty papers”﻿—he knew by experience that, as bedclothes, newspapers are warm, if noisy﻿—“and get on in the morning afore people’s up.”

He collected all the paper and straw﻿—there was a good deal littered about in the house﻿—and made a heap in the corner, out of the way of the window. He did not feel afraid of sleeping in an empty house, only very lordly and magnificent because he had a whole house to himself. The food still left in his pockets served for supper, and you could drink quite well at the washhouse tap by putting your head under and turning it on very slowly.

And for a final enjoyment he laid out his treasures on the newspaper﻿—Tinkler and the seal in the middle and the pearly counters arranged in patterns round them, circles and squares and oblongs. The seeds lay very flat and fitted close together. They were excellent for making patterns with. And presently he made, with triple lines of silvery seeds, a six-pointed star, something like this﻿—

with the rattle and the seal in the middle, and the light from the street lamp shone brightly on it all.

“That’s the prettiest of the lot,” said Dickie Harding, alone in the empty house.

And then the magic began.

===Chinese===

「我就在这儿过夜，」他说；「报纸多着呢」——凭经验他知道，报纸当被褥，暖和是暖和，就是吵人——「明儿一早，赶在人家起身之前就上路。」

他把屋里的报纸和稻草归拢起来——屋里散落着好不少——在墙角堆作一堆，避着窗户。睡在一座空房子里，他并不害怕，只觉得自己好不威风、好不了得，因为整整一座房子都归了他一个人。口袋里剩下的吃食当了晚饭；水也喝得挺好——把头凑到洗衣房的水龙头底下，把开关拧得极慢极慢就行。

最后一件乐子，是他把宝贝们在报纸上摆开——叮铃和印章摆在正当中，珠光光的籽儿在四周围出种种花样，圆形、方形、长条形。籽儿躺得平平的，挨得紧紧的，摆花样真是再好没有。不多一会儿，他用三道银亮亮的籽儿线摆出一颗六角星，就像这样——

摇铃和印章摆在正中，街灯的光亮堂堂地照着这一切。

「这一颗是里头顶美的，」迪基·哈丁说，独自一个人，在空房子里。

接着，魔法开始了。

## X The Noble Deed / 第十章 高尚之举

===Original===
When Lord Arden and Elfrida and Edred reached the castle and found that Dickie had not come back, the children concluded that Beale had persuaded him to stay the night at the cottage. And Lord Arden thought that the children must be right. He was extremely annoyed both with Beale and with Dickie for making such an arrangement without consulting him.

“It is impertinent of Beale and thoughtless of the boy,” he said; “and I shall speak a word to them both in the morning.”

But when Edred and Elfrida were gone to bed Lord Arden found that he could not feel quite sure or quite satisfied. Suppose Dickie was not at Beale’s? He strolled up to the cottage to see. Everything was dark at the cottage. He hesitated, then knocked at the door. At the third knock Beale, very sleepy, put his head out of the window.

“Who’s there?” said he.

“I am here,” said Lord Arden. “Richard is asleep, I suppose?”

“I suppose so, my lord,” said Beale, sleepy and puzzled.

===Chinese===
阿登勋爵（Lord Arden）和埃尔弗里达（Elfrida）、埃德雷德（Edred）回到城堡，发现迪基没有回来，两个孩子断定是比尔劝他留在小屋里过夜了。阿登勋爵认为孩子们准没猜错。他对比尔和迪基都非常恼火，怪他们没跟自己商量就做出这样的安排。

「比尔这样做太放肆，那孩子也太欠考虑，」他说，「明儿早上我要跟他们两个都谈一谈。」

可是等埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达上了床，阿登勋爵却发现自己怎么也放不下心，怎么也不满意。万一迪基并不在比尔那儿呢？他信步走到小屋去看。小屋里一片漆黑。他犹豫了一下，敲了敲门。敲到第三下，比尔睡意惺忪地把头探出窗外。

「谁呀？」他说。

「是我，」阿登勋爵说，「理查德睡下了吧？」

「想必是睡下了，老爷，」比尔说，又困又纳闷。

===Original===
“You have given me some anxiety. I had to come up to make sure he was here.”

“But ’e *ain’t* ’ere,” said Beale. “Didn’t you pick ’im up with the dogcart, same as you said you would?”
