“No,” shouted Lord Arden. “Come down, Beale, and get a lantern. There must have been an accident.”

The bedroom window showed a square of light, and Lord Arden below heard Beale blundering about above.

===Chinese===
「你让我担了番心。我不得不走上来，亲眼看看他是不是在这儿。」

「可他*不在这儿*呀，」比尔说，「您不是照您说好的，用轻便马车把他接走了吗？」

「没有！」阿登勋爵大声喊道。「下来，比尔，去提个提灯来。准是出了事。」

卧室的窗户亮起一方光，阿登勋爵站在楼下，听见比尔在楼上跌跌撞撞地摸黑乱转。

===Original===
“ ’Ere’s your coat,” Mrs. Beale’s voice sounded; “never mind lacing up of your boots. You orter gone a bit of the way with ’im.”

“Well, I offered for to go, didn’t I?” Beale growled, blundered down the stairs and out through the washhouse, and came round the corner of the house with a stable lantern in his hand. He came close to where Lord Arden stood—a tall, dark figure in the starlight—and spoke in a voice that trembled.

“The little nipper,” he said; and again, “the little nipper. If anything’s happened to ’im! Swelp me! gov’ner—my lord, I mean. What I meanter say, if anything’s *’appened* to *’im!* One of the best!”

===Chinese===
「你的外衣在这儿，」比尔太太的声音响起，「靴带就别系了。你早该陪他走上一段路的。」

「嘿，我不是说了要陪他去的吗，不是吗？」比尔嘟囔着，跌跌撞撞下了楼，穿过洗衣房出来，手里提着一盏马厩提灯，绕过屋角。他走到阿登勋爵站的地方近旁——星光下，那是一道高高的黑影——说话的声音都在发颤。

「那个小不点儿，」他说；又说了一遍，「那个小不点儿。他要是有个三长两短！老天爷作证！老爷子——我是说，老爷。我想说的是，他要是有个三长两短！顶好顶好的一个孩子！」

===Original===
The two men went quickly towards the gate. As they passed down the quiet, dusty road Beale spoke again.

“I wasn’t no good—I don’t deceive you, guv’ner—a no account man I was, swelp me! And the little ’un, ’e tidied me up and told me tales and kep’ me straight. It was ’is doing me and ’Melia come together. An’ the dogs an’ all. An’ the little one. An’ ’e got me to chuck the cadgin’. An’ worse. ’E don’t know what I was like when I met ’im. Why, I set out to make a blighted burglar of ’im—you wouldn’t believe!”

And out the whole story came as Lord Arden and he went along the gray road, looking to right and left where no bushes were nor stones, only the smooth curves of the down, so that it was easy to see that no little boy was there either.

===Chinese===
两个人快步朝栅栏门走去。沿着那条安静的、满是尘土的路往下走时，比尔又开了口。

「我从前不是个好东西——我不骗您，老爷——我就是个不中用的货，老天爷作证！可那个小人儿，是他把我拾掇得像个人样，讲故事给我听，让我走上正道。多亏了他，我和米莉亚（’Melia）才成了家。还有那几条狗，全靠他。还有小家伙。是他劝我把偷鸡摸狗的营生戒了。还有更不堪的。他不知道我刚遇上他的时候我是个什么样的人。嘿，我起先还打算把他培养成一个该死的贼呢——您简直不会相信！」

于是，在阿登勋爵和他沿着灰色的大路往前走的时候，那件事从头到尾都倒了出来。两人左顾右盼，可路边既没有灌木也没有石头，只有丘陵圆滑平缓的曲线，一眼就能看出，那里也不可能藏着一个小男孩。

===Original===
They looked for Dickie to right and left and here and there under bushes, and by stiles and hedges, and with trembling hearts they searched in the little old chalk quarry, and the white moon came up very late to help them. But they did not find him, though they roused a dozen men in the village to join in the search, and old Beale himself, who knew every yard of the ground for five miles round, came out with the spaniel who knew every inch of it for ten. But True rushed about the house and garden whining and yelping so piteously that ’Melia tied him up, and he stayed tied up.

And so, when Edred and Elfrida came down to breakfast, Mrs. Honeysett met them with the news that Dickie was lost and their father still out looking for him.

===Chinese===
他们左左右右、到处寻找迪基——灌木底下，栅门旁、树篱边，又提心吊胆地搜了那座小小的旧白垩采石场。白色的月亮很晚才升起来帮他们的忙。可他们没有找到他。他们叫醒了村里十几个人加入搜寻；老比尔自己熟悉方圆五英里内的每一寸地皮，也带着那条熟识方圆十英里每一寸地皮的猎犬出了门。特拉斯蒂（True）急得在屋里屋外、园子里乱窜，呜呜地哀叫，叫得那么凄惨，米莉亚只好把它拴了起来，它就一直被拴着。

就这样，埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达下楼来吃早饭时，哈尼塞特太太（Mrs. Honeysett）迎面带来的消息是：迪基失踪了，他们的父亲还在外面找他。

===Original===
“It’s that beastly magic,” said Edred as soon as the children were alone. “He’s done it once too often, and he’s got stuck some time in history and can’t get back.”

“And we can’t do anything. We can’t get to him,” said Elfrida. “Oh! if only we’d got the old white magic and the Mouldiwarp to help us, we could find out what’s become of him.”

“Perhaps he has fallen down a disused mine,” Edred suggested, “and is lying panting for water, and his faithful dog has jumped down after him and broken all its dear legs.”

===Chinese===
「都怪那该死的魔法，」等只剩下孩子们自己，埃德雷德立刻说。「他施展的次数太多了，这回准是卡在历史上的哪个时候回不来了。」

「我们又什么忙也帮不上。我们够不着他，」埃尔弗里达说。「唉！要是我们还有那种古老的白魔法，还有莫德瓦普（Mouldiwarp）帮我们，就能弄清楚他到底怎么了。」

「说不定他掉进了一口废矿井，」埃德雷德猜道，「正躺在下面渴得直喘气，他那只忠心的狗也跟着跳了下去，把四条可爱的腿全摔断了。」

===Original===
Elfrida melted to tears at this desperate picture, melted to a speechless extent.

“We can’t do anything,” said Edred again; “don’t snivel like that, for goodness’ sake, Elfrida. This is a man’s job. Dry up. I can’t think, with you blubbing like that.”

“I’m not,” said Elfrida untruly, and sniffed with some intensity.

===Chinese===
这幅凄惨的图景把埃尔弗里达说化了，化成了眼泪，化得连话都说不出来。

「我们什么忙也帮不上，」埃德雷德又说了一遍，「看在老天份上，别那样抽抽搭搭的，埃尔弗里达。这是男人家的事。别哭了。你哭成那样，我都没法想事情。」

「我没哭，」埃尔弗里达言不由衷地说，用力吸了吸鼻子。

===Original===
“If you could make up some poetry now,” Edred went on, “would that be any good?”

“Not without the dresses,” she sniffed. “You know we always had dresses for our magic, or nearly always; and they have to be dead and gone people’s dresses, and you’ll only go to the dead and gone people’s time when the dresses were worn. Oh! dear Dickie, and if he’s really down a mine, or things like that, what’s the good of anything?”

“I’m going to try, anyway,” said Edred, “at least you must too. Because I can’t make poetry.”

===Chinese===
「你要是这会儿能编出几句诗来，」埃德雷德接着说，「那管用吗？」

「没有衣裳就不管用，」她抽着鼻子说。「你知道的，我们施魔法向来得换衣裳，差不多回回都得换；而且必须是早已作古的人穿过的衣裳，你也只会去到那些衣裳当年穿的那个久远年代。唉！亲爱的迪基，他要是真掉在矿井里，或者遇上诸如此类的事，那还有什么法子管用呢？」

「反正我要试一试，」埃德雷德说，「至少你也得试。因为我作不出诗来。」

===Original===
“No more can I when I’m as unhappy as this. Poetry’s the last thing you think of when you’re mizzy.”

“We could dress up, anyway,” said Edred hopefully. “The bits of armor out of the hall, and the Indian feather headdresses father brought home, and I have father’s shooting-gaiters and brown paper tops, and you can have Aunt Edith’s Roman sash. It’s in the right-hand corner drawer. I saw it on the wedding day when I went to get her prayerbook.”

“I don’t want to dress up,” said Elfrida; “I want to find Dickie.”

===Chinese===
「我难过成这样，也作不出来。人愁惨到这个地步，脑子里哪还想得起诗来。」

「至少我们可以穿上行头装扮起来，」埃德雷德满怀希望地说。「用大厅里那几件盔甲，还有爸爸带回来的印第安羽毛头饰。我有爸爸的打猎护腿，再配上牛皮纸做的靴筒；伊迪丝阿姨那条罗马式腰带你可以用，就在右手边那个角上的抽屉里。婚礼那天我去替她拿祈祷书，看见过它。」

「我不想装扮，」埃尔弗里达说，「我要找到迪基。」

===Original===
“I don’t want to dress up either,” said Edred; “but we must do something, and perhaps, I know it’s just only perhaps, it might help if we dressed up. Let’s try it, anyway.”

Elfrida was too miserable to argue. Before long two most miserable children faced each other in Edred’s bedroom, dressed as Red Indians so far as their heads and backs went. Then came lots of plate armor for chest and arms; then, in the case of Elfrida, petticoats and Roman sash and Japanese wickerwork shoes and father’s shooting-gaiters made to look like boots by brown paper tops. And in the case of Edred, legs cased in armor that looked like cricket pads, ending in jointed foot-coverings that looked like chrysalises. (I am told the correct plural is chrysalides, but life would be dull indeed if one always used the correct plural.) They were two forlorn faces that looked at each other as Edred said—

“Now the poetry.”

===Chinese===
「我也不想装扮，」埃德雷德说，「可我们总得做点什么。也许——我知道只是也许——装扮起来兴许能有点帮助。反正咱们试试吧。」

埃尔弗里达难过得连争辩的力气都没有了。没多久，两个凄凄惨惨的孩子就在埃德雷德的卧室里面对面站着，脑袋和脊背倒装扮得像红印第安人；胸前和胳膊上再套上一大堆板甲；到了下半身，埃尔弗里达是衬裙加罗马式腰带，脚上是日本藤编鞋，外面套着爸爸的打猎护腿，再用牛皮纸做了个靴筒，看上去就像长靴；埃德雷德呢，腿上裹着像板球护膝的甲片，末端的护脚是带节的，看上去活像虫蛹。（有人告诉我，chrysalis 的复数正确写法该是 chrysalides，可是一个人若凡事都用正确的复数形式，生活未免太乏味了。）两张孤零零的小脸面面相觑，这时埃德雷德开口说——

「现在作诗。」

===Original===
“I can’t,” said Elfrida, bursting into tears again; “I *can’t*! So there. I’ve been trying all the time we’ve been dressing, and I can only think of—

> “Oh, call dear Dickie back to me,

 I cannot play alone;

 The summer comes with flower and bee,

 Where is dear Dickie gone?

And I know that’s no use.”

===Chinese===
「我作不出，」埃尔弗里达说着又哭了起来。「我*作不出*！就是这样嘛。装扮的这工夫我一直在想，可我脑子里只有——

> 「哦，把亲爱的迪基唤回来，

我一个人玩不下去；

夏天带着花儿和蜜蜂来了，

亲爱的迪基去了哪里？

我知道这根本没用。」

===Original===
“I should think not,” said Edred. “Why, it isn’t your own poetry at all. It’s Felicia M. Hemans’. I’ll try.” And he got a pencil and paper and try he did, his very hardest, be sure. But there are some things that the best and bravest cannot do. And the thing Edred couldn’t do was to make poetry, however bad. He simply couldn’t do it, any more than you can fly. It wasn’t in him, any more than wings are on you.

> “Oh, Mouldiwarp, you said we must

 Not have any more magic. But we trust

 You won’t be hard on us, because Dickie is lost

 And we don’t know how to find him.”

===Chinese===
「我看也是没用，」埃德雷德说。「这压根儿不是你自己作的诗，这是费利西亚·M·赫曼斯（Felicia M. Hemans）的。我来试试。」他拿起铅笔和纸，认认真真地试了——相信我，他使出了浑身的劲。可有些事情，再出色、再勇敢的人也做不到。埃德雷德做不到的那件事，就是作诗，哪怕作得再糟也不行。他就是作不出来，就像你不会飞一样。这份本事不在他身上，就像你背上没有长翅膀一样。

> 「哦，莫德瓦普，你说过我们

不可再施展魔法。可我们相信

你不会为难我们，因为迪基失踪了，

我们不知道该去哪里找他。」

===Original===
That was the best Edred could do, and I tell it to his credit, he really did feel doubtful whether what he had so slowly and carefully written was indeed genuine poetry. So much so, that he would not show it to Elfrida until she had begged very hard indeed. At about the thirtieth “Do, please! Edred, do!” he gave her the paper. No little girl was ever more polite than Elfrida or less anxious to hurt the feelings of others. But she was also quite truthful, and when Edred said in an ashamed, muffled voice, “Is it all right, do you think?” the best she could find by way of answer was, “I don’t know much about poetry. We’ll try it.”

And they did try it, and nothing happened.

“I knew it was no good,” Edred said crossly; “and I’ve made an ass of myself for nothing.”

===Chinese===
这是埃德雷德所能作出的全部了。说句公道话，他心里着实犯嘀咕，不知道自己费了半天劲、仔仔细细写下的这东西，究竟算不算真正的诗。他疑虑到这种地步：埃尔弗里达不苦苦哀求，他就不肯给她看。哀求到差不多第三十声「给我看看嘛！埃德雷德，给我看看！」时，他才把纸递了过去。没有哪个小姑娘比埃尔弗里达更懂礼貌，更不愿意伤别人的心。可她也十分诚实，当埃德雷德羞愧地闷声闷气问「你觉得还行吗？」时，她能想出的最好的回答是：「我对诗懂得不多。咱们试试吧。」

他们试了，什么也没发生。

「我就知道没用，」埃德雷德气恼地说，「白白出了回洋相。」

===Original===
“Well, I’ve often made one of myself,” said Elfrida comfortingly, “and I will again if you like. But I don’t suppose it’ll be any more good than yours.”

Elfrida frowned fiercely and the feathers on her Indian headdress quivered with the intensity of her effort.

“Is it coming?” Edred asked in anxious tones, and she nodded distractedly.

===Chinese===
「唉，我常出洋相，」埃尔弗里达安慰他说，「你要愿意，我还可以再出一回。不过我猜也不会比你的那首更管用。」

埃尔弗里达狠狠地皱起眉头，头上的印第安羽毛头饰随着她使劲的劲儿直颤。

「来了吗？」埃德雷德焦灼地问，她心不在焉地点了点头。

===Original===
> “Great Mouldiestwarp, on you we call

 To do the greatest magic of all;

 To show us how we are to find

 Dear Dickie who is lame and kind.

 Do this for us, and on our hearts we swore

 We’ll never ask you for anything more.”

“I don’t see that it’s so much better than mine,” said Edred, “and it ought to be *swear*, not *swore*.”

“I don’t think it is. But you didn’t finish yours. And it couldn’t be ‘swear,’ because of rhyming,” Elfrida explained. “But I’m sure if the Mouldiestwarp hears it he won’t care tuppence whether it’s swear or swore. He is much too great. He’s far above grammar, I’m sure.”

“I wish everyone was,” sighed Edred, and I dare say you have often felt the same.

===Chinese===
> 「伟大的莫迪斯特瓦普（Mouldiestwarp），我们向你呼求，

请施展一切魔法中最伟大的魔法；

指点我们怎样才能找到

亲爱的迪基，他跛足又心好。

请为我们做成这件事，我们指心发过誓，

往后再也不向你求第二件事。」

「我看也不见得比我的高明多少，」埃德雷德说，「而且这里该用 *swear*，不该用 *swore*。」

「我也觉得不见得。可你的还没作完呢。而且这儿没法用 swear，得押韵呀，」埃尔弗里达解释说。「不过我敢说，莫迪斯特瓦普听了才不会在乎 swear 还是 swore 呢。他太伟大了。我敢肯定，语法这类东西他老人家根本不放在话下。」

「但愿人人都像他这样才好，」埃德雷德叹了口气。我敢说，你也常常有同感吧。

===Original===
“Well, fire away! Not that it’s any good. Don’t you remember you can only get at the Mouldiestwarp by a noble deed? And wanting to find Dickie isn’t noble.”

“No,” she agreed; “but then if we could get Dickie back by doing a noble deed we’d do it like a shot, wouldn’t we?”

“Oh! I suppose so,” said Edred grumpily; “fire away, can’t you?”

Elfrida fired away, and the next moment it was plain that Elfrida’s poetry was more potent than Edred’s; also that a little bad grammar is a trifle to a mighty Mouldiwarp.

===Chinese===
「好啦，念吧！虽说念了也白搭。你不记得吗？只有立下一桩高尚的功绩，才能见到莫迪斯特瓦普。而想找到迪基，这可算不上高尚。」

「是不算高尚，」她同意道，「可话又说回来，要是能靠立一桩高尚的功绩把迪基找回来，我们会一口气就答应下来，对不对？」

「哦！我想是的，」埃德雷德没好气地说，「快念吧，行不行？」

埃尔弗里达念了起来。下一刻事情就明明白白了：埃尔弗里达的诗比埃德雷德的灵验得多；也证明了一点，对一位法力无边的莫德瓦普来说，小小的语法错误不过区区小事。

===Original===
For the walls of Edred’s room receded further and further, till the children found themselves in a great white hall with avenues of tall pillars stretching in every direction as far as you could see. The hall was crowded with people dressed in costumes of all countries and all ages—Chinamen, Indians, Crusaders in armor, powdered ladies, doubleted gentlemen, Cavaliers in curls, Turks in turbans, Arabs, monks, abbesses, jesters, grandees with ruffs round their necks, and savages with kilts of thatch. Every kind of dress you can think of was there. Only all the dresses were white. It was like a *redoute*, which is a fancy-dress ball where the guests may wear any dress they choose, only all the dresses must be of one color.

Elfrida saw the whiteness all about her and looked down anxiously at her clothes and Edred’s, which she remembered to have been of rather odd colors. Everything they wore was white now. Even the Roman sash, instead of having stripes blue and red and green and black and yellow, was of five different shades of white. If you think there are not so many shades of white, try to paper a room with white paper and get it at five different shops.

===Chinese===
因为埃德雷德房间的四壁越退越远，退呀退呀，孩子们发觉自己已经站在一座雪白的大厅里，一排排高大的柱子形成的通道朝四面八方伸展开去，一眼望不到头。大厅里挤满了人，穿着世界各国、各个时代的服装——有中国人、印第安人、披甲的十字军、扑了发粉的贵妇人、穿紧身上衣的绅士、戴着卷发的骑士党人、缠头巾的土耳其人、阿拉伯人、修士、女修道院院长、弄臣、颈上围轮状皱领的大贵族，还有腰围茅草短裙的野人。你能想到的服装样样都有。只是一律都是白的。这就像一场化装舞会（法文叫 *redoute*），宾客想穿什么就穿什么，只是所有的衣裳都必须是同一种颜色。

埃尔弗里达看见周围一片白色，焦急地低头看看自己的衣裳和埃德雷德的——她记得那原是几种挺古怪的颜色。如今他们身上穿的一切都变白了。就连那条罗马式腰带，也不再是蓝、红、绿、黑、黄五色条纹，而是五种深浅不同的白。你要是不信白色能有这么多种深浅，拿白纸去糊一间屋子试试，记得到五家不同的铺子去买纸。

===Original===
The people round the children pushed them gently forward. And then they saw that in the middle of the hall was a throne of silver, spread with a fringed cloth of checkered silver and green, and on it, with the Mouldiwarp standing on one side and the Mouldierwarp on the other, the Mouldiestwarp was seated in state and splendor. He was much larger than either of the other moles, and his fur was as silvery as the feathers of a swan.

Everyone in the room was looking at the two children, and it seemed impossible for them not to advance, though slowly and shyly, right to the front of the throne.

Arrived there, it seemed right to bow, very low. So they did it.

Then the Mouldiwarp said—

“What brings you here?”

“Kind magic,” Elfrida answered.

===Chinese===
周围的人轻轻把两个孩子往前推。这时他们看见大厅正中有一座白银宝座，铺着带流苏的银绿格子座布；莫德瓦普立在宝座一边，莫迪尔瓦普（Mouldierwarp）立在另一边，莫迪斯特瓦普端坐在正中，一派威仪堂堂。他比另外两只鼹鼠都要大得多，皮毛银亮，如同天鹅的羽毛。

厅里的每一个人都在望着这两个孩子，他们似乎不由自主地向前走去，虽然又慢又怯，一直走到宝座跟前。

到了那儿，似乎就该深深地行礼，弯得很低很低。于是他们就照做了。

这时莫德瓦普说——

「是什么把你们带到这里来的？」

「善良的魔法。」埃尔弗里达回答。

===Original===
And the Mouldierwarp said—

“What is your desire?”

And Edred said, “We want Dickie, please.”

Then the Mouldiestwarp said, and it was to Edred that he said it—

“Dickie is in the hands of those who will keep him from you for many a day unless you yourself go, alone, and rescue him. It will be difficult, and it will be dangerous. Will you go?”

“Me? Alone?” said Edred rather blankly. “Not Elfrida?”

“Dickie can only be ransomed at a great price, and it must be paid by you. It will cost you more to do it than it would cost Elfrida, because she is braver than you are.”

===Chinese===
莫迪尔瓦普说——

「你们想求什么？」

埃德雷德说：「我们想要迪基回来，劳驾了。」

这时莫迪斯特瓦普开了口，他这话是对着埃德雷德说的——

「迪基落在一些人手里，除非你亲自一个人去救他，否则他们会长久地不让他回到你们身边。这一去会很艰难，也很危险。你去吗？」

「我？一个人去？」埃德雷德有些发愣地问。「不是埃尔弗里达？」

「赎回迪基要付出极高的代价，而这份代价必须由你来付。你去做这件事，付出的会比埃尔弗里达多，因为她比你勇敢。」

===Original===
Here was a nice thing for a boy to have said to him, and before all these people too! To ask a chap to do a noble deed and in the same breath to tell him he is a coward!

Edred flushed crimson, and a shudder ran through the company.

“Don’t turn that horrible color,” whispered a white toreador who was close to him. “This is the *white* world. No crimson allowed.”

Elfrida caught Edred’s hand.

“Edred is quite as brave as me,” she said. “He’ll go. Won’t you?”

“Of course I will,” said Edred impatiently.

“Then ascend the steps of the throne,” said the Mouldiestwarp, very kindly now, “and sit here by my side.”

===Chinese===
一个男孩子听了这话该多不是滋味，何况还是当着这么多人的面说的！让人去立高尚的功绩，可紧接着又说他是个胆小鬼！

埃德雷德的脸涨得通红，满厅的人都打了个寒噤。

「快别变成那种可怕的颜色，」挨着他的一位白衣斗牛士悄声说。「这是*白色*的世界。不许有猩红色。」

埃尔弗里达一把抓住埃德雷德的手。

「埃德雷德跟我一样勇敢，」她说。「他会去的。对不对？」

「我当然去，」埃德雷德不耐烦地说。

「那就登上宝座的台阶，」莫迪斯特瓦普说，语气现在十分和蔼，「坐到我身边来。」

===Original===
Edred obeyed, and the Mouldiestwarp leaned towards him and spoke in his ear.

So that neither Elfrida nor any of the great company in the White Hall could hear a word, only Edred alone.

“If you go to rescue Richard Arden,” the Mouldiestwarp said, “you make the greatest sacrifice of your life. For he who was called Richard Harding is Richard Arden, and it is he who is Lord Arden and not you or your father. And if you go to his rescue you will be taking from your father the title and the Castle, and you will be giving up your place as heir of Arden to your cousin Richard who is the rightful heir.”

“But how is he the rightful heir?” Edred asked, bewildered.

===Chinese===
埃德雷德遵命照办，莫迪斯特瓦普便朝他俯过身来，对着他的耳朵说话。

因此，无论是埃尔弗里达，还是白厅里的满厅宾客，一个字也听不见，只有埃德雷德一个人听得见。

「你若去救理查德·阿登，」莫迪斯特瓦普说，「你就是在做出你一生最大的牺牲。因为那个名叫理查德·哈丁的人，其实就是理查德·阿登；阿登勋爵是他，而不是你，也不是你的父亲。你若去救他，就是把爵位和城堡从你父亲手里拿走，就是把你作为阿登继承人的位置让给你的堂兄理查德——他才是名正言顺的继承人。」

「可他怎么会是名正言顺的继承人呢？」埃德雷德摸不着头脑地问。

===Original===
“Three generations ago,” said the Mouldiestwarp, “a little baby was stolen from Arden. Death came among the Ardens and that child became the heir to the name and the lands of Arden. The man who stole the child took it to a woman in Deptford, and gave it in charge to her to nurse. She knew nothing but that the child’s clothes were marked Arden, and that it had, tied to its waist, a coral and bells engraved with a coat of arms. The man who had stolen the child said he would return in a month. He never returned. He fought in a duel and was killed. But the night before the duel he wrote a letter saying what he had done and put it in a secret cupboard behind a picture of a lady who was born an Arden, at Talbot Court. And there that letter is to this day.”

“I hope I shan’t forget it all,” said Edred.

“None ever forgets what I tell them,” said the Mouldiestwarp. “Finding that the man did not return, the Deptford woman brought up the child as her own. He grew up, was taught a trade and married a working girl. The name of Arden changed itself, as names do, to Harding. Their child was the father of Richard whom you know. And he is Lord Arden.”

===Chinese===
「三代人以前，」莫迪斯特瓦普说，「一个婴儿从阿登家被偷走了。死神降临到阿登家，那孩子便成了阿登姓氏和领地的继承人。偷孩子的那个人把他带到德普特福德（Deptford）一个女人那里，托付她哺养。她只知道孩子衣裳上绣着阿登的姓氏，腰上系着一副刻着纹章的珊瑚铃铛。偷孩子的那个人说他一个月内回来，可他再也没有回来。他去决斗，送了命。不过在决斗的前一夜，他写下一封信，说明了自己做过的事，把它藏进塔尔博特府（Talbot Court）一幅画像后面的暗橱里，那画像上是一位出身阿登家的小姐。直到今天，那封信还放在那儿。」

「但愿我别把这些忘了，」埃德雷德说。

「听过我说话的，没有一个会忘，」莫迪斯特瓦普说。「那德普特福德的女人见那人不回来，就把孩子当自己的养大了。孩子长大，学了门手艺，娶了个做工的姑娘。阿登这个姓氏像姓氏常有的那样，讹变成了哈丁。他们的孩子，就是你认识的理查德的父亲。而他，就是阿登勋爵。」

===Original===
“Yes,” said Edred submissively.

“You will never tell your father this,” the low, beautiful voice went on; “you must not even tell your sister till you have rescued Dickie and made the sacrifice. This is the one supreme chance of all your life. Every soul has one such chance, a chance to be perfectly unselfish, absolutely noble and true. You can take this chance. But you must take it alone. No one can help you. No one can advise you. And you must keep the nobler thought in your own heart till it is a noble deed. Then, humbly and thankfully in that you have been permitted to do so fine and brave a thing and to draw near to the immortals of all ages who have such deeds to do and have done them, you may tell the truth to the one who loves you best, your sister Elfrida.”

“But isn’t Elfrida to have a chance to be noble too?” Edred asked.

===Chinese===
「是。」埃德雷德低声下气地应道。

「这件事你永远不能告诉你的父亲，」那低沉而美妙的声音继续说。「在救出迪基、做出牺牲之前，连你的妹妹也不能告诉。这是你一生仅有的一次至高无上的机会。每一个灵魂都有这样一次机会——一次做到大公无私、绝对高尚和真实的机会。你可以抓住这次机会，但你必须独自去抓。没有人能帮你，没有人能给你出主意。你必须把这高尚的念头藏在自己心里，直到它变成一桩高尚的功绩。到那时，你怀着谦卑与感恩——感激上天容许你做成这样一件美好而勇敢的事，容许你亲近古往今来那些负有并完成了这类功绩的不朽者——才可以把真话告诉你最爱你的人，你的妹妹埃尔弗里达。」

「可是埃尔弗里达难道就没有机会显得高尚了吗？」埃德雷德问。

===Original===
“She will have a thousand chances to be good and noble. And she will take them all. But she will never know that she has done it,” said the Mouldiestwarp gravely. “Now—are you ready to do what is to be done?”

“It seems very unkind to daddy,” said Edred, “stopping his being Lord Arden and everything.”

“To do right often seems unkind to one or another,” said the Mouldiestwarp, “but think. How long would your father wish to keep his house and his castle if he knew that they belonged to someone else?”

“I see,” said Edred, still doubtfully. “No, of course he wouldn’t. Well, what am I to do?”

===Chinese===
「她会有千百次机会去行善、去高尚，而且她一次也不会放过。只是她永远不知道自己做到了。」莫迪斯特瓦普严肃地说。「现在——你准备好去做那件该做的事了吗？」

「这对爸爸太狠心了，」埃德雷德说，「不让他当阿登勋爵，什么都不让他当。」

「做正确的事，常常会显得对这个人或那个人狠心，」莫迪斯特瓦普说，「可是想一想：你父亲若是知道那宅子和城堡原属于别人，他还愿意把它们占下去多久呢？」

「我明白了，」埃德雷德说，还是将信将疑。「是啊，他当然不愿意。那么，我该做什么呢？」

===Original===
“When Dickie’s father died, a Deptford woman related to Dickie’s mother kept the child. She was not kind to him. And he left her. Later she met a man who had been a burglar. He had entered Talbot Court, opened a panel, and found that old letter that told of Dickie’s birth. He and she have kidnapped Dickie, hoping to get him to sign a paper promising to pay them money for giving him the letter which tells how he is heir to Arden. But already they have found out that a letter signed by a child is useless and unlawful. And they dare not let Richard go for fear of punishment. So, if you choose to do nothing your father is safe and you will inherit Arden.”

“What am I to do?” Edred asked again—“to get Dickie back, I mean.”

“You must go alone and at night to Beale’s cottage, open the door and you will find Richard’s dog asleep before the fire. You must unchain the dog and take him to the milestone by the crossroads. Then go where the dog goes. You will need a knife to cut cords with. And you will need all your courage. Look in my eyes.”

===Chinese===
「迪基的父亲死后，一个跟迪基母亲沾亲的德普特福德女人收养了这孩子。她待他不好，孩子就离开了她。后来她遇上了一个人，那人从前做过贼。他进过塔尔博特府，打开一块护壁暗板，发现了那封讲明迪基身世的老信。他和她绑走了迪基，想逼他在一张字据上签字，答应付给他们一笔钱，换那封写明他是阿登继承人的信。可是他们已经发现，小孩子签的字既无用又不合法。他们又怕受惩罚，不敢放理查德走。所以，你若袖手不管，你父亲便安然无恙，阿登家业就归你继承。」

「那我该做什么？」埃德雷德又问——「我是说，怎样才能把迪基救回来。」

「你必须在夜里独自到比尔的小屋去，推开门，会看见理查德的狗睡在火炉前。你要解开狗链，把它带到岔路口的里程碑那儿。然后狗往哪儿走，你就往哪儿走。你要带一把刀子割绳索。你还需要用上你全部的勇气。看着我的眼睛。」

===Original===
Edred looked in the eyes of the Mouldiestwarp and saw that they were no longer a mole’s eyes but were like the eyes of all the dear people he had ever known, and through them the soul of all the brave people he had ever read about looked out at him and said, “Courage, Edred. Be one of us.”

“Now look at the people on the Hall,” said the Mouldiestwarp.

Edred looked. And behold, they were no longer strangers. He knew them all. Joan of Arc and Peter the Hermit, Hereward and Drake, Elsa whose brothers were swans, St. George who killed the dragon, Blondel who sang to his king in prison, Lady Nithsdale who brought her husband safe out of the cruel Tower. There were captains who went down with their ships, generals who died fighting for forlorn hopes, patriots, kings, nuns, monks, men, women, and children—all with that light in their eyes which brightens with splendor the dreams of men.

===Chinese===
埃德雷德望进莫迪斯特瓦普的眼睛，看见那不再是鼹鼠的眼睛，倒像他平生认识的每一个亲爱之人的眼睛；他读到过的所有勇敢者的灵魂，正从那双眼睛深处望着他，说：「拿出勇气来，埃德雷德。来做我们中间的一个吧。」

「再看看厅里的人。」莫迪斯特瓦普说。

埃德雷德望去。看哪，他们不再是陌生人了。他全都认得：圣女贞德（Joan of Arc）和隐修士彼得（Peter the Hermit），赫里沃德和德雷克（Drake），兄弟变成天鹅的艾尔莎（Elsa），屠龙的圣乔治（St. George），在狱中为国王歌唱的布隆代尔（Blondel），把丈夫从残酷的伦敦塔里安然救出的尼思代尔夫人（Lady Nithsdale）。还有与座舰同沉的舰长，为绝望的事业战死的将军；爱国者，君王，修女，修士，男人，女人，孩子——人人眼里都有一点光，正是这点光，把人类的梦照得灿烂生辉。

===Original===
And as he came down off the throne the great ones crowded round him, clasping his hand and saying—

“Be one of us, Edred. Be one of us.”

Then an intense white light shone so that the children could see nothing else. And then suddenly there they were again within the narrow walls of Edred’s bedroom.

“Well,” said Elfrida in tones of brisk commonplace, “what did it say to you? I say, you do look funny.”

“Don’t!” said Edred crossly. He began to tear off the armor. “Here, help me to get these things off.”

===Chinese===
他走下宝座时，那些伟大的人物都拥到他身边，紧握他的手，说着——

「来做我们中间的一个吧，埃德雷德。来做我们中间的一个吧。」

随后一道强烈的白光亮起，别的什么孩子们都看不见了。转眼之间，他们又站在了埃德雷德卧室那四堵窄墙之内。

「呃，」埃尔弗里达用轻快平常的口气说，「它跟你说什么了？我说，你瞧着可真滑稽。」

「别说了！」埃德雷德没好气地说。他动手扯那身盔甲。「来，帮我把这些东西卸下来。」

===Original===
“But what did it say?” Elfrida asked, helpfully.

“I can’t tell you. I’m not going to tell anyone till it’s over.”

“Oh, just as you like,” said Elfrida; “keep your old secrets,” and left him.

That was hard, wasn’t it?

“I can’t help it, I tell you. Oh! Elfrida, if *you’re* going to bother it’s just a little bit too much, that’s all.”

“You really mustn’t tell me?”

===Chinese===
「可它到底说什么了？」埃尔弗里达殷勤地问。

「我不能告诉你。事情办完之前，我谁也不告诉。」

「哦，随你的便，」埃尔弗里达说，「守着你的老秘密吧。」说完便撇下了他。

这一下可真叫人难受，是不是？

「我说了我没办法。唉！埃尔弗里达，你要是也要来缠我，那可就稍稍有点过了，就这样。」

「你真的什么也不能告诉我？」

===Original===
“I’ve told you so fifty times,” he said. Which was untrue. You know he had really only told her twice.

“Very well, then,” she said heroically, “I won’t ask you a single thing. But you’ll tell me the minute you can, won’t you? And you’ll let me help?”

“Nobody can help, no one can advise me,” Edred said. “I’ve got to do it off my own bat if I do it at all. Now you just shut up, I want to think.”

This unusual desire quite awed Elfrida. But it irritated her too.

“Perhaps you’d like me to go away,” she said ironically.

And Edred’s wholly unexpected reply was, “Yes, please.”

===Chinese===
「我跟你说过五十遍了，」他说。这话不实。你知道，他其实只对她说过两遍。

「那好，」她豪爽地说，「我什么都不问了。可你一能说就得告诉我，行不行？还有，你得让我帮忙。」

「没有人能帮忙，没有人能给我出主意，」埃德雷德说。「这件事要是非做不可，我就得全凭自己。好了，你先别说话，我要想想。」

这样不寻常的请求让埃尔弗里达大为惊异，可也惹恼了她。

「也许你巴不得我走开吧，」她话里带刺地说。

谁知埃德雷德全然出人意料的回答竟是：「好啊，劳驾。」

===Original===
So she went.

And when she was gone Edred sat down on the box at the foot of his bed and tried to think. But it was not easy.

“I ought to go,” he told himself.

“But think of your father,” said something else which was himself too.

He thought so hard that his thoughts got quite confused. His head grew very hot, and his hands and feet very cold. Mrs. Honeysett came in, exclaimed at his white face, felt his hands, said he was in a high fever, and put him to bed with wet rags on his forehead and hot-water bottles to his feet. Perhaps he was feverish. At any rate he could never be sure afterwards whether there really had been a very polite and plausible black mole sitting on his pillow most of the day saying all those things which the part of himself that he liked least agreed with. Such things as—

===Chinese===
于是她走了。

她走后，埃德雷德在床脚的木箱上坐下来，竭力去想。可想起来谈何容易。

「我应该去。」他对自己说。

「可你想想你的父亲。」另一个声音说，那声音也是他自己。

他苦想苦熬，思绪乱成了一团。他的头热得发烫，手脚却冰凉。哈尼塞特太太进来看见他脸色煞白，吓了一跳，摸了摸他的手，说他发高烧了，把他按到床上，额头敷上湿布，脚边放上热水袋。他也许真有点发烧。反正后来他永远拿不准，那天大半天里，是不是真有一只彬彬有礼、花言巧语的黑鼹鼠坐在他枕头上，说着那些话——他身上那个他最不喜欢的部分，对那些话句句赞同。诸如这样的话——

===Original===
“Think of your father.

“No one will ever know.

“Dickie will be all right somehow.

“Perhaps you only dreamed that about Dickie being shut up somewhere and it’s not true.

“Anyway, it’s not your business, is it?” And so on. You know the sort of thing.

Elfrida was not allowed to come into the room for fear Edred should be ill with something catching. So he lay tossing all day, hearing the black mole, or something else, say all these things and himself saying, “I must go.

“Oh! poor Dickie.

“I promised to go.

“Yes, I will go.”

===Chinese===
「想想你的父亲。

「谁也不会知道。

「迪基总会有办法平安无事的。

「说不定迪基被关在什么地方那件事只是你做的一场梦，根本不是真的。

「再说了，这本来就不关你的事，对不对？」诸如此类。你懂那种话的调调。

不许埃尔弗里达进房间，怕埃德雷德得的是传染病。他就这样翻来覆去躺了一整天，听着黑鼹鼠，或者别的什么，把这些话说个不停，而他自己则说：「我必须去。

「唉！可怜的迪基。

「我答应过要去。

「对，我要去。」

===Original===
And late that night when Lord Arden had come home and had gone to bed, tired out by a long day’s vain search for the lost Dickie, and when everybody was asleep, Edred got up and dressed. He put his bedroom candle and matches in his pocket, crept downstairs and out of the house and up to Beale’s. It was a slow and nervous business. More than once on the staircase he thought he heard a stair creak behind him, and again and again as he went along the road he fancied he heard a soft footstep pad-padding behind him, but of course when he looked round he could see no one was there. So presently he decided that it was cowardly to keep looking round, and besides, it only made him more frightened. So he kept steadily on and took no notice at all of a black patch by the sweetbrier bush by Beale’s cottage door just exactly as if someone was crouching in the shadow.

He pressed his thumb on the latch and opened the door very softly. Something moved inside and a chain rattled. Edred’s heart gave a soft, uncomfortable jump. But it was only True, standing up to receive company. He saw the whiteness of the dog and made for it, felt for the chain, unhooked it from the staple in the wall, and went out again, closing the door after him, and followed very willingly by True. Again he looked suspiciously at the shadow of the great sweetbrier, but the dog showed no uneasiness, so Edred knew that there was nothing to be afraid of. True, in fact, was the greatest comfort to him. He told Elfrida afterwards that it was all True’s doing; he could never, he was sure, have gone on without that good companion.

===Chinese===
那天深夜，阿登勋爵为寻找失踪的迪基白白奔波了一整天，回到家倒头睡了；等大家都睡熟了，埃德雷德爬起来穿好衣裳。他把卧室的蜡烛和火柴装进口袋，蹑手蹑脚下了楼，出了家门，朝比尔家走去。这一路走得又慢又揪心。楼梯上，他不止一次觉得身后有级楼梯吱呀响了一声；走在路上，他一次又一次仿佛听见身后有轻轻的脚步声嚓嚓作响；当然，他回头看时，一个人影也没有。于是他很快拿定主意：老回头张望是胆小鬼的行径，再说，那样只会叫自己更害怕。所以他只管稳稳地往前走，对比尔家门前那丛野蔷薇旁边的一团黑影毫不理会——那团黑影活像有人蹲伏在暗处。

他用拇指按住门闩，轻轻推开门。屋里有什么东西动了一下，铁链哗啦响。埃德雷德的心不自在地轻轻一跳。原来只是特拉斯蒂，站起来迎接客人呢。他看清了那团狗的白影，朝它走去，摸到铁链，从墙上的环钉上摘下来，又走出来随手带上门，特拉斯蒂心甘情愿地跟在后面。他又狐疑地看了看那丛大野蔷薇的影子，可狗一点不安的样子也没有，埃德雷德便知道没什么可怕的。说实话，特拉斯蒂是他最大的安慰。后来他告诉埃尔弗里达，这一切全亏了特拉斯蒂；他确信，若没有那位好伙伴，他绝走不下去。

===Original===
True followed at the slack chain’s end till they got to the milestone, and then suddenly he darted ahead and took the lead, the chain stretched taut, and the boy had all his work cut out to keep up with the dog. Up the hill they went on to the downs, and in and out among the furze bushes. The night was no longer dark to Edred. His eyes had got used to the gentle starlight, and he followed the dog among the gorse and brambles without stumbling and without hurting himself against the million sharp spears and thorns.

Suddenly True paused, sniffed, sneezed, blew through his nose and began to dig.

===Chinese===
特拉斯蒂松着链子在前面跟着，一直走到里程碑；到了那儿，它忽然蹿到前头领起路来，链子绷得笔直，小男孩得使足了劲才跟得上狗。他们上了山，走上丘陵地，在金雀花丛里钻进钻出。夜对埃德雷德来说不再黑了。他的眼睛已经适应了温柔的星光，跟着狗在荆棘和金雀花丛中穿行，没有跌一跤，也没有被千百万根尖矛和棘刺扎伤。

忽然，特拉斯蒂停住了，嗅了嗅，打了个喷嚏，鼻子呼哧呼哧地喷气，接着刨起土来。

===Original===
“Come on, come on, good dog,” said Edred, “come on, True,” for his fancy pictured Dickie a prisoner in some lonely cottage, and he longed to get to it and set him free and get safe back home with him. So he pulled at the chain. But True only shook himself and went on digging. The spot he had chosen was under a clump of furze bigger than any they had passed. The sharp furze-spikes pricked his nose and paws, but True was not the dog to be stopped by little things like that. He only stopped every now and then to sneeze and blow, and then went on digging.

Edred remembered the knife he had brought. It was the big pruning-knife out of the drawer in the hall. He pulled it out. He would cut away some of the furze branches. Perhaps Dickie was lying bound, hidden in the middle of the furze bush.

“Dickie,” he said softly, “Dickie.”

But no one answered. Only True sneezed and snuffed and blew and went on digging.

===Chinese===
「快点，快点，好狗，」埃德雷德说，「快点，特拉斯蒂。」他满脑子想的是迪基被囚在一座荒僻的村舍里，一心盼着赶到那儿救他出来，再平平安安地同他一起回家。于是他拽了拽链子。可特拉斯蒂只是抖了抖身子，接着刨。它挑中的地方在一丛金雀花底下，比他们一路经过的哪一丛都大。尖利的金雀花刺扎着它的鼻子和爪子，可特拉斯蒂不是那种会被这点小玩意儿拦住的狗。它只是时不时停下来打喷嚏、呼哧两声，然后接着刨。

埃德雷德想起了他带来的刀。那是大厅抽屉里的大修枝刀。他把刀抽出来，想砍掉几根金雀花的枝子。说不定迪基正被捆着，藏在金雀花丛当中呢。

「迪基，」他轻轻地喊，「迪基。」

可没有人应声。只有特拉斯蒂打着喷嚏、嗅着、喷着气，一个劲儿地刨。

===Original===
So then Edred took hold of a branch of furze to cut it, and it was loose and came away in his hand without any cutting. He tried another. That too was loose. He took off his jacket and threw it over his hands to protect them, and seizing an armful of furze pulled, and fell back, a great bundle of the prickly stuff on top of him. True was pulling like mad at the chain. Edred scrambled up; the furze he had pulled away disclosed a hole, and True was disappearing down it. Edred saw, as the dog dragged him close to the hole, that it was a large one, though only part of it had been uncovered. He stooped to peer in, his foot slipped on the edge, and he fell right into it, the dog dragging all the time.

“Stop, True; lie down, sir!” he said, and the dog paused, though the chain was still strained tight.

Then Edred was glad of his bedroom candle. He pulled it out and lighted it and blinked, perceiving almost at once that he was in the beginning of an underground passage. He looked up; he could see above him the stars plain through a net of furze bushes. He stood up and True went on. Next moment he knew that he was in the old smugglers’ cave that he and Elfrida had so often tried to find.

===Chinese===
于是埃德雷德抓住一根金雀花枝想砍断它，谁知那枝子是活的，没用刀就随手脱落下来。他又试一根，也是活的。他脱下外衣裹住两手护着，抱住一大捧金雀花用力一拽，整个人仰面摔倒，一大捆带刺的东西压在他身上。特拉斯蒂疯了似的拽着链子。埃德雷德爬起来；他拽掉的金雀花露出一个洞口，特拉斯蒂正往洞里钻。狗把他拖到洞口近旁时，他看清那是个大洞，虽然才扒开了一部分。他弯腰朝里张望，脚在洞边上滑了一下，整个人栽了进去——狗还在一个劲儿地拖。

「停下，特拉斯蒂；趴下，听话！」他说。狗停住了，虽然链子还绷得紧紧的。

这时埃德雷德庆幸自己带了卧室的蜡烛。他掏出来点上，眨着眼睛，几乎立刻就看出自己正处在一座地下通道的入口。他抬头望，头顶上能清清楚楚地看见星星，隔着一层金雀花织成的网。他站起身，特拉斯蒂继续往前走。转眼间他明白了，这就是那座走私贩子的老岩洞——他和埃尔弗里达找过多少回都没找到的那个。

===Original===
The dog and the boy went on, along a passage, down steps cut in the rock, through a rough, heavy door, and so into the smugglers’ cave itself, an enormous cavern as big as a church. Out of an opening at the upper end a stream of water fell, and ran along the cave clear between shores of smooth sand.

And, lying on the sand near the stream, was something dark.

True gave a bound that jerked the chain out of Edred’s hand, and leaped upon the dark thing, licking it, whining, and uttering little dog moans of pure love and joy. For the dark something was Dickie, fast asleep. He was bound with cords, his poor lame foot tied tight to the other one. His arms were bound too. And now he was awake.

“Down, True!” he said. “Hush! Ssh!”

“Where are they—the man and woman?” Edred whispered.

===Chinese===
狗和人继续往前走，穿过一条通道，走下凿在岩石里的台阶，经过一扇粗糙沉重的门，就这样进了走私岩洞本身——一个教堂那么大的巨大洞窟。洞窟高处的一个开口里泻下一股水流，沿着洞底流去，清凌凌的，两岸是平滑的沙。

而在水边不远的沙地上，躺着一个黑黑的东西。

特拉斯蒂猛地一蹿，链子从埃德雷德手里挣脱出去，它扑到那个黑黑的东西身上，又舔又呜咽，发出小狗满心挚爱与欢喜的细细哀鸣。原来那黑黑的东西就是迪基，睡得正香。他被绳索捆着，那只可怜的跛脚同另一只脚捆得紧紧的，两条胳膊也捆着。这会儿他醒了。

「趴下，特拉斯蒂！」他说。「别出声！嘘！」

「他们在哪儿——那一男一女？」埃德雷德悄声问。

===Original===
“Oh, Edred! You! You perfect brick!” Dickie whispered back. “They’re in the further cave. I heard them snoring before I went to sleep.”

“Lie still,” said Edred; “I’ve got a knife. I’ll cut the cords.”

He cut them, and Dickie tried to stand up. But his limbs were too stiff. Edred rubbed his legs, while Dickie stretched his fingers to get the pins and needles out of his arms.

Edred had stuck the candle in the sand. It made a ring of light round them. That was why they did not see a dark figure that came quietly creeping across the sand towards them. It was quite close to them before Edred looked up.

“Oh!” he gasped, and Dickie, looking up, whispered, “It’s all up—*run*. Never mind me. I shall get away all right.”

===Chinese===
「哦，埃德雷德！是你！你真是个大好人！」迪基悄声答道。「他们在里面那个洞里。我睡觉前听见他们打呼噜来着。」

「躺着别动，」埃德雷德说，「我带了刀。我来割绳子。」

他割断了绳子，迪基想站起来，可四肢麻得撑不住。埃德雷德替他揉腿，迪基则伸张着手指，好把胳膊里的麻劲儿散一散。

埃德雷德把蜡烛插在沙里，烛光围着他们罩了一圈。正因如此，他们才没有看见一个黑影正悄悄爬着穿过沙地朝他们过来。等埃德雷德抬头，那黑影已经到了跟前。

「啊！」他倒抽一口气。迪基抬头一看，低声说：「全完了——*快跑*。别管我。我自己脱得了身。」

===Original===
“No,” said Edred, and then with a joyous leap of the heart perceived that the dark figure was Elfrida in her father’s ulster.

(“I hadn’t time to put on my stockings,” she explained later. “You’d have known me a mile off by my white legs if I hadn’t covered them up with this.”)

“Elfrida!” said both boys at once.

“Well, you didn’t think I was going to be out of it,” she said. “I’ve been behind you all the way, Edred. Don’t tell me anything. I won’t ask any questions, only come along out of it. Lean on me.”

===Chinese===
「不，」埃德雷德说。随即他心头一喜，认出那黑影是埃尔弗里达，裹着她父亲的长外套。

（「我来不及穿长袜，」她后来解释说。「我腿白，要不用这个盖住，隔一英里远你们也认得出是我。」）

「埃尔弗里达！」两个男孩不约而同地喊。

「怎么，你以为我会置身事外吗？」她说。「一路上我都在你后头跟着呢，埃德雷德。什么都别跟我说，我什么也不问，只管咱们一块儿离开这儿。靠着我。」

===Original===
They got him up to the passage, one on each side, and by that time Dickie could use his legs and his crutch. They got home and roused Lord Arden, and told him Dickie was found and all about it, and he roused the house, and he and Beale and half-a-dozen men from the village went up to the cave and found that wicked man and woman in a stupid sleep, and tied their hands and marched them to the town and to the police-station.

When the man was searched the letter was found on him which the man—it was that redheaded man you have heard of—had taken from Talbot Court.

“I wish you joy of your good fortune, my boy,” said Lord Arden when he had read the letter. “Of course we must look into things, but I feel no doubt at all that you *are* Lord Arden!”

===Chinese===
他们一边一个架着他走到通道里，那时迪基已经能用自己的腿、拄自己的拐了。他们回到家，叫醒了阿登勋爵，告诉他迪基找到了，又把前后经过讲了一遍。阿登勋爵唤起全家，和比尔带上村里的六七个人赶到岩洞，发现那对作恶的男女正酣睡不醒，便捆了他们的手，押着他们进城，送到警察局去。

搜那男人的身时，从他身上搜出了那封信——就是你们听说过的那个红头发男人从塔尔博特府偷走的那封。

「恭喜你交上好运，我的孩子，」阿登勋爵读完信说。「自然，事情还得细查，可我丝毫也不怀疑，你*就是*阿登勋爵！」

===Original===
“I don’t want to be,” said Dickie, and that was true. Yet at the same time he did want to be. The thought of being Richard, Lord Arden, he who had been just little lame Dickie of Deptford, of owning this glorious castle, of being the master of an old name and an old place, this thought sang in his heart a very beautiful tune. Yet what he said was true. There is so often room in our hearts for two tunes at a time. “I don’t want to be. You ought to be, sir. You’ve been so kind to me,” he said.

“My dear boy,” said the father of Edred and Elfrida, “I did very well without the title and the castle, and if they’re yours I shall do very well without them again. You shall have your rights, my dear boy, and I shan’t be hurt by it. Don’t you think that.”

Dickie thought several things and shook the other’s hand very hard.

The tale of Dickie’s rescue from the cave was the talk of the countryside. True was praised much, but Edred more. Why had no one else thought of putting the dog on the scent? Edred said that it was mostly True’s doing. And the people praised his modesty. And nobody, except perhaps Elfrida, ever understood what it had cost Edred to go that night through the dark and rescue his cousin.

===Chinese===
「我不想当，」迪基说，这话是真的。可与此同时，他又确实想当。想到自己——从前不过是德普特福德那个瘸腿的小迪基——将要成为理查德，阿登勋爵，将要拥有这座辉煌的城堡，成为一个古老姓氏、一方古老宅地的主人，这念头在他心里唱着一支十分动听的曲子。然而他说的话也是真的。我们的心里往往容得下两支曲子同时唱。「我不想当。该您当，先生。您待我一直那么好，」他说。

「我亲爱的孩子，」埃德雷德和埃尔弗里达的父亲说，「没有头衔和城堡，我日子过得一直很好；它们若是你的，我没了它们照样过得很好。你的权利就该归你，我亲爱的孩子，我不会为这个伤心的。你可别这么想。」

迪基心里转了好些念头，紧紧地握了握对方的手。

迪基从岩洞获救的故事成了乡里乡外的谈资。特拉斯蒂受尽称赞，埃德雷德受的称赞更多。怎么旁人就没想到放狗去循味追踪呢？埃德雷德说，这多半是特拉斯蒂的功劳。人们于是又夸他谦逊。可是那一夜，埃德雷德穿行在黑暗里去救他的堂兄，这要付出多大的代价——除了也许埃尔弗里达，谁也不明白。

===Original===
Edred’s father and Mrs. Honeysett agreed that Edred had done it in the delirium of a fever, brought on by his anxiety about his friend and playmate. People do, you know, do odd things in fevers that they would never do at other times.

The redheaded man and the woman were tried at the assizes and punished. If you ask me how they knew about the caves which none of the country people seemed to know of, I can only answer that I don’t know. Only I know that everyone you know knows lots of things that you don’t know they know.

When they all went a week later to explore the caves, they found a curious arrangement of brickwork and cement and clay, shutting up a hole through which the stream had evidently once flowed out into the open air. It now flowed away into darkness. Lord Arden pointed out how its course had been diverted and made to run down underground to the sea.

“We might let it come back to the moat,” said Edred. “It used to run that way. It says so in the *History of Arden*.”

“We must decide that later,” said his father, who had a long blue lawyer’s letter in his pocket.

===Chinese===
埃德雷德的父亲和哈尼塞特太太都认为，埃德雷德是烧糊涂了才干出这事的——他惦记朋友和游伴，急出了那场烧。你知道，人发烧的时候，常会做出平时绝不会做的怪事来。

红头发男人和那女人经过巡回法庭审判，受到了惩处。你若问我，那些乡下人好像谁也不知道的岩洞，他们是怎么知道的，我只能回答：我不知道。我只知道，你认识的每一个人，都懂得许多你不知道他们懂得的事情。

一星期后，他们全体去探那几座岩洞，发现一处古怪的砌造，用砖、灰泥和黏土封住了一个洞口——那股水流显然曾从这里流出，去见天日。如今它流进黑暗里去了。阿登勋爵指出，水道曾被人改过，叫它转入地下，一直流向大海。

「咱们可以叫它流回护城河去，」埃德雷德说。「它从前就是打那儿流的。《阿登史》上是这么写的。」

「这事回头再定，」他父亲说，口袋里装着一封律师写的长长的蓝色信函。

## I Tinkler and the Moonflower / 第一章 叮铃与月光花

===Original===
Dickie lived at New Cross. At least the address was New Cross, but really the house where he lived was one of a row of horrid little houses built on the slope where once green fields ran down the hill to the river, and the old houses of the Deptford merchants stood stately in their pleasant gardens and fruitful orchards. All those good fields and happy gardens are built over now. It is as though some wicked giant had taken a big brush full of yellow ochre paint, and another full of mud color, and had painted out the green in streaks of dull yellow and filthy brown; and the brown is the roads and the yellow is the houses. Miles and miles and miles of them, and not a green thing to be seen except the cabbages in the greengrocers’ shops, and here and there some poor trails of creeping-jenny drooping from a dirty windowsill. There is a little yard at the back of each house; this is called “the garden,” and some of these show green﻿—but they only show it to the houses’ back windows. You cannot see it from the street. These gardens are green, because green is the color that most pleases and soothes men’s eyes; and however you may shut people up between bars of yellow and mud color, and however hard you may make them work, and however little wage you may pay them for working, there will always be found among those people some men who are willing to work a little longer, and for no wages at all, so that they may have green things growing near them.

But there were no green things growing in the garden at the back of the house where Dickie lived with his aunt. There were stones and bones, and bits of brick, and dirty old dishcloths matted together with grease and mud, worn-out broom-heads and broken shovels, a bottomless pail, and the mouldy remains of a hutch where once rabbits had lived. But that was a very long time ago, and Dickie had never seen the rabbits. A boy had brought a brown rabbit to school once, buttoned up inside his jacket, and he had let Dickie hold it in his hands for several minutes before the teacher detected its presence and shut it up in a locker till school should be over. So Dickie knew what rabbits were like. And he was fond of the hutch for the sake of what had once lived there.

And when his aunt sold the poor remains of the hutch to a man with a barrow who was ready to buy anything, and who took also the pails and the shovels, giving threepence for the lot, Dickie was almost as unhappy as though the hutch had really held a furry friend. And he hated the man who took the hutch away, all the more because there were empty rabbit-skins hanging sadly from the back of the barrow.

===Chinese===
迪基（Dickie）住在纽克罗斯（New Cross）。地址起码是纽克罗斯，可他真正住的那所房子，是一排恶心的小房子中的一座，盖在一面山坡上——想当年，青青的田野顺着山坡一路铺到河边，德普特福德（Deptford）商人们的老宅，端端正正立在宜人的花园和结满果子的果园里。那些好田野、好花园，如今统统盖了房子。就像某个坏心眼儿的巨人，拿一把蘸满赭黄颜料的大刷子，再拿另一把蘸满泥浆色的大刷子，把绿色一道道涂掉，涂成暗黄和脏褐；褐色是马路，黄色是房子。一英里又一英里，又一英里，满眼见不到一件绿东西，只有菜贩子铺子里的卷心菜，还有这儿那儿从脏窗台上垂下来的几绺可怜巴巴的过路黄。每所房子后头都有个小院子；人家管它叫「花园」，有些「花园」里确实见得着绿色——可只给房子的后窗看，从街上是瞧不见的。这些园子之所以绿，是因为绿色最能悦人眼、安人心；不论你把人们怎样关在黄色和泥浆色的栅栏中间，不论你叫他们做工做得多苦，也不论你付给他们的工钱多薄，那些人里头总找得出几个，肯再多干一会儿活，而且一个子儿工钱也不要，只为身边能有绿色的东西长着。

可是迪基同他婶婶住的那所房子，后院里并没有什么绿东西生长。院里有石头有骨头，有碎砖，有叫油泥烂泥粘成一团的脏抹布，有磨秃了的笤帚头和断了柄的铁锹，有一只没底的木桶，还有一只兔笼的霉烂残余——从前有过兔子住在那儿。可那已经是很久很久以前的事，迪基从来没见过那些兔子。有一回，一个男孩把一只棕色兔子揣在夹克里、扣上扣子带到学校来，让迪基捧在手心里捧了好几分钟，才叫老师发觉，把兔子锁进柜子，直到放了学。所以迪基知道兔子长什么样。他疼爱那只兔笼，是为了那曾在笼里住过的东西。

后来婶婶把兔笼那点可怜的残余，卖给了一个推小车收破烂的——那人来者不拒，什么都肯收，把木桶铁锹也一齐收走，统共给了三便士。迪基难过极了，仿佛笼里真住过一位毛茸茸的朋友。他恨那个把兔笼拉走的人，尤其因为小车后头，凄凄惨惨地挂着几张空兔皮。

===Original===
It is really with the going of that rabbit-hutch that this story begins. Because it was then that Dickie, having called his aunt a Beast, and hit at her with his little dirty fist, was well slapped and put out into the bereaved yard to “come to himself,” as his aunt said. He threw himself down on the ground and cried and wriggled with misery and pain, and wished﻿—ah, many things.

“Wot’s the bloomin’ row now?” the Man Next Door suddenly asked; “been hittin’ of you?”

“They’ve took away the ’utch,” said Dickie.

“Well, there warn’t nothin’ in it.”

“I diden want it took away,” wailed Dickie.

“Leaves more room,” said the Man Next Door, leaning on his spade. It was Saturday afternoon and the next-door garden was one of the green ones. There were small grubby daffodils in it, and dirty-faced little primroses, and an arbor beside the water-butt, bare at this time of the year, but still a real arbor. And an elder-tree that in the hot weather had flat, white flowers on it big as tea-plates. And a lilac-tree with brown buds on it. Beautiful. “Say, matey, just you chuck it! Chuck it, I say! How in thunder can I get on with my digging with you ’owlin’ yer ’ead off?” inquired the Man Next Door. “You get up and peg along in an’ arst your aunt if she’d be agreeable for me to do up her garden a bit. I could do it odd times. You’d like that.”

===Chinese===
说真的，这个故事，就是从那只兔笼的离去开始的。因为就在那时候，迪基骂了婶婶一声「畜生」，还挥起他那只脏兮兮的小拳头打她，于是挨了结结实实的耳光，被撵到那伤心的院子里去「醒一醒神儿」——这是婶婶的话。他扑通躺在地上，又哭又扭，满心的痛苦委屈，盼着——啊，盼着好多好多东西。

「又闹哪门子鬼呀？」隔壁的男人冷不丁问；「挨打啦？」

「他们把兔笼拉走啦，」迪基说。

「哼，那笼里本来也什么都没有。」

「我就不愿意叫它拉走，」迪基哭道。

「空出来倒宽敞，」隔壁的男人拄着铁锹说。那是星期六下午，隔壁的园子恰好是绿的那种。园里立着几株灰头土脸的小水仙，几朵脏脸蛋儿的小报春花，雨水桶旁边有一座凉亭——眼下光秃秃的，可到底是一座真凉亭。还有一棵接骨木，热天里开一嘟噜一嘟噜扁扁的白花，大得像茶碟。还有一棵丁香，缀着棕色的芽苞。真美。「我说，伙计，你给我打住！打住，听见没有！你嚎成这样，我这地还怎么挖哇？」隔壁的男人问。「你起来，撒开腿进屋去，问问你婶婶，肯不肯让我把她那园子拾掇拾掇。我抽空儿就能弄。你乐意吧。」

===Original===
“Not ’arf!” said Dickie, getting up.

“Come to yourself, eh?” sneered the aunt. “You mind, and let it be the last time you come your games with me, my beauty. You and your tantrums!”

Dickie said what it was necessary to say, and got back to the “garden.”

“She says she ain’t got no time to waste, an’ if you ’ave she don’t care what you does with it.”

===Chinese===
「那还用说！」迪基说着爬起来。

「醒过神儿来了，呃？」婶婶讥笑道。「你给我记着，往后再敢跟我耍这套把戏，就是末一回，我的小美人儿。瞧你那撒泼劲儿！」

迪基把该说的话说了，回到那「花园」里。

「她说她没工夫瞎耽误；她要是你有那工夫，你拿它干什么她也不管。」

===Original===
“There’s a dirty mug you’ve got on you,” said the Man Next Door, leaning over to give Dickie’s face a rub with a handkerchief hardly cleaner. “Now I’ll come over and make a start.” He threw his leg over the fence. “You just peg about an’ be busy pickin’ up all them fancy articles, and nex’ time your aunt goes to Buckingham Palace for the day we’ll have a bonfire.”

“Fifth o’ November?” said Dickie, sitting down and beginning to draw to himself the rubbish that covered the ground.

“Fifth of anything you like, so long as *she* ain’t about,” said he, driving in the spade. “ ’Ard as any old doorstep it is. Never mind, we’ll turn it over, and we’ll get some little seedses and some little plantses and we shan’t know ourselves.”

“I got a ’apenny,” said Dickie.

“Well, I’ll put one to it, and you leg ’long and buy seedses. That’s wot you do.”

===Chinese===
「瞧你那脏脸，活像个小泥碗，」隔壁的男人说着探过身来，拿一块手帕——那手帕也不见得干净多少——给迪基抹了把脸。「这就过来，先开个头。」他一抬腿跨过栅栏。「你就在这儿转悠，忙着把那些好宝贝拾掇到一块儿；下回你婶婶上白金汉宫玩一天去，咱们就生一堆大火。」

「十一月五号？」迪基说着坐下来，开始把满地的破烂往自己跟前扒拉。

「随便几号都行，只要*她*不在跟前，」那人说着一锹下去。「硬得跟哪家旧门口的石阶一样。不要紧，咱们把它翻个身，弄点儿小种子、小秧苗来种上，咱们就认不出这地方了。」

「我有一个半便士，」迪基说。

「好，我再添一个，你撒腿去买种子去。就这么办。」

===Original===
Dickie went. He went slowly, because he was lame. And he was lame because his “aunt” had dropped him when he was a baby. She was not a nice woman, and I am glad to say that she goes out of this story almost at once. But she did keep Dickie when his father died, and she might have sent him to the workhouse. For she was not really his aunt, but just the woman of the house where his father had lodged. It was good of her to keep Dickie, even if she wasn’t very kind to him. And as that is all the good I can find to say about her, I will say no more. With his little crutch, made out of a worn-out broom cut down to his little height, he could manage quite well in spite of his lameness.

He found the corn-chandler’s﻿—a really charming shop that smelled like stables and had deep dusty bins where he would have liked to play. Above the bins were delightful little square-fronted drawers, labelled Rape, Hemp, Canary, Millet, Mustard, and so on; and above the drawers pictures of the kind of animals that were fed on the kind of things that the shop sold. Fat, oblong cows that had eaten Burley’s Cattle Food, stout pillows of wool that Ovis’s Sheep Spice had fed, and, brightest and best of all, an incredibly smooth-plumaged parrot, rainbow-colored, cocking a black eye bright with the intoxicating qualities of Perrokett’s Artistic Bird Seed.

“Gimme,” said Dickie, leaning against the counter and pointing a grimy thumb at the wonder﻿—“gimme a penn’orth o’ that there!”

“Got the penny?” the shopman asked carefully.

Dickie displayed it, parted with it, and came home nursing a paper bag full of rustling promises.

===Chinese===
迪基去了。他走得慢，因为他瘸。他之所以瘸，是因为他还是个婴儿的时候，「婶婶」把他摔了。她不是个好人；我很高兴地说，她差不多马上就要从这个故事里退场了。可是迪基的爸爸死后，到底是她收留了迪基，要不然她本可以把他送进济贫院。因为她其实并不是他的婶婶，不过是他爸爸寄住那所房子里的女人。她肯收留迪基，这是她的好处，哪怕她待他并不怎么好。既然我能替她找出好处来的就只有这一条，我就再多说的没有了。拄着他那根小拐杖——一根磨秃了的旧笤帚柄，锯短到跟他小小的个头一般高——他虽然瘸，倒也对付得相当利索。

他找到了那家谷物饲料铺——真是家招人喜欢的铺子，一股马厩味儿，深深的木柜子里积着灰，他真想钻进去玩耍。柜子上头是一排惹人喜爱的小抽屉，方方的脸儿，标着：油菜籽、大麻籽、金丝雀草籽、黍子、芥子，等等；抽屉再上头，画着些牲口，正是吃这铺子里所卖的东西长大的那种牲口：肥墩墩、长条条的牛，那是吃了「伯利牌牛饲料」的；一团团厚墩墩、枕头似的羊毛，那是「奥维斯牌绵羊香料」喂出来的；而最鲜亮、最好的一张，是一只羽毛光滑得叫人不肯相信的鹦鹉，虹彩流溢，歪着一只亮闪闪的黑眼睛——那眼神的得意劲儿，分明是叫「佩罗凯特牌艺术鸟食」（Perrokett’s Artistic Bird Seed）的美妙功效灌醉的。

「给我来，」迪基倚着柜台，一只脏拇指直直指向那宝贝——「给我来一便士的那个！」

「带着一便士呢？」店伙计小心地问。

迪基把便士亮出来，交出去，回到家时怀里搂着一个纸袋，满袋窸窸窣窣的指望。

===Original===
“Why,” said the Man Next Door, “that ain’t seeds. It’s parrot food, that is.”

“It said the Ar-something Bird Seed,” said Dickie, downcast; “I thought it ’ud come into flowers like birds-same colors as wot the poll parrot was, dontcherknow?”

“And so it will like as not,” said the Man Next Door comfortably. “I’ll set it along this end soon’s I’ve got it turned over. I lay it’ll come up something pretty.”

So the seed was sown. And the Man Next Door promised two more pennies later for *real* seed. Also he transplanted two of the primroses whose faces wanted washing.

It was a grand day for Dickie. He told the whole story of it that night when he went to bed to his only confidant, from whom he hid nothing. The confidant made no reply, but Dickie was sure this was not because the confidant didn’t care about the story. The confidant was a blackened stick about five inches long, with little blackened bells to it like the bells on dogs’ collars. Also a rather crooked bit of something whitish and very hard, good to suck, or to stroke with your fingers, or to dig holes in the soap with. Dickie had no idea what it was. His father had given it to him in the hospital where Dickie was taken to say goodbye to him. Goodbye had to be said because of father having fallen off the scaffolding where he was at work and not getting better. “You stick to that,” father had said, looking dreadfully clean in the strange bed among all those other clean beds; “it’s yourn, your very own. My dad give it to me, and it belonged to his dad. Don’t you let anyone take it away. Some old lady told the old man it ’ud bring us luck. So long, old chap.”

===Chinese===
「咦，」隔壁的男人说，「这可不是种子。这是鹦鹉食，这是。」

「上头写的是『阿——什么——鸟食』来着，」迪基耷拉着脑袋说；「我当它开起花来，准跟鸟儿似的——跟那只大鹦鹉一个颜色，您知道不？」

「八成还真是这样，」隔壁的男人舒舒服服地说。「等我把这头翻好了，我马上把它种在这头儿。我保它长出个好看的东西来。」

种子就这样下了地。隔壁的男人还许下，往后买*正经*种子，再添两个便士。他还移栽了两棵报春花——那两张小脸，正该洗一洗了。

对迪基来说，这是了不起的一天。当天夜里上床，他把这一整天的故事，从头到尾讲给他唯一的知己听，什么事也不瞒着这位知己。知己没有答话，可迪基深信，这并不是因为知己不爱听这故事。这位知己是一根熏黑的小棍儿，约莫五英寸长，上头拴着些熏黑的小铃铛，跟狗项圈上的铃铛一个样。另外还有一段弯弯扭扭的白东西，又白又硬，可以含在嘴里吮，可以用手指头摩挲，还可以拿它在肥皂上钻窟窿。迪基全然不知道它是什么。那是他爸爸给他的，在迪基被带去同爸爸告别的那家医院里。告别是非说不可的了，因为爸爸从干活的脚手架上摔下来，再也不见好。「你把它攥住喽，」爸爸说，躺在那张陌生的床上，周围是别的那些干净的床，他显得干净得吓人；「它是你的，实实在在是你自个儿的。我爸爸给我，又是他爸爸传给他的。谁也别想从你手里拿走。有位老太太跟老头子说过，它能给咱们带来好运。再会吧，老伙计。」

===Original===
Dickie remembered every word of that speech, and he kept the treasure. There had been another thing with it, tied on with string. But Aunt Maud had found that, and taken it away “to take care of,” and he had never seen it again. It was brassy, with a white stone and some sort of pattern on it. He had the treasure, and he had not the least idea what it was, with its bells that jangled such pretty music, and its white spike so hard and smooth. He did not know﻿—but I know. It was a rattle-a baby’s old-fashioned rattle﻿—or, if you would rather call it that, a “coral and bells.”

“And we shall ’ave the fairest flowers of hill and dale,” said Dickie, whispering comfortably in his dirty sheets, “and greensward. Oh! Tinkler dear, ’twill indeed be a fair scene. The gayest colors of the rainbow amid the Ague Able green of fresh leaves. I do love the Man Next Door. He has indeed a ’art of gold.”

That was how Dickie talked to his friend Tinkler. You know how he talked to his aunt and the Man Next Door. I wonder whether you know that most children can speak at least two languages, even if they have never had a foreign nurse or been to foreign climes﻿—or whether you think that you are the only child who can do this.

Believe me, you are not. Parents and guardians would be surprised to learn that dear little Charlie has a language quite different from the one he uses to them﻿—a language in which he talks to the cook and the housemaid. And yet another language﻿—spoken with the real accent too﻿—in which he converses with the boot-boy and the grooms.

Dickie, however, had learned his second language from books. The teacher at his school had given him six﻿—*Children of the New Forest*, *Quentin Durward*, *Hereward the Wake*, and three others﻿—all paper-backed. They made a new world for Dickie. And since the people in books talked in this nice, if odd, way, he saw no reason why he should not﻿—to a friend whom he could trust.

===Chinese===
那段话，迪基一个字一个字全记得，宝贝他也一直留着。原本还有一样东西，用细绳跟它拴在一起。可莫德婶婶发现了，拿走「替他保管」去了，从此再没见过。那东西是黄铜色的，镶一块白石头，上头有些花纹。他留着这件宝贝，却压根儿不知道它是什么，只知道它的小铃铛摇起来是那样好听的音乐，那根白色的小尖锥又硬又滑。他不知道——可我知道。那是一只摇铃（rattle）——旧式的婴儿摇铃——或者说，你爱怎么叫就怎么叫，一副「珊瑚铃铛」。

「咱们还要有山间谷地顶美的花儿呢，」迪基躺在脏床单里，舒舒服服地悄声说，「还有绿茵茵的草地。哦！亲爱的叮铃（Tinkler），那真要成一片美景了。彩虹顶鲜亮的颜色，开在嫩叶那种『阿格留布尔』——也就是宜人——的绿色里。我真喜欢隔壁的男人。他真真有一颗金子心。」

迪基就是这样跟他的朋友叮铃说话的。他对婶婶和隔壁的男人怎么说话，你是知道的。可是大多数孩子至少会讲两种语言——哪怕他们从来没请过外国保姆，也没出过洋远门——这一点，你不知道吧？还是说，你以为天底下只有你一个孩子会这个？

相信我，你不是。要是爸爸妈妈们、监护人老爷们知道，他们亲爱的小查理还另有一套语言，跟对他们讲的那套大不相同——那是用来对厨娘和女佣说话的——准会大吃一惊。可他还有第三套语言，也是一口地道腔调，专门用来跟擦靴子的小厮和马夫们交谈。

不过，迪基的第二种语言是从书上学来的。学校里的老师送过他六本——*《新森林的孩子们》*、*《昆丁·达沃德》*、*《觉醒者赫里沃德》*，还有三本——全是纸皮封面的。它们给迪基开辟了一个新世界。既然书里的人物都这么说话——好听得紧，虽然怪里怪气——他觉得，他对一位信得过的朋友，也不妨这么说话。

===Original===
I hope you’re not getting bored with all this.

You see, I must tell you a little about the kind of boy Dickie was and the kind of way he lived, or you won’t understand his adventures. And he had adventures﻿—no end of adventures﻿—as you will see presently.

Dickie woke, gay as the spring sun that was trying to look in at him through his grimy windows.

“Perhaps he’ll do some more to the garden today!” he said, and got up very quickly.

He got up in the dirty, comfortless room and dressed himself. But in the evening he was undressed by kind, clean hands, and washed in a big bath half-full of hot, silvery water, with soap that smelled like the timber-yard at the end of the street. Because, going along to school, with his silly little head full of Artistic Bird Seeds and flowers rainbow-colored, he had let his crutch slip on a banana-skin and had tumbled down, and a butcher’s cart had gone over his poor lame foot. So they took the hurt foot to the hospital, and of course he had to go with it, and the hospital was much more like the heaven he read of in his books than anything he had ever come across before.

===Chinese===
我希望你们没让这些絮叨讲烦了。

要知道，我必须先讲一讲迪基是个什么样的孩子、过的是什么样的日子，不然你们就不明白他后来那些奇遇。而奇遇他是有的——数也数不清的奇遇——你们马上就会看到。

迪基醒来，快活得像春天的太阳——那太阳正努力从他的脏窗户里朝里张望呢。

「说不定他今天还要弄园子呢！」他说着，一骨碌起了床。

他是在那间又脏又不舒服的屋子里起床、自己穿的衣服。可是到晚上，替他脱衣服的是一双和和气气、干干净净的手，把他放进半桶热腾腾、亮银银的水里洗澡，用的肥皂有街尾木材场的那种气味。因为那天早晨上学去，他那颗傻乎乎的小脑袋里装满了艺术鸟食和虹彩的花朵，拐杖在一块香蕉皮上一滑，摔了个跟头，一辆肉铺的马车，正好从他那只可怜的瘸脚上碾了过去。于是人们把那只伤脚送进医院——他自然得跟着去——而那家医院，比他从前见过的任何东西，都更像他书里读到的那种天堂。

===Original===
He noticed that the nurses and the doctors spoke in the kind of words that he had found in his books, and in a voice that he had not found anywhere; so when on the second day a round-faced, smiling lady in a white cap said, “Well, Tommy, and how are we today?” he replied﻿—

“My name is far from being Tommy, and I am in Lux Ury and Af Fluence, I thank you, gracious lady.”

At which the lady laughed and pinched his cheek.

When she grew to know him better, and found out where he had learned to talk like that, she produced more books. And from them he learned more new words. They were very nice to him at the hospital, but when they sent him home they put his lame foot into a thick boot with a horrid, clumpy sole and iron things that went up his leg.

===Chinese===
他留意到，护士们和医生们说话用的字眼儿，正是他在书里找到的那一种，而且用的是一种他从来没在别处听过的嗓音。所以到第二天，一位戴白帽子的圆脸太太笑眯眯地问：「喂，汤米，今天咱们怎么样啊？」他回答说——

「我的名字远非汤米。我此刻正身在『奢·华』与『富·足』之中，谢谢您，尊贵的夫人。」

太太听了哈哈大笑，拧了拧他的脸蛋。

后来她跟他熟了，探明了他是打哪儿学来这样说话的，就又拿出更多的书来。他从这些书里又学到许多新字眼。在医院里，人人待他都极好；可是出院的时候，他们给他那只瘸脚套上了一只厚靴子，底子又笨又沉，还带着铁条，一直箍到腿上。

===Original===
His aunt and her friends said, “How kind!” but Dickie hated it. The boys at school made game of it﻿—they had got used to the crutch﻿—and that was worse than being called “Old Dot-and-go-one,” which was what Dickie had got used to﻿—so used that it seemed almost like a pet name.

And on that first night of his return he found that he had been robbed. They had taken his Tinkler from the safe corner in his bed where the ticking was broken, and there was a soft flock nest for a boy’s best friend.

He knew better than to ask what had become of it. Instead he searched and searched the house in all its five rooms. But he never found Tinkler.

===Chinese===
婶婶和她的朋友们都说：「多好的东西！」可迪基恨透了它。学校的男孩子们拿它取笑——拐杖他们原是习惯了——这比叫他「一颠一颠老头儿」还要难受；那绰号迪基本已听惯，惯得几乎像个爱称。

而回家后的头一夜，他就发现自己遭了贼。他们从床铺里那个安全的角落——褥子破了个口子、絮窝软乎乎的地方，一个男孩子搁他顶好的朋友的地方——把他的叮铃偷走了。

他懂得不该去问它落到哪儿了。他把全屋五间房搜了一遍又一遍。可是叮铃再没找回来。

===Original===
Instead he found next day, when his aunt had gone out shopping, a little square of cardboard at the back of the dresser drawer, among the dirty dusters and clothes pegs and string and corks and novelettes.

It was a pawn-ticket﻿—“Rattle. One shilling.”

Dickie knew all about pawn-tickets. You, of course, don’t. Well, ask some grown-up person to explain; I haven’t time. I want to get on with the story.

Until he had found that ticket he had not been able to think of anything else. He had not even cared to think about his garden and wonder whether the Artistic Bird Seeds had come up parrot-colored. He had been a very long time in the hospital, and it was August now. And the nurses had assured him that the seeds must be up long ago﻿—he would find everything flowering, you see if he didn’t.

===Chinese===
倒是第二天，趁婶婶出门买东西，他在碗柜抽屉后头，脏掸子、衣夹、绳头、软木塞和小言情小说中间，找到一小方纸板。

那是一张当票——「摇铃。一先令。」

当票是怎么一回事，迪基全懂。你呢，当然不懂。那么，请哪位大人解释给你听吧；我没工夫，故事还得往下讲呢。

在找到那张票以前，他什么心思都没有，别的什么都想不进去。他连自己的园子都顾不上想，顾不上琢磨艺术鸟食开没开出鹦鹉颜色的花来。他在医院里住了很久很久，如今已是八月。护士们早向他担保过：种子老早该出土了——回去一看，保管满园都在开花，不信你就瞧着。

===Original===
And now he went out to look. There was a tangle of green growth at the end of the garden, and the next garden was full of weeds. For the Man Next Door had gone off to look for work down Ashford way, where the hop-gardens are, and the house was to let.

A few poor little pink and yellow flowers showed stunted among the green where he had sowed the Artistic Bird Seed. And, towering high above everything else-oh, three times as high as Dickie himself﻿—there was a flower﻿—a great flower like a sunflower, only white.

“Why,” said Dickie, “it’s as big as a dinner-plate.”

It was.

===Chinese===
如今他出去看了。园子头上乱蓬蓬长了一片绿；隔壁的园子已长满野草。因为隔壁的男人上阿什福德（Ashford）那边找活儿去了——那地方长啤酒花——房子也招了租。

在他下种的地方，绿丛中间，可怜巴巴地探出几株又矮又蔫的粉红黄花。而在所有东西上头高高地耸着——哦，足有迪基本人三个高——有一朵花——一朵大花，像向日葵，只是白的。

「咦，」迪基说，「跟一只菜盘子一样大。」

的确是。

===Original===
It stood up, beautiful and stately, and turned its cream-white face towards the sun.

“The stalk’s like a little tree,” said Dickie; and so it was.

It had great drooping leaves, and a dozen smaller white flowers stood out below it on long stalks, thinner than that needed to support the moonflower itself.

“It *is* a moonflower, of course,” he said, “if the other kind’s sunflowers. I love it! I love it! I love it!”

===Chinese===
它亭亭地立着，又美又庄严，把乳白的脸盘转向太阳。

「这梗子活像一棵小树，」迪基说；果然像。

它垂着大大的叶子，底下沿着长梗又挺出十几朵小白花，那梗比擎着月光花（moonflower）本朵的那根还要细。

「它*就是*一朵月光花，准没错，」他说，「既然向日葵那一类叫葵。我爱它！我爱它！我爱它！」

===Original===
He did not allow himself much time for loving it, however; for he had business in hand. He had, somehow or other, to get a shilling. Because without a shilling he could not exchange that square of cardboard with “Rattle” on it for his one friend, Tinkler. And with the shilling he could. (This is part of the dismal magic of pawn-tickets which some grownup will kindly explain to you.)

“I can’t get money by the sweat of my brow,” said Dickie to himself; “nobody would let me run their errands when they could get a boy with both legs to do them. Not likely. I wish I’d got something I could sell.”

He looked round the yard﻿—dirtier and nastier than ever now in the parts that the Man Next Door had not had time to dig. There was certainly nothing there that anyone would want to buy, especially now the rabbit-hutch was gone. Except﻿… why, of course﻿—the moonflowers!

He got the old worn-down knife out of the bowl on the back kitchen sink, where it nestled among potato peelings like a flower among foliage, and carefully cut half a dozen of the smaller flowers. Then he limped up to New Cross Station, and stood outside, leaning on his crutch, and holding out the flowers to the people who came crowding out of the station after the arrival of each train-thick, black crowds of tired people, in too great a hurry to get home to their teas to care much about him or his flowers. Everybody glanced at them, for they were wonderful flowers, as white as water-lilies, only flat﻿—the real sunflower shape﻿—and their centres were of the purest yellow gold color.

===Chinese===
可是他没舍得在「爱它」上头多花工夫；因为他手头有正事。他总得想办法弄到一先令。因为不有一先令，他就不能拿那方写着「摇铃」二字的纸板，换回他唯一的朋友叮铃；有了先令，就能换回来。（这便是当票那套愁人的魔法的一部分，回头哪位大人自会好心讲给你听。）

「靠汗珠子挣饭吃，我是挣不来的，」迪基自言自语；「人家雇得着两条腿囫囵的男孩跑腿，就决不肯叫我。没门儿。我巴不得有样什么能卖的东西。」

他环顾院子——隔壁的男人来不及挖的那些地方，如今比先前更脏更糟。那儿确实没有一样人肯买的东西，尤其如今兔笼也没了。除非——咦，对呀——月光花！

他从后厨房水槽的盆里——那把刀窝在土豆皮中间，像一朵花窝在叶子里——摸出那把磨秃了的旧刀，小心地剪下五六朵小的。然后他一瘸一拐走到纽克罗斯车站，拄着拐杖站在外头，把花举给每趟车到站后从站里涌出来的人看——一群群又稠又黑的疲惫的人，急着赶回家吃茶点，顾不上多看他，也顾不上多看他的花。人人都要瞟一眼，因为那实在是奇花，白得像睡莲，只是平的——正经的向日葵形状——花心是最纯的金黄色。

===Original===
“Pretty, ain’t they?” one black-coated person would say to another. And the other would reply﻿—

“No. Yes. I dunno! Hurry up, can’t you?”

It was no good. Dickie was tired, and the flowers were beginning to droop. He turned to go home, when a sudden thought brought the blood to his face. He turned again quickly and went straight to the pawnbroker’s. You may be quite sure he had learned the address on the card by heart.

He went boldly into the shop, which had three handsome gold balls hanging out above its door, and in its window all sorts of pretty things-rings, and chains, and brooches, and watches, and china, and silk handkerchiefs, and concertinas.

===Chinese===
「真俊，是不是？」一个黑衣人常对另一个这样说。另一个就回答——

「不。是。我说不上！你就不能快着点儿？」

没有用。迪基累了，花也开始蔫了。他转身要回家，忽然一个念头，叫他血一下子涌上脸。他霍地又转回身，直奔当铺而去。你可以十拿九稳，那张卡片上的地址，他早背得滚瓜烂熟。

他大着胆子走进铺子。铺子门楣上头挑着三颗体面的金球，橱窗里摆着各式各样的漂亮东西——戒指、链子、胸针、表、瓷器、绸手帕，还有六角手风琴。

===Original===
“Well, young man,” said the stout gentleman behind the counter, “what can we do for you?”

“I want to pawn my moonflowers,” said Dickie.

The stout gentleman roared with laughter, and slapped a stout leg with a stout hand.

“Well, that’s a good ’un!” he said, “as good a one as ever I heard. Why, you little duffer, they’d be dead long before you came back to redeem them, that’s certain.”

===Chinese===
「喂，小伙子，」柜台后头一位胖先生说，「我们能为你效点什么劳？」

「我要当了这些月光花，」迪基说。

胖先生放声大笑，一只有力的手直拍一条粗腿。

「哈哈，这才叫妙事！」他说，「我活这么大听过的头一份儿妙事。嘿，你这个小傻瓜，等你回来赎它们，它们早死透啦，这是准的。」

===Original===
“You’d have them while they were alive, you know,” said Dickie gently.

“What are they? Don’t seem up to much. Though I don’t know that I ever saw a flower just like them, come to think of it,” said the pawnbroker, who lived in a neat villa at Brockley and went in for gardening in a gentlemanly, you-needn’t-suppose-I-can’t-afford-a-real-gardener-if-I-like sort of way.

“They’re moonflowers,” said Dickie, “and I want to pawn them and then get something else out with the money.”

“Got the ticket?” said the gentleman, cleverly seeing that he meant “get out of pawn.”

===Chinese===
「可它们活着的时候，是归您的呀，您知道，」迪基轻轻地说。

「这是什么花？瞧着不怎么样。不过说真的，细想起来，这样的花我还真没见过，」当铺老板说。他住在布罗克利（Brockley）一幢齐整的小洋房里，弄弄园艺，是那种绅士派头的弄法——你可别以为他要有兴致，还请不起一个正经园丁。

「这是月光花，」迪基说，「我要把它们当了，再拿钱赎点别的出来。」

「带着票呢？」先生说，他机灵，听懂了「赎出来」的意思。

===Original===
“Yes,” said Dickie; “and it’s my own Tinkler that my daddy gave me before he died, and my aunt Missa propagated it when I was in hospital.”

The man looked carefully at the card.

“All right,” he said at last; “hand over the flowers. They are not so bad,” he added, more willing to prize them now that they were his (things do look different when they are your own, don’t they?). “Here, Humphreys, put these in a jug of water till I go home. And get this out.”

A pale young man in spectacles appeared from a sort of dark cave at the back of the shop, took flowers and ticket, and was swallowed up again in the darkness of the cave.

===Chinese===
「带着呢，」迪基说；「是我自个儿的叮铃，我爸爸临死给我的。我进医院的时候，被我婶婶米莎给『侵吞』了。」

那人把卡片仔细看了看。

「行，」他终于说；「把花给我吧。还不赖，」他添了一句——如今花归了他，他倒乐意抬举它们了（东西一成了自己的，看着就两样，是不是？）。「喂，汉弗莱斯（Humphreys），把这些花插在一罐水里，搁到我回家。把这个赎出来。」

一个戴眼镜的苍白年轻伙计，从铺子后头一个黑洞洞的窖窟里应声出来，接过花和当票，又一头没进那窖窟的黑暗里。

===Original===
“Oh, thank you!” said Dickie fervently. “I shall live but to repay your bounteous gen’rosity.”

“None of your cheek,” said the pawnbroker, reddening, and there was an awkward pause.

“It’s not cheek; I meant it,” said Dickie at last, speaking very earnestly. “You’ll see, some of these days. I read an interesting Nar Rataive about a Lion the King of Beasts and a Mouse, that small and Ty Morous animal, which if you have not heard it I will now Pur seed to relite.”

“You’re a rum little kid, I don’t think,” said the man. “Where do you learn such talk?”

===Chinese===
「哦，谢谢您！」迪基满心热诚地说。「我这条命，往后就用来报答您的慷慨大恩。」

「少跟我耍贫嘴，」当铺老板说，脸红了；跟着是一阵尴尬的冷场。

「这不是耍贫嘴；我是真心话，」迪基终于说，说得极其恳切。「往后您就瞧着吧。我读过一篇有趣的『叙·述』，讲一头狮子——百兽之王——和一只耗子，那只小小的、『怯·懦』的动物；您要是还没听过，我这就『进·行』把它讲述一番。」

「你这个小家伙可真够怪的，我看是不假。」那人说。「这种话你打哪儿学来的？」

===Original===
“It’s the wye they talk in books,” said Dickie, suddenly returning to the language of his aunt. “You bein’ a toff I thought you’d unnerstand. My mistike. No ’fense.”

“Mean to say you can talk like a book when you like, and cut it off short like that?”

“I can Con-vers like Lords and Lydies,” said Dickie, in the accents of the gutter, “and your noble benefacteriness made me seek to express my feelinks with the best words at me Command.”

“Fond of books?”

“I believe you,” said Dickie, and there were no more awkward pauses.

===Chinese===
「书里的人就这么说话呗，」迪基说，忽然又换回了跟他婶婶说话的那种腔调。「您是位上等人，我当您听得懂。我认错人了。没恶意。」

「照你喜欢就能说书腔，说变又能一下掐断，是这意思吧？」

「我能跟老爷太太们一个模样地『会·谈』，」迪基拖着贫民窟的腔调说，「您高贵的恩情，叫我恨不得拿我手头顶好的字眼儿，来表一表我的心意。」

「爱读书？」

「那还用说，」迪基说。从此再没有尴尬的冷场了。

===Original===
When the pale young man came back with something wrapped in a bit of clean rag, he said a whispered word or two to the pawnbroker, who unrolled the rag and looked closely at the rattle.

“So it is,” he said, “and it’s a beauty too, let alone anything else.”

“Isn’t he?” said Dickie, touched by this praise of his treasured Tinkler.

“I’ve got something else here that’s got the same crest as your rattle.”

===Chinese===
苍白年轻伙计捧着一件用干净布片包着的东西回来了，凑着当铺老板的耳朵低低说了句什么，老板便把布解开，凑近细看那只摇铃。

「没错，」他说，「还是件好东西，别的且不说。」

「可不是嘛！」迪基说，他那宝贝叮铃得到这样一句夸奖，心里热乎乎的。

「我这几儿还有一样别的东西，上头的纹章跟你这摇铃上的是一个样。」

===Original===
“Crest?” said Dickie; “isn’t that what you wear on your helmet in the heat and press of the Tower Nament?”

The pawnbroker explained that crests no longer live exclusively on helmets, but on all sorts of odd things. And the queer little animal, drawn in fine scratches on the side of the rattle, was, it seemed, a crest.

“Here, Humphreys,” he added, “give it a rub up and bring that seal here.”

The pale young man did something to Tinkler with some pinky powder and a brush and a wash-leather, while his master fitted together the two halves of a broken white cornelian.

===Chinese===
「纹章？」迪基说；「那不就是您在『图尔纳门特』——马上比武大会——的酷热酣战之中，戴在头盔顶上的那种东西吗？」

当铺老板解释说，纹章如今早已不单住在头盔上，各样稀奇古怪的东西上头都住。摇铃侧面上用细细的刻痕画着的那只奇怪小兽，看来，就是个纹章。

「喂，汉弗莱斯，」他又说，「给它擦一擦，把那枚印章拿来。」

苍白年轻伙计拿一种粉红的粉子、一把刷子和一块麂皮，替叮铃做了些什么；他主人则把半枚碎成两半的白光玉髓拼到了一起。

===Original===
“It came out of a seal,” he said, “and I don’t mind making you a present of it.”

“Oh!” said Dickie, “you are a real rightern.” And he rested his crutch against the counter expressly to clasp his hands in ecstasy as boys in books did.

“My young man shall stick it together with cement,” the pawnbroker went on, “and put it in a little box. Don’t you take it out till tomorrow and it’ll be stuck fast. Only don’t go trying to seal with it, or the sealing-wax will melt the cement. It’ll bring you luck, I shouldn’t wonder.”

(It did; and such luck as the kind pawnbroker never dreamed of. But that comes further on in the story.)

===Chinese===
「这是从一枚印章上取下来的，」他说，「白送给你，我不心疼。」

「哦！」迪基说，「您真是个地地道道的大好人！」他把拐杖往柜台上一靠，像书里的男孩那样，特意双手一握，喜得出了神。

「叫我的伙计用胶把它粘好，」当铺老板接着说，「搁进一个小匣子。明天以前别去动它，一宿就粘得牢牢的。只是千万别拿它去封火漆，火漆一热，胶就化了。我说不定，它还能给你带来好运呢。」

（它果然带来了；而且是那位好心的当铺老板做梦也想不到的好运。可那是故事后头的事了。）

===Original===
Dickie left the shop without his moonflowers, indeed, but with his Tinkler now whitely shining, and declared to be “real silver, and mind you take care of it, my lad,” his white cornelian seal carefully packed in a strong little cardboard box with metal corners. Also a broken-backed copy of *Ingoldsby Legends* and one of “Mrs. Markham’s *English History*,” which had no back at all. “You must go on trying to improve your mind,” said the pawnbroker fussily. He was very pleased with himself for having been so kind. “And come back and see me﻿—say next month.”

“I will,” said Dickie. “A thousand blessings from a grateful heart. I will come back. I say, you are good! Thank you, thank you﻿—I will come back next month, and tell you everything I have learned from the Perru Sal of your books.”

“Perusal,” said the pawnbroker﻿—“that’s the way to pernounce it. Goodbye, my man, and next month.”

But next month found Dickie in a very different place from the pawnbroker’s shop, and with a very different person from the pawnbroker who in his rural retirement at Brockley gardened in such a gentlemanly way.

===Chinese===
迪基离开铺子的时候，月光花固然没带上，叮铃却回来了，白亮白亮地闪着光，老板还郑重声明过：「是正经白银，你给我好好爱惜，我的孩子」；那枚白光玉髓印章（white seal），也仔细装进一个包着金属包角的小硬纸盒。此外还有一本散了脊的*《英戈兹比传说》*，和一本干脆没有脊的「马卡姆夫人的*《英国史》*」。「你可得接着修身进学呀，」当铺老板忙忙叨叨地说。他为自己这样仁义，心里十分受用。「过些日子再来看我——就说下个月吧。」

「我一定来，」迪基说。「一颗感恩的心，献上千千万万的祝福。我一定来。我说，您太好啦！谢谢您，谢谢您——下个月我一定来，把我从『细·读』您这些书里学来的一切，全都讲给您听。」

「是『细读』，」当铺老板说——「得这么念。再会吧，我的孩子，下个月见。」

可是到了下个月，迪基已在一个同当铺老板的铺子大不相同的地方，同一个也大不相同的人在一起了——那位老板这时正在布罗克利的乡居里，那样绅士派头地弄着他的园艺呢。

===Original===
Dickie went home-his aunt was still out. His books told him that treasure is best hidden under loose boards, unless of course your house has a secret panel, which his had not. There was a loose board in his room, where the man “saw to” the gas. He got it up, and pushed his treasures as far in as he could-along the rough, crumbly surface of the lath and plaster.

Not a moment too soon. For before the board was coaxed quite back into its place the voice of the aunt screamed up.

“Come along down, can’t you? I can hear you pounding about up there. Come along down and fetch me a ha’porth o’ wood﻿—I can’t get the kettle to boil without a fire, can I?”

When Dickie came down his aunt slightly slapped him, and he took the halfpenny and limped off obediently.

===Chinese===
迪基回了家——婶婶还没回来。他的书告诉过他，财宝顶好藏在活动地板底下，当然，除非你家有一块暗板，他家没有。他屋里就有一块活动的地板，在管煤气的人「照料」煤气表的地方。他把地板撬起来，把宝贝尽可能往里推——推过板条和灰泥那粗糙酥松的表面。

一刻也不算早。因为那块地板还没哄回原处，婶婶的嗓门就在楼下尖叫上来了。

「还不快下来？我听见你在楼上咚咚咚地砸呢。下来，给我捎半便士的柴火来——不生火，壶水能开吗？」

迪基下来，婶婶轻轻给了他一个耳光，他接过那半便士，乖乖地一瘸一拐去了。

===Original===
It was a very long time indeed before he came back. Because before he got to the shop with no window to it, but only shutters that were put up at night, where the wood and coal were sold, he saw a Punch and Judy show. He had never seen one before, and it interested him extremely. He longed to see it unpack itself and display its wonders, and he followed it through more streets than he knew; and when he found that it was not going to unpack at all, but was just going home to its bed in an old coach-house, he remembered the firewood; and the halfpenny clutched tight and close in his hand seemed to reproach him warmly.

He looked about him, and knew that he did not at all know where he was. There was a tall, thin, ragged man lounging against a stable door in the yard where the Punch and Judy show lived. He took his clay pipe out of his mouth to say﻿—

“What’s up, matey? Lost your way?”

Dickie explained.

===Chinese===
他实在去了好半天好半天。因为还没走到那家没有橱窗、只在夜里上窗板的柴炭铺，他先看见一场潘趣和朱迪木偶戏（Punch and Judy show）。他从没见过木偶戏，一下子给迷住了。他盼着看它拆开包袱、变出万般奇景，就跟着它走，走过了多少条他自己也数不清的街；后来他看出，它压根儿不打算拆包，只是要回老马车房里它的铺上去睡觉，他才想起柴火来；那半便士在他手心里攥得紧紧的，倒好像热辣辣地埋怨起他来。

他四下张望，明白自己全然不知到了什么地方。在木偶戏安身的那座院子里，一个又高又瘦、衣衫褴褛的汉子，正懒洋洋地靠在一扇马厩门上。他从嘴里拿下陶土烟斗，说——

「怎么啦，伙计？迷路啦？」

迪基原原本本说了。

===Original===
“It’s Lavender Terrace where I live,” he ended﻿—“Lavender Terrace, Rosemary Street, Deptford.”

“I’m going that way myself,” said the man, getting away from the wall. “We’ll go back by the boat if you like. Ever been on the boat?”

“No,” said Dickie.

“Like to?”

“Don’t mind if I do,” said Dickie.

===Chinese===
「我家住在薰衣草台街，」他末了说——「德普特福德，薰衣草台街（Lavender Terrace），迷迭香街。」

「我也正往那边去，」那人说，从墙边直起身来。「你要乐意，咱们坐船回去。坐过船没有？」

「没有，」迪基说。

「想坐不？」

「坐坐也不碍事，」迪基说。

===Original===
It was very pleasant with the steamboat going along in such a hurry, pushing the water out of the way, and puffing and blowing, and something beating inside it like a giant’s heart. The wind blew freshly, and the ragged man found a sheltered corner behind the funnel. It was so sheltered, and the wind had been so strong that Dickie felt sleepy. When he said, “ ’Ave I bin asleep?” the steamer was stopping at a pier at a strange place with trees.

“Here we are!” said the man. “ ’Ave you been asleep? Not ’alf! Stir yourself, my man; we get off here.”

“Is this Deptford?” Dickie asked. And the people shoving and crushing to get off the steamer laughed when he said it.

“Not exackly,” said the man, “but it’s all right. This ’ere’s where we get off. You ain’t had yer tea yet, my boy.”

===Chinese===
那真是舒服极了：汽船急急忙忙往前赶，把水推开，呼哧呼哧地喘，船身里头有什么东西咚咚地擂着，像一颗巨人的心。风迎面吹得爽利，褴褛汉子在烟囱后头寻了个避风的角落。那儿遮得严实，风又刮得猛，迪基竟瞌睡起来。等他说出「我睡着了吗？」，汽船已经靠上一处陌生地方的码头，四下里长着树。

「到啦！」那人说。「你睡着啦？睡得死死的！打起精神来，我的好人；咱们在这儿下船。」

「这儿是德普特福德吗？」迪基问。挤着搡着下船的人听了都笑了。

「不全是，」那人说，「不过不要紧。咱们就在这儿下。你的茶点还没吃呢，我的孩子。」

===Original===
It was the most glorious tea Dickie had ever imagined. Fried eggs and bacon﻿—he had one egg and the man had three-bread and butter﻿—and if the bread was thick, so was the butter﻿—and as many cups of tea as you liked to say thank you for. When it was over the man asked Dickie if he could walk a little way, and when Dickie said he could they set out in the most friendly way side by side.

“I like it very much, and thank you kindly,” said Dickie presently. “And the tea and all. An’ the egg. And this is the prettiest place ever I see. But I ought to be getting ’ome. I shall catch it a fair treat as it is. She was waitin’ for the wood to boil the kettle when I come out.”

“Mother?”

“Aunt. Not me real aunt. Only I calls her that.”

===Chinese===
那是迪基想都想不出的最阔气的一顿茶点。煎蛋和咸肉——他一个蛋，那人三个——面包抹黄油——面包厚，黄油也不含糊——还有茶，只要你肯道一声谢，就随你添。吃完了，那人问迪基还能不能走一小段路；迪基说能，两人便亲亲热热、肩并肩地上了路。

「我打心眼儿里喜欢，多谢您的好意，」迪基走了不大一会儿说。「茶点也好，什么都好。还有那个蛋。这地方，是我见过的顶好看的地方。可我该回家了。如今这样回去，也少不了一顿好受的。我出来的时候，她正等柴火烧水呢。」

「妈妈？」

「婶婶。不是亲婶婶。我就是这么叫她。」

===Original===
“She any good?”

“Ain’t bad when she’s in a good temper.”

“That ain’t what she’ll be in when you gets back. Seems to me you’ve gone and done it, mate. Why, it’s hours and hours since you and me got acquainted. Look! the sun’s just going.”

It was, over trees more beautiful than anything Dickie had ever seen, for they were now in a country road, with green hedges and green grass growing beside it, in which little round-faced flowers grew﻿—daisies they were﻿—even Dickie knew that.

===Chinese===
「她待人好吗？」

「脾气好的时候不坏。」

「等你回去，她那个脾气可好不了。依我看，伙计，你这一下可是把自己撂进去了。嗐，咱们认识，都有了好几个钟头啦。瞧！太阳正落呢。」

正是落着呢，落在一片树梢后头——那树，是迪基生平见过的最美的东西，因为他们这会儿已走在一条乡间路上，路旁长着青青的树篱和青青的草地，草里开着些小小的圆脸蛋儿的花——是雏菊——连迪基都认得。

===Original===
“I got to stick it,” said Dickie sadly. “I’d best be getting home.”

“I wouldn’t go ’ome, not if I was you,” said the man. “I’d go out and see the world a bit, I would.”

“What﻿—me?” said Dickie.

“Why not? Come, I’ll make you a fair offer. Ye come alonger me an’ see life! I’m a-goin’ to tramp as far as Brighton and back, all alongside the sea. Ever seed the sea?”

“No,” said Dickie. “Oh, no﻿—no, I never.”

===Chinese===
「这一顿我挨定了，」迪基愁苦地说。「我顶好还是回家去。」

「我要是你，就不回那个家，」那人说。「我就出去见见世面，我就会这么干。」

「什么——叫我？」迪基说。

「怎么不？来，我跟你公平交易。你跟我走，见见世面去！我打算一路徒步，走到布赖顿（Brighton）再走回来，全程贴着海边。见过海没有？」

「没有，」迪基说。「哦，没有——没有，从来没有。」

===Original===
“Well, you come alonger me. I ain’t ’it yer, have I, like what yer aunt do? I give yer a ride in a pleasure boat, only you went to sleep, and I give you a tea fit for a hemperor. Ain’t I?”

“You ’ave that,” said Dickie.

“Well, that’ll show you the sort of man I am. So now I make you a fair offer. You come longer me, and be my little ’un, and I’ll be your daddy, and a better dad, I lay, nor if I’d been born so. What do you say, matey?”

The man’s manner was so kind and hearty, the whole adventure was so wonderful and new﻿…

===Chinese===
「那好，你跟我走。我打过你没有？你婶婶可打过。我请你坐了游船，虽然你睡着了；我还请你吃了一顿皇帝吃了也够格的茶点。是不是？」

「是这样，」迪基说。

「这就叫你看看我是个什么人。所以现在我跟你公平交易。你跟我走，做我的小家伙，我给你当爸爸，我保准比亲生的还当得好。你说怎么样，伙计？」

那人的态度那样和气、那样热心，这整个儿冒险又是那样奇妙、那样新鲜……

===Original===
“Is it country where you going?” said Dickie, looking at the green hedge.

“All the way, pretty near,” said the man. “We’ll tramp it, taking it easy, all round the coast, where gents go for their outings. They’ve always got a bit to spare then. I lay you’ll get some color in them cheeks o’ yours. They’re like putty now. Come, now. What you say? Is it a bargain?”

“It’s very kind of you,” said Dickie, “but what call you got to do it? It’ll cost a lot﻿—my victuals, I mean. What call you got to do it?”

The man scratched his head and hesitated. Then he looked up at the sky and then down at the road﻿—they were resting on a heap of stones.

===Chinese===
「你要去的是乡下吗？」迪基望着青青的树篱说。

「差不多一路都是，」那人说。「咱们不慌不忙地走，沿海绕一大圈，正是老爷们出门游玩的那条道。那时候他们手头总有富余。我保准叫你那张小脸吃上点血色。如今白得跟腻子似的。来吧。你说怎么样？就这么讲定了？」

「您心眼儿真好，」迪基说，「可您图什么呀？得花好些钱呢——我是说我的饭食。您图什么呀？」

那人挠了挠头皮，迟疑了。他抬头望了望天，又低头望了望路——他们正在一堆石头上歇脚。

===Original===
At last he said, “You’re a sharp lad, you are﻿—bloomin’ sharp. Well, I won’t deceive you, matey. I want company. Tramping alone ain’t no beano to me. An’ as I gets my living by the sweat of charitable ladies an’ gents it don’t do no harm to ’ave a little nipper alongside. They comes down ’andsomer if there’s a nipper. An’ I like nippers. Some blokes don’t, but I do.”

Dickie felt that this was true. But﻿—“We’ll be beggars, you mean?” he said doubtfully.
